# Agnostic Active Learning Is Always Better Than Passive Learning

Steve Hanneke Department of Computer Science Purdue University steve.hanneke@gmail.com

# Abstract

This work resolves a long-standing open question of central importance to the theory of active learning, closing a qualitative and quantitative gap in our understanding of active learning in the non-realizable case. We provide the first sharp characterization of the optimal first-order query complexity of agnostic active learning, and propose a new general active learning algorithm which achieves it. Remarkably, the optimal query complexity admits a leading term which is *always* strictly smaller than the sample complexity of passive supervised learning (by a factor proportional to the best-in-class error rate). This was not previously known to be possible. For comparison, in all previous general analyses, the leading term exhibits an additional factor, such as the disagreement coefficient or related complexity measures, and therefore only provides improvements over passive learning in restricted cases. The present work completely removes such factors from the leading term, implying that *every concept class benefits from active learning in the non-realizable case*. Whether such benefits are possible has been the driving question underlying the past two decades of research on the theory of agnostic active learning. This work finally settles this fundamental question.

# 1 Introduction

Active learning is a well-known powerful variant of supervised learning, in which the learning algorithm interactively participates in the process of labeling the training examples. In this setting, there is a pool (or stream) of unlabeled examples, and the learning algorithm selects individual examples and queries an oracle (typically a human labeler) to observe their labels. This happens sequentially, so that the learner has observed previously-queried labels before deciding which example to query next. The intended purpose of active learning is to reduce the overall number of labels necessary for learning to a given accuracy, called the *query complexity*. We are therefore particularly interested in using active learning in scenarios where its query complexity is significantly smaller than the number of randomly-sampled training examples which would be needed to achieve the same accuracy, called the *sample complexity* of *passive* supervised learning.

Active learning has not only been incredibly useful for many practical machine learning problems (e.g., [Cohn et al.,](#page-11-0) [1996;](#page-11-0) [Tong and Koller,](#page-15-0) [2001;](#page-15-0) [Zhu et al.,](#page-16-0) [2003;](#page-16-0) [Olsson,](#page-15-1) [2009;](#page-15-1) [Settles,](#page-15-2) [2012;](#page-15-2) [Ren et al.,](#page-15-3) [2021;](#page-15-3) [Mosqueira-Rey et al.,](#page-14-0) [2023\)](#page-14-0) but has also given rise to a rich and nuanced theoretical literature (e.g., [Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005,](#page-12-0) [2011;](#page-12-1) [Balcan et al.,](#page-10-0) [2009;](#page-10-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b,](#page-13-0) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014;](#page-16-1) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015;](#page-13-2) see Appendix [A](#page-17-0) for a detailed survey). Moreover, the insights and techniques discovered in this literature have had tremendous influence on other branches of the learning theory literature (e.g., [Awasthi et al.,](#page-10-1) [2014;](#page-10-1) [Foster et al.,](#page-12-2) [2021;](#page-12-2) [Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2016a,](#page-13-4)[b,](#page-13-5) [2024;](#page-13-6) [Zhivotovskiy and Hanneke,](#page-16-2) [2018;](#page-16-2) [Simon,](#page-15-4) [2015;](#page-15-4) [Balcan and Long,](#page-10-2) [2013;](#page-10-2) [El-Yaniv and Wiener,](#page-12-3) [2010;](#page-12-3) [Balcan et al.,](#page-10-3) [2022\)](#page-10-3).

Within the literature on the theory of active learning, a central topic which has garnered by-far the most interest is that of *agnostic* active learning: that is, the study of active learning algorithms capable of providing performance guarantees even in noisy or otherwise non-realizable learning problems, without assumptions on the form of the noise. This line of work was initiated by the groundbreaking A<sup>2</sup> algorithm (Agnostic Active) of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford](#page-10-4) [\(2005,](#page-10-4) [2006,](#page-10-5) [2009\)](#page-10-0) (with its general analysis later given by [Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b\)](#page-13-0) and concurrently a lower bound analysis of [Kääriäinen](#page-14-1) [\(2005,](#page-14-1) [2006\)](#page-14-2) (later strengthened by [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009\)](#page-11-1). These results were later refined and extended in numerous ways. However, throughout this two-decades long history, there has persisted a significant gap between the sharpest known upper and lower bounds on the optimal query complexity. Moreover, this gap represents an important *qualitative* distinction: while the lower bound is always smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning, the existing upper bounds only reflect such improvements under further restrictive conditions (e.g., bounded disagreement coefficient). Thus, the issue of resolving this gap is of central importance to this subject, since it has implications for answering the question:

*Does every concept class admit benefits from using active learning instead of passive learning?*

The main contribution of the present work is to establish that this is indeed true, and in fact the known lower bound is always attainable. To achieve this, we introduce new algorithmic principles for active learning (the AVID principle), improving concentration of error estimates via adaptively isolating regions where the error estimates have high variance and allocating more queries to such regions.

# 2 Background and Summary of the Main Result

Let <sup>C</sup> be any concept class[<sup>1</sup>](#page-1-0) (a set of functions X → {0, 1} on a set X called the *instance space*) and denote by d = VC(C) the VC dimension of C [\(Vapnik and Chervonenkis,](#page-16-3) [1971;](#page-16-3) see Definition [4\)](#page-32-0). Let P be an (unknown) joint distribution on X × {0, 1}, and define the *error rate* of any *classifier* h : X → {0, 1} as er<sup>P</sup> (h) := P((x, y) : h(x) ̸= y). In the *active learning* problem, there is a sequence (X1, Y1), . . . ,(Xm, Ym) of i.i.d. samples from P, but the learner initially only observes the X<sup>i</sup> values (the *unlabeled* examples). It then has the capability to *query* any example X<sup>i</sup> , which reveals the corresponding true label Y<sup>i</sup> , in a *sequential* manner (i.e., it chooses its next query X<sup>i</sup> ′ after observing the label Y<sup>i</sup> of its previous query point Xi). After a number of such queries, the learner returns a classifier hˆ. The goal is to achieve a small *excess* error rate er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε while making as few queries as possible. We are particularly interested in quantifying the number of queries sufficient to achieve this, as a function of ε and the value of the *best-in-class* error rate infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h), known as a *first-order* query complexity bound.

Specifically, for any ε, δ, β ∈ (0, 1), the *optimal query complexity*, QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C), is defined as the minimal Q ∈ <sup>N</sup> for which there exists an active learner <sup>A</sup><sup>a</sup> such that (for a sufficiently large number m of unlabeled examples), for every P with infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β, with probability at least 1 − δ, <sup>A</sup><sup>a</sup> makes at most Q queries and returns hˆ satisfying er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε. The main quantity for comparison is the *sample complexity* of supervised *passive learning*. A passive learner A<sup>p</sup> simply trains on n *labeled* training examples (X1, Y1), . . . ,(Xn, Yn) sampled i.i.d. from P to produce a classifier hˆ. For ε, δ, β ∈ (0, 1), the *optimal sample complexity* of passive learning, Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>), is defined as the minimal size n ∈ N of such a training sample for which there exists a passive learner <sup>A</sup><sup>p</sup> such that, for every P with infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β, with probability at least 1 − δ, <sup>A</sup><sup>p</sup> returns hˆ satisfying er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε. We remark that, in both the active and passive cases, these definitions place no restrictions on the computational efficiency of the learning algorithms, but rather focus on the *data efficiency*, which is our primary interest in this work (see Section [G\)](#page-68-0).

Since both the query complexity and sample complexity concern the number of *labels* sufficient for learning, it is natural to compare QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) with Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) to quantify the benefits of active learning. Thus, the primary interest in the theory of agnostic active learning is quantifying how much smaller QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) is compared to Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>). Since our interest is *agnostic* learning, it is most interesting to focus on the regime where P is far-from-realizable: that is, where β is much larger than ε. In this regime, it is well known from the works of [Vapnik and Chervonenkis](#page-16-4) [\(1974\)](#page-16-4); [Devroye and Lugosi](#page-12-4) [\(1995\)](#page-12-4); [Hanneke, Larsen, and Zhivotovskiy](#page-13-7) [\(2024b\)](#page-13-7) that the optimal sample

<sup>1</sup>To focus on non-trivial cases, we suppose |C| ≥ 3. We also suppose X is equipped with a σ-algebra specifying its measurable subsets, and we adopt the standard mild measure-theoretic restrictions on the σ-algebra and the class C from empirical process theory: namely, the image-admissible Suslin property [\(Dudley,](#page-12-5) [1999\)](#page-12-5).

complexity of passive learning satisfies <sup>M</sup>p(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ β ε 2 d + log δ . In comparison, the known lower bound for active learning is QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω β ε 2 d + log δ [\(Kääriäinen,](#page-14-2) [2006;](#page-14-2) [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009\)](#page-11-1). Thus, the strongest improvement we might hope from active learning is a factor of β (representing the *best-in-class* error rate).

However, in the prior literature, this β-factor improvement has only been demonstrated in upper bounds under *restrictions* to <sup>C</sup> or P. Specifically, every general upper bound on QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) in the literature has the form c(β)d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> (ignoring logs), where c(β) is a (C, P)-dependent quantity. For instance, one commonly appearing such quantity c(β) is the *disagreement coefficient* θ(β) of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0). We refer the reader to Appendix [A](#page-17-0) for a detailed survey of such quantities c(β) which have appeared in the literature. Importantly, for all such upper bounds in the literature, the corresponding factor c(β) has the property that there exist simple classes C and distributions P for which c(β) ≥ β (see [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015;](#page-13-2) [Hanneke,](#page-13-5) [2016b,](#page-13-5) [2024\)](#page-13-6): for instance, even for linear classifiers on R <sup>2</sup> or singletons on <sup>N</sup>. Note that when c(β) ≥ 1 β , a query complexity c(β)d β ε <sup>2</sup> becomes *no smaller* than d β ε <sup>2</sup> , the sample complexity of *passive* learning. Moreover, one can show that avoiding such d β ε <sup>2</sup> query complexities would require new algorithmic techniques (see Appendix [A\)](#page-17-0). Naturally, the question of refining such c(β) factors has been a subject of much interest for many years. In particular, it has remained open whether such factors might even be *avoided entirely*, so that the β-factor improvement might *always* be achievable. In a series of talks, I conjectured that the lower bound Ω β ε d + log 1 δ is *always* sharp (in the far-from-realizable regime), and even offered a sizable prize for a solution (along with lower-order terms) (e.g., [Hanneke and Nowak,](#page-13-8) [2019\)](#page-13-8).

Contributions of this Work: In the present work, we completely resolve this question. We prove that (in the above regime) QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ β ε 2 d + log δ . In other words, the β-factor improvement is *always* achievable, the known lower bound is *sharp*, and there is *no need* for restrictions on (C, P) or additional factors c(β) as appear in all prior works.

Extending to the *full range* of β, the more-general form of the bound we prove also includes an additive *lower-order* term to account for the small-β regime. In the simplest such bound (Theorem [1\)](#page-3-0), this lower-order term is simply O˜ d ε , so that the general form is QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = O˜ d β ε <sup>2</sup> + d ε (Theorem [3](#page-4-0) and Appendix [F](#page-51-0) refine this lower-order term for some classes). For comparison, the general form of the passive sample complexity is Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ˜ d β ε <sup>2</sup> + d ε . We note that, even in the *nearly-realizable* regime (β = O˜(ε)), it is known that <sup>d</sup> ε is a lower bound on the query complexity for many classes C [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014;](#page-13-1) see Appendix D of [Hanneke and](#page-13-2) [Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2), so that this term is sometimes unavoidable, and hence the benefits of active learning can wane in the nearly-realizable regime. Likewise, the lower bound d β ε <sup>2</sup> implies the benefits can also diminish in the very-high-noise regime (β = Ω(1)). In contrast, as discussed above, in the *far-from-realizable* regime (√ ε ≤ β ≪ 1), the bound is of order d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> , reflecting a β-factor improvement over the sample complexity of passive learning d β ε <sup>2</sup> . Additionally, the intermediate regime of *moderate-size* <sup>β</sup> (i.e., <sup>ε</sup> <sup>≪</sup> β < √ ε) *also* exhibits improvements over passive learning for all <sup>C</sup>: in this regime, <sup>M</sup>p(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω d β ε , whereas QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = O˜ d ε ≪ d β ε 2 , reflecting an improvement by a factor O˜( β ). Altogether, this result reveals a previously-unknown and truly remarkable fact: QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) ≪ Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) in all regimes ε ≪ β ≪ 1, or in other words, in all regimes outside the nearly-realizable and very-high-noise cases, the following is true:

*For every concept class* C*, the optimal query complexity of agnostic active learning is strictly smaller than the optimal sample complexity of agnostic passive learning.*

This result resolves an important long-standing open question central to the past two decades of research on the theory of agnostic active learning.

# 3 Main Results

Formally, the following theorem expresses the new upper bound, together with known lower bounds for comparison [\(Kääriäinen,](#page-14-2) [2006;](#page-14-2) [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009;](#page-11-1) [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). A more-detailed version of the result appears in Theorem [5](#page-33-0) (Appendix [C\)](#page-33-1).

Theorem 1. *For every concept class* C*, letting* d = VC(C)*,* ∀ε, δ ∈ (0, 1/8)*,* ∀β ∈ [0, 1]*,*

$$\mathbb{QC}_a(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta, \mathbb{C}) = O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) + \tilde{O}\left(\frac{\mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon}\right)$$

*and* QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω β ε d + log δ *. Moreover, for every* d ∈ N *there exists* C *with* VC(C) = d *such that* QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω β ε d + log δ + d ε 

We provide a new general active learning algorithm Aavid achieving this upper bound in Section [4.](#page-4-1) Importantly, the algorithm *does not need to know* β (or anything else about P) to achieve this guarantee: i.e., it is completely *adaptive* to the value β. Moreover, the number of *unlabeled* examples the algorithm requires is only Θ˜ d β ε <sup>2</sup> + d ε , of the same order as the sample complexity of passive learning; it can also adaptively determine how many unlabeled examples to use without knowing β.

The AVID Principle: The main innovation underlying the algorithm, which enables it to achieve this query complexity, represents a new principle for the design of active learning learning algorithms, which we call *Adaptive Variance Isolation by Disagreements* (AVID). The algorithm adaptively partitions the instance space X into *regions*, with the aim of *isolating* a region ∆ ⊆ X where it is most challenging to learn, due to exceptionally high *variance* in the error estimation problem in the ∆ region (where ∆ will be defined as a union of pairwise *disagreement* regions witnessing the high variance, carefully selected to ensure PX (∆) = O(β)). It then allocates disproportionately *more* queries to this challenging region ∆ compared to the (considerably-easier) remaining region X \ ∆. This idea has interesting connections to techniques explored in other branches of the literature (e.g., [Hanneke, Larsen, and Zhivotovskiy,](#page-13-7) [2024b;](#page-13-7) [Bousquet and Zhivotovskiy,](#page-11-2) [2021;](#page-11-2) [Puchkin and](#page-15-5) [Zhivotovskiy,](#page-15-5) [2022\)](#page-15-5), discussed in Appendix [A.](#page-17-0)

### 3.1 Refinement of the Lower-order Term for Some Classes

The AVID principle already suffices to achieve the query complexity bound in Theorem [1.](#page-3-0) Moreover, for *most* concept classes of interest, the query complexity bound in Theorem [1](#page-3-0) is already *optimal*, matching a lower bound (up to log factors in the lower-order term): e.g., linear classifiers in R k , k ≥ 2 [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). However, while the lead term β ε d + log δ is already optimal for every concept class <sup>C</sup>, there do exist some special classes <sup>C</sup> for which a further refinement of the *lower-order* term <sup>d</sup> ε is possible (e.g., threshold classifiers **1**[a,∞) on R). As our second main result, we provide a refinement of the upper bound in Theorem [1](#page-3-0) to capture such special classes, thereby establishing a query complexity bound which is nearly optimal for *every* concept class.

Since such refinements are only possible for some concept classes, the expression of this refinement necessarily depends on an additional complexity measure of the class C. We prove that the *optimal* lower-order term in the query complexity is well-captured by a quantity known as the *star number* of C, introduced by [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2). In particular, [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) showed that the star number precisely characterizes the optimal query complexity in the *realizable case* (β = 0); since this is a limiting case of agnostic learning, it is natural that this quantity plays a crucial role in characterizing the optimal lower-order term. The formal definition is as follows.

Definition 2. *For any concept class* C*, the* star number s = s(C) *is the supremum* n ∈ N *for which* ∃x1, . . . , x<sup>n</sup> ∈ X *and* h0, h1, . . . , h<sup>n</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> *such that* ∀i, j ∈ {1, . . . , n}*,* hi(x<sup>j</sup> ) ̸= h0(x<sup>j</sup> ) ⇔ i = j*.*

The star number essentially describes a scenario which is intuitively challenging for active learners in the realizable case, wherein there is a set of instances x<sup>j</sup> and a *default* labeling h0(x<sup>j</sup> ), but the *target* concept is some h<sup>i</sup> which differs from h<sup>0</sup> at just *one* instance x<sup>i</sup> , unknown to the learner (which must therefore query nearly all of these x<sup>j</sup> instances, searching for the special point x<sup>i</sup> , in order to identify the target concept hi). [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) provide numerous examples calculating s for various concept classes. For instance, thresholds on R have s = 2 and decision stumps on R <sup>k</sup> have s = 2k. However, it is worth noting that s is typically large (or infinite) for most concept classes of interest in learning theory (e.g., s = ∞ for linear classifiers on R k , k ≥ 2). This fact is important to the present work, since [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2); [Hanneke](#page-13-5) [\(2016b,](#page-13-5) [2024\)](#page-13-6) have shown that the c(β) factors (discussed in Section [2](#page-1-1) above) appearing in all previous general upper bounds *all* become no smaller than s ∧ β in the worst case over distributions (subject to the β constraint). Thus, all general upper bounds c(β)d β ε <sup>2</sup> from the prior literature become no smaller than d β ε <sup>2</sup> in the worst case when s = ∞. In a sense, this means Theorem [1](#page-3-0) is actually *most* interesting in the (typical) case of s = ∞, since *no* previously known upper bounds offer any improvements over passive learning in this case (without further restrictions to P), in stark contrast to Theorem [1](#page-3-0) which has *no dependence* on s and provides improvements over passive learning in the lead term for *every* concept class.

Nonetheless, the special structure of classes with s < ∞ turns out to provide some additional advantages for active learning, so that in order to state a general query complexity bound which is optimal for *every* concept class C, we need to account for this structure, via a dependence on s in the lower-order term. Specifically, by combining the AVID principle with existing principles for active learning (namely, *disagreement-based* queries), we can take further advantage of the power of active learning, thereby enabling a refinement of the lower-order term for classes with s < ∞. The following result presents a new general query complexity bound reflecting such refinements, together with a known lower bound for comparison (due to [Kääriäinen,](#page-14-2) [2006;](#page-14-2) [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta,](#page-11-1) [and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009;](#page-11-1) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). The implication is that this new upper bound is nearly optimal for *every* concept class C (including the lower-order term, up to a factor of d, which we discuss below). A more-detailed version of the result appears in Theorem [5](#page-33-0) of Appendix [C](#page-33-1) (and distribution-dependent variants are presented in Appendix [F,](#page-51-0) replacing s with variants of the *disagreement coefficient*).

Theorem 3. *For every* C*, letting* d = VC(C) *and* s = s(C)*,* ∀ε, δ ∈ (0, 1/8)*,* ∀β ∈ [0, 1]*,*

$$\begin{aligned} \text{QC}_a(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta, \mathbb{C}) &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) + \tilde{O}\left(\left( \mathbf{s} \wedge \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right) \mathbf{d} \right), \\ \text{and } \text{QC}_a(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta, \mathbb{C}) &= \Omega\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \mathbf{s} \wedge \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right). \end{aligned}$$

We may note that the upper bound in Theorem [1](#page-3-0) is an immediate implication of Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (we have stated Theorem [1](#page-3-0) separately merely to emphasize that the improvements over passive learning are available without any special properties of C such as finite star number). Theorem [3](#page-4-0) provides a refinement in the lower-order term compared to Theorem [1](#page-3-0) when s < ε . In particular, for s < ∞, the asymptotic dependence on ε in the lower-order term is log<sup>2</sup> ε . We leave open the question of whether this can be further refined to log ε , which would match a known lower bound on this dependence for all infinite classes [\(Kulkarni, Mitter, and Tsitsiklis,](#page-14-3) [1993;](#page-14-3) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). The only significant difference between the upper and lower bounds in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) is the factor of d in the lower-order term. I conjecture this term can be further refined to O˜ s ∧ d ε , which is known to be sharp for some classes [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2), and would fully answer a question posed by [Hanneke and Nowak](#page-13-8) [\(2019\)](#page-13-8). Beyond this, it is known that a gap between such lower-order terms in general upper and lower bounds is unavoidable if the only dependence on C is via d and s. Specifically, it follows from arguments in Appendix D of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) that for some classes C this term should be Θ˜ s ∧ d ε while for other classes <sup>C</sup> the term should be Θ˜ s ∧ 1 <sup>ε</sup> + d . Thus, obtaining matching (big-Θ) upper and lower bounds would require introducing a new complexity measure reflecting the distinctions between these types of classes, which we leave as an open question.

# 4 Algorithm and Outline of the Analysis

We next present the algorithm achieving Theorems [1](#page-3-0) and [3](#page-4-0) and a sketch of its analysis (the complete formal proof is given in Appendix [E\)](#page-36-0). Before stating the algorithm, we first introduce a few additional definitions and convenient notational conventions.

Error and disagreement regions: For any function h : X → {0, 1}, define its *error region*

$$\text{ER}(h) := \{(x, y) \in \mathcal{X} \times \{0, 1\} : h(x) \neq y\}.$$

In particular, note that er<sup>P</sup> (h) = P(ER(h)). For any set V ⊆ <sup>C</sup> define the *region of disagreement*: DIS(V ) := {x ∈ X : ∃f, g ∈ V, f(x) ̸= g(x)}. For any two functions f, g : X → {0, 1}, abbreviate by {f ̸= g} := {x ∈ X : f(x) ̸= g(x)} their *pairwise disagreement region*.

Overloaded set notation: For convenience, we adopt a convention of treating sets A ⊆ X as notationally interchangeable with their *labeled extension* A × {0, 1} ⊆ X × {0, 1}. For instance, for functions f, g, h : X → {0, 1}, we may write ER(h) ∩ {f ̸= g}, which, by the above convention, is interpreted as ER(h)∩({f ̸= g}×{0, 1}). We also overload notation for set intersections to allow for intersections of sets with *sequences*: that is, for any set Z, sequence S = {z1, . . . , zm} ∈ Z<sup>m</sup>, and set A ⊆ Z, we define S∩A as the *subsequence* {z<sup>i</sup> : i ≤ m, z<sup>i</sup> ∈ A}, and likewise S \A := S∩(Z \A). We also apply these conventions in combination: i.e., for a sequence S ∈ (X × {0, 1}) <sup>m</sup> and a set ∆ ⊆ X , we define S ∩ ∆ := S ∩ (∆ × {0, 1}) and S \ ∆ := S ∩ ((X \ ∆) × {0, 1}).

Empirical estimates: We will make use of *empirical estimates* of quantities such as er<sup>P</sup> (h) and PX(f ̸= g). For any set Z and sequence S = {z1, . . . , zm} ∈ Z<sup>m</sup>, for any set A ⊆ Z, define the *empirical measure*: Pˆ <sup>S</sup>(A) := <sup>1</sup> <sup>m</sup> |S ∩ A| = 1 m P<sup>m</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 **1**[z<sup>i</sup> ∈ A]. Again, we also apply these conventions in combination: i.e., for S ∈ (X × {0, 1}) ∗ and ∆ ⊆ X , we define Pˆ <sup>S</sup>(∆) := Pˆ <sup>S</sup>(∆ × {0, 1}). For any sequence S ∈ (X × {0, 1}) ∗ and function h : X → {0, 1}, define its *empirical error rate* (or *empirical risk*): ˆerS(h) := Pˆ <sup>S</sup>(ER(h)).

Decision lists: We will often express *decision-list* aggregations of functions f, g : X → {0, 1}. For instance, for any set ∆ ⊆ X , we may write h = f**1**X \<sup>∆</sup> + g**1**<sup>∆</sup> to express a function h with h(x) = f(x) for x /∈ ∆ and h(x) = g(x) for x ∈ ∆.

# 4.1 The AVID Agnostic Algorithm: Adaptive Variance Isolation by Disagreements

We are now ready to describe the algorithm achieving the upper bounds in Theorems [1](#page-3-0) and [3](#page-4-0) (for full formality, some additional technical minutiae for the definition are given in Section [C\)](#page-33-1). Fix any values ε, δ ∈ (0, 1) (the error and confidence parameters input to the learner). Fix any distribution P (unknown to the learner) and let (X1, Y1), . . . ,(Xm, Ym) be independent Pdistributed random variables (for any sufficiently large m, quantified explicitly in Theorem [5\)](#page-33-0). The algorithm is stated in Figure [1,](#page-6-0) expressed in terms of certain quantities and data subsets defined as follows.[<sup>2</sup>](#page-5-0) Let C := <sup>11</sup> <sup>10</sup> , <sup>N</sup> := log<sup>C</sup> 2 ε , and for each k ∈ <sup>N</sup> define ε<sup>k</sup> := C 1−k and <sup>m</sup><sup>k</sup> := Θ ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>d</sup> log 1 ε<sup>k</sup> + log δ (see Section [<sup>C</sup>](#page-33-1) for the precise constants). In Step [3,](#page-6-1) <sup>C</sup> ′ denotes an appropriate universal constant (see Section [C\)](#page-33-1). As defined in Figure [1,](#page-6-0) the algorithm makes use of different portions of the data (S k , S 2 k , S 3 k,i, S 4 k ) for different purposes, and to complete the definition of the algorithm we next specify how these data subsets are defined in the algorithm. We first split the initial 2M<sup>1</sup> := 2P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 m<sup>k</sup> examples {(X1, Y1), . . . ,(X2M<sup>1</sup> , Y2M<sup>1</sup> )} into consecutive disjoint contiguous segments S 1 1 , . . . , S<sup>1</sup> <sup>N</sup> +1, S<sup>4</sup> 1 , . . . , S<sup>4</sup> <sup>N</sup> +1, with the segments S 1 k and S 4 k being of size mk. The algorithm also allocates disjoint segments (S 2 k , S 3 k,i) of the remaining data {(X<sup>i</sup> , Yi) : 2M<sup>1</sup> < i ≤ m}, but does so *adaptively* during its execution. Specifically, if and when the algorithm reaches Step [2](#page-6-2) with a value k, or reaches Step [9](#page-6-3) (in which case let k = N + 1), for the value i<sup>k</sup> and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> as defined at that time in the algorithm, it constructs a data subset S 2 k , allocating to S 2 k the next m′ k consecutive examples which have not yet been allocated to any data subset S 1 k′ , S 2 <sup>k</sup>′ , S 3 k′ ,i′ , S 4 <sup>k</sup>′ (i.e., *fresh*, previously-unused, examples), where, letting <sup>p</sup>ˆ<sup>k</sup> := 2P<sup>ˆ</sup> S<sup>4</sup> k (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), we define m′ k := Θ pˆ<sup>k</sup> ε k d + log 3+N−k δ (see Section [C](#page-33-1) for the precise constants). Similarly, if and when the algorithm reaches Step [5](#page-6-4) with some values of (k, i), it constructs a data subset S 3 k,i, allocating to S 3 k,i the next m<sup>k</sup> consecutive examples which have not yet been allocated.

For simplicity, we have expressed the algorithm as representing a set of surviving concepts V<sup>k</sup> ⊆ C. However, it should be clear from the definition that running the algorithm does not require explicitly storing Vk. Rather, the various uses of this set can be implemented as constrained optimization problems (in Steps [4](#page-6-5)[-6](#page-6-6) and hˆk), where the constraints are merely the inequalities which would define the sets Vk′ , k ′ ≤ k, and Step [3](#page-6-1) is then replaced by simply adding one more constraint to the constraint set.

Algorithm Aavid Input: Error parameter ε, Confidence parameter δ, Unlabeled data X1, . . . , X<sup>m</sup> Output: Classifier hˆ 0. Initialize i = i<sup>1</sup> = 0, ∆<sup>0</sup> = ∅, V<sup>0</sup> = <sup>C</sup> 1. For k = 1, . . . , N 2. Query all examples in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> 3. V<sup>k</sup> ← n h ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> : ˆer1,2 k (h) ≤ ˆer1,2 k (hˆ <sup>k</sup>) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ o 4. If V<sup>k</sup> = ∅ or ˆer1,2 k (hˆ <sup>k</sup>) < minh∈V<sup>k</sup> ˆer1,2 k (h) − ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ , Then Return hˆ := hˆ k 5. While maxf,g∈V<sup>k</sup> <sup>P</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> S<sup>3</sup> k,i ({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+2 6. (f, g) ← argmax(<sup>f</sup> ′ ,g′)∈V k Pˆ S<sup>3</sup> k,i ({f ′ ̸= g ′} \ ∆i) 7. ∆i+1 ← ∆<sup>i</sup> ∪ {f ̸= g}, and update i ← i + 1 8. ik+1 ← i 9. Query all examples in S 1 <sup>N</sup> +1 ∩ DN \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>N</sup> +1 and S 2 <sup>N</sup> +1 ∩ <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup><sup>N</sup> +1 and Return <sup>h</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> := <sup>h</sup>ˆ<sup>N</sup> +1

Figure 1: The AVID Agnostic algorithm. Notations N, Dk−1, εk, hˆk, S <sup>k</sup>, S <sup>k</sup>, S k,i, ˆer1,2 k defined in the text.

To complete the definition of the algorithm, we define Dk−1, ˆer1,2 k , and hˆ <sup>k</sup>, appearing in the algorithm, as follows. For each value of k encountered in the '*For*' loop, as well as for k = N + 1 in the case the algorithm reaches Step [9,](#page-6-3) define (where Vk−<sup>1</sup> and ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> are as defined in the algorithm):

Dk−<sup>1</sup> := DIS(Vk−1),

$$\forall h, \operatorname{êr}_k^{1,2}(h) := \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\operatorname{ER}(h) \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + \hat{P}_{S_k^2}(\operatorname{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_{i_k}), \quad (1)$$

$$V_{k-1}^{(4)} := \{f \mathbb{1}_{\{f=g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}} + h_1 \mathbb{1}_{\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}} + h_2 \mathbb{1}_{\Delta_{i_k}} : f, g \in V_{k-1}, h_1, h_2 \in \mathbb{C}\}, \quad (2)$$

and 
$$\hat{h}_k := \operatorname{argmin}_{h \in V_{k-1}^{(4)}} \hat{e}_k^{1,2}(h)$$
. (3)

This completes the definition of the Aavid algorithm.

We remark that the examples in S 3 k,i and S 4 k are *never queried* in the algorithm, and thus the algorithm (necessarily) only uses the unlabeled X<sup>i</sup> values in these data subsets (to estimate certain marginal P<sup>X</sup> probabilities), so in fact these can be regarded as *unlabeled* data subsets. Similarly, the algorithm only queries a *portion* of S 1 k and S 2 k , and the remaining unqueried portions are in fact *never used* by the algorithm. For notational simplicity, we do not make these facts explicit in the notation.

Description of the algorithm: We briefly summarize the behavior of the algorithm (with explanations following in Section [4.2\)](#page-7-0). As the algorithm iterates over rounds k of the '*For*' loop, it maintains a partition of the space into a region ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and its complement X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> . In each round, the algorithm refines a set V<sup>k</sup> of surviving concepts from C, aiming to prune out suboptimal concepts (Step [3\)](#page-6-1). There are two crucial aspects of this, both in how the estimates of er<sup>P</sup> (h) are defined, and in the choice of function hˆ <sup>k</sup> to which we compare. For the purpose of error estimation, in Step [2](#page-6-2) it queries a number of random examples in X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (or rather, the slightly smaller region Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , since examples in X \ Dk−<sup>1</sup> are uninformative for estimating error *differences*) and a number of random examples in ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> . It uses the examples from each of the two regions to estimate the error rate of each h in that region, and combines these two estimates into an overall error estimate ˆer1,2 k (h) as in [\(1\)](#page-6-7). It then prunes suboptimal concepts from Vk−1, removing all h ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> having estimated error ˆer1,2 k (h) > ˆer1,2 k (hˆ <sup>k</sup>) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ . The reason ˆer1,2 k (h) estimates error rates in the two regions separately is that, as it will turn out, we require a disproportionately larger number of samples to accurately estimate the error rates in the region ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> compared to the complement X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> : for the latter, we use the samples in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (queried in Step [2\)](#page-6-2), where S 1 k has a modest size m<sup>k</sup> = Θ˜ d ε<sup>k</sup> , while for the former we use the samples in S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (also queried in Step [2\)](#page-6-2), where S 2 k has a potentially larger size m′ k roughly Θ˜ <sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (∆ik )d ε k . The other crucial aspect in Step [3](#page-6-1) is how we define the function hˆ <sup>k</sup> to which we compare. For this, rather than (the seemingly-natural idea of) simply comparing to the smallest ˆer1,2 k (h) among h ∈ Vk−1, we instead compare to an even smaller value: the smallest ˆer1,2 k (h) among a more-complex class V (4) k−1 defined in [\(2\)](#page-6-8), comprised of *decision list*

functions which use one concept h<sup>2</sup> for predictions in ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , and use (equivalently) a majority vote of three concepts f, g, h<sup>1</sup> for predictions in X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> . hˆ <sup>k</sup> is defined as a minimizer of ˆer1,2 k in V (4) k−1 , as in [\(3\)](#page-6-9). This use of a more-complex comparator function is critical for certain parts of the proof (namely, keeping PX (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) small). However, given that hˆ <sup>k</sup> is chosen from a more-complex class, it becomes possible that hˆ <sup>k</sup> may be *substantially better* than all h ∈ Vk. In this event, the algorithm terminates early and returns hˆ <sup>k</sup> (Step [4\)](#page-6-5). Otherwise, if it makes it past this early-stopping case, its next objective is to define the region ∆<sup>i</sup>k+1 for use in the next iteration. This occurs in the '*While*' loop (Steps [5-](#page-6-4)[7\)](#page-6-10). On each round of this loop, it uses a fresh data set S 3 k,i of size <sup>m</sup><sup>k</sup> <sup>=</sup> Θ˜ d ε<sup>k</sup> to check whether there exist f, g ∈ V<sup>k</sup> significantly *distant* from each other in the region X \ ∆<sup>i</sup> (Step [5\)](#page-6-4). If so, it adds their pairwise disagreement region {f ̸= g} to the ∆<sup>i</sup> region to define ∆i+1 and increments i (Step [7\)](#page-6-10). It repeats this until no such pair f, g exists, at which time it defines ik+1 = i (Step [8\)](#page-6-11) and proceeds to the next iteration of the '*For*' loop. After N = O(log(1/ε)) such iterations, it returns hˆ<sup>N</sup> +1 (Step [9\)](#page-6-3).

We note that the algorithm's returned classifier hˆ might *not* be an element of <sup>C</sup> (known as an *improper* learner), but rather can be represented as a (shallow) *decision list* of concepts from C. This aspect is quite important to certain parts of the proof, and we leave open the question of whether Theorems [1](#page-3-0) and [3](#page-4-0) are achievable by a proper learner (see Appendix [G\)](#page-68-0). We also remark that the Dk−<sup>1</sup> set is *only* needed for establishing Theorem [3:](#page-4-0) the algorithm achieves the query complexity bound in Theorem [1](#page-3-0) even if we replace Dk−<sup>1</sup> with the full space X everywhere.

# 4.2 Principles and Outline of the Proof

Next we explain the high-level principles underlying the design of the algorithm, highlighting the *two key innovations* compared to previous approaches, which enable the improved query complexity guarantee (namely, separating out the ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> regions, and the definition of hˆ <sup>k</sup>).

Empirical localization: The principles underlying the design of the algorithm begin with a familiar principle from statistical learning: *empirical localization* [\(Koltchinskii,](#page-14-4) [2006;](#page-14-4) [Bartlett,](#page-10-6) [Bousquet, and Mendelson,](#page-10-6) [2005\)](#page-10-6). Specifically, the uniform Bernstein inequality (Lemma [7\)](#page-35-0) implies that for an i.i.d. data set S, the sample complexity of uniform concentration of differences |( ˆerS(f) − ˆerS(g)) − (er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g))| becomes smaller when the *diameter* diam(C) = supf,g∈<sup>C</sup> PX (f ̸= g) of the concept class is small, noting that PX (f ̸= g) bounds the *variance* of loss differences **1**[f(x) ̸= y] − **1**[g(x) ̸= y]. Quantitatively, for any 0 < ε′ < diam(C), Θ˜ d diam(C) (ε ′) samples S suffice to guarantee |( ˆerS(f) − ˆerS(g)) − (er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g))| ≤ ε ′ . This fact leads to a natural well-known algorithmic principle, wherein we can *prune* from C concepts h having ˆerS(h) − minh′∈<sup>C</sup> ˆerS(h ′ ) > ε′ (as the above inequality implies these verifiably have suboptimal error rates), leaving a subset V ′ <sup>1</sup> of surviving concepts, while preserving h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V ′ 1 , where h ⋆ := argminh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h). Moreover, if these surviving concepts V ′ <sup>1</sup> have diam(V ′ 1 ) < diam(C), we get an *improved* concentration guarantee for ˆerS(f) − ˆerS(g) among f, g ∈ V ′ 1 from the uniform Bernstein inequality, which enables us to prune *even more* concepts from V ′ 1 , leaving a set V ′ <sup>2</sup> of surviving concepts, and so on for V ′ 3 , V ′ 4 , . . .. Quantitatively, we can combine this with a schedule of resolutions εk, so that as long as h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V ′ k−1 and diam(V ′ k−1 ) ≤ εk, an i.i.d. data set S 1 k of size m<sup>k</sup> = Θ˜ d ε<sup>k</sup> = Ω˜ d diam(V k−1 ) ε k suffices to guarantee ˆerS<sup>1</sup> k (f) − ˆerS<sup>1</sup> k (g) − er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g)   ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> C′ , enabling us to further reduce to a subset V ′ <sup>k</sup> = n h ∈ V ′ k−1 : ˆerS<sup>1</sup> (h) ≤ minh′∈<sup>V</sup> k−1 ˆerS<sup>1</sup> k (h ′ ) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ o for which all h ∈ V ′ k have er<sup>P</sup> (h) − er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) ≤ 2 ε<sup>k</sup> C′ , while preserving h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V ′ k . Iterating this N = Θ log<sup>C</sup> ε times (recalling ε<sup>k</sup> = C 1−k ) results in a subset V ′ N of concepts h with er<sup>P</sup> (h) − er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) ≤ ε.

Disagreement-based active learning: An additional observation, underlying many active learning algorithms (*disagreement-based* methods), is that the above argument still holds while replacing ˆerS<sup>1</sup> k (h) with Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(h)∩D′ k−1 ), where D′ k−1 := DIS(V ′ k−1 ). To see this, note that ∀h, h′ ∈ V ′ k−1 , Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(h)∩D′ k−1 )−Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(h ′ )∩D′ k−1 ) = ˆerS<sup>1</sup> k (h)− ˆerS<sup>1</sup> k (h ′ ). Thus, we may equivalently define V ′ <sup>k</sup> = n h ∈ V ′ k−1 : Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> (ER(h) ∩ D′ k−1 ) ≤ minh′∈<sup>V</sup> k−1 Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> (ER(h ′ ) ∩ D′ k−1 ) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ o . Moreover, as long as diam(V ′ k−1 ) ≤ εk, we have PX(D′ k−1 ) ≤ sε<sup>k</sup> [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). Since the quantities in V ′ k only rely on the labels of examples in D′ <sup>k</sup>−<sup>1</sup> ∩ S 1 k , constructing V ′ k only requires

a number of queries O(sεkmk) ∧ mk. Summing over k, these queries total to at most the claimed lower-order term in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (though note that even without this D′ k−1 refinement we still recover the lower-order term from Theorem [1\)](#page-3-0). So far, this is all essentially standard reasoning commonly followed in the prior literature on active learning (e.g., [Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Koltchinskii,](#page-14-5) [2010\)](#page-14-5).

Handling non-shrinking diameter: However, the above algorithmic principle breaks down if we reach a k with diam(V ′ k−1 ) ̸= O(εk). This failure can easily occur in the agnostic setting, where it is possible for the set V ′ k−1 above to contain multiple relatively-good functions f, g which are nevertheless *far* from each other.[<sup>3</sup>](#page-8-0) This is the motivation for the *first key innovation* in <sup>A</sup>avid: namely, if we ever reach such a k, where the V<sup>k</sup> set does not naturally have diam(Vk) ≤ εk+1 (as tested in Step [5\)](#page-6-4), the algorithm *removes* a portion of the space X to *artificially* reduce the diameter. Specifically, it identifies a pair f, g ∈ V<sup>k</sup> with PX(f ̸= g) > εk+1 (intuitively, an *obstruction* to having low diameter) and *separates out* their pairwise disagreement region {f ̸= g} from the region of focus of the algorithm (Steps [5-](#page-6-4)[7\)](#page-6-10).[<sup>4</sup>](#page-8-1) Having set aside this region, the algorithm continues, focusing on the remaining set X \ {f ̸= g}. This step is repeated, and these set-aside regions {f ̸= g} are altogether captured in the set ∆<sup>i</sup> (Step [7\)](#page-6-10). Thus, we repeatedly find pairs f, g ∈ V<sup>k</sup> with PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+1 (Steps [5-](#page-6-4)[6\)](#page-6-6) and add {f ̸= g} to ∆<sup>i</sup> (Step [7\)](#page-6-10) until the diameter of V<sup>k</sup> on X \ ∆<sup>i</sup> is reduced below εk+1. At that point, the algorithm proceeds to the next round (k ← k + 1). On the next round k, since we have (artificially) ensured the diameter of Vk−<sup>1</sup> is at most ε<sup>k</sup> in the region X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , the uniform Bernstein argument implies m<sup>k</sup> examples S 1 k suffice to guarantee every f, g ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> have Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(f) ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(g) ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) within ± ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>2</sup>C′ of P(ER(f) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − P(ER(g) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) .

Error in the ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> region: There remains the issue of estimating error rates in the ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> *isolated* region. For this, the algorithm uses a data set S 2 k of size m′ <sup>k</sup> ≈ d <sup>P</sup>X(∆ik ) ε k , queries all examples in S <sup>k</sup>∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , and uses these to estimate the error rates P(ER(h)∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) in the ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> region. By a refinement of the uniform convergence bound of [Talagrand](#page-15-6) [\(1994\)](#page-15-6) accounting for an *envelope* set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (Lemma [8\)](#page-35-1), this number m′ k of examples suffices to ensure Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> k (ER(h) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − P(ER(h) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> )  ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ for every h ∈ <sup>C</sup>. Combining this with the above error-differences estimates in the X \∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> region, we can guarantee that the functions f, g ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> have ˆer1,2 k (f) − ˆer1,2 k (g) − (er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g))  ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> C′ , recalling the definition of ˆer1,2 k from [\(1\)](#page-6-7). Altogether, we conclude that, as long as h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−1, a set V ′′ k := h ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> : ˆer1,2 k (h) ≤ minh′∈Vk−<sup>1</sup> ˆer1,2 k (h ′ ) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ would contain only functions h satisfying er<sup>P</sup> (h) − er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) ≤ 2 ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>C</sup>′ while preserving h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V ′′ k . The actual definition of V<sup>k</sup> in Step [3](#page-6-1) is only slightly different from this, for reasons we discuss next.

Bounding the size of ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> : Since the number of queries in S 2 <sup>k</sup>∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> is ≈ dPX (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) <sup>2</sup>/ε<sup>2</sup> k , if we hope to achieve a query complexity with lead term O˜ d β 2 ε it is crucial to guarantee PX (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = O(β). This is the motivation for the *second key innovation* in Aavid: defining the update in V<sup>k</sup> by comparison to the function hˆ <sup>k</sup> in [\(3\)](#page-6-9), rather than the best h ′ ∈ Vk−1. This turns out to be the most subtle part of the argument, requiring precise choices in the design of the algorithm. The essential argument is as follows. Suppose the algorithm reaches Step [6](#page-6-6) for some (k, i), so that it will add {f ̸= g} to the ∆<sup>i</sup> region. We then want to argue that P(ER(h ⋆ ) ∩ {f ̸= g} \ ∆i) = Ω(P({f ̸= g} \ ∆i)): that is, each time we add to ∆<sup>i</sup> , we *chop off* a portion of ER(h ⋆ ) of size (under P) proportional to the increase in PX (∆i). Clearly if we can show this is always the case, we will inductively maintain PX (∆i) = O(β), resulting in the claimed leading term in the query complexity. Now, to show this indeed occurs, we first note that one of f, g must err on at least *half* of {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ; w.l.o.g. suppose it is f: that is, P(ER(f) ∩ {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≥ 2 PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). Now consider a function f <sup>⋆</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>f</sup>**1**{f=g}\∆ik + h <sup>⋆</sup>**1**{f̸=g}\∆ik + f**1**∆ik which replaces f by h ⋆ in the region {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> . Note that, if h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−1, then f <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V (4) k−1 defined in [\(2\)](#page-6-8). Since hˆ <sup>k</sup> has minimal ˆer1,2 k

<sup>3</sup> For instance, for <sup>C</sup> the class of *intervals* **1**[a,b] on <sup>R</sup>, with P<sup>X</sup> = Uniform([0, 1]) and P(Y = 1|X) = **1**[0,1/4]∪[3/4,1](X), the concepts **1**[0,1/4] and **1**[3/4,1] are both optimal among <sup>C</sup>, yet distance 1/2 apart.

<sup>4</sup>This reasoning is somewhat reminiscent of the motivation for the *splitting* approach to active learning [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005\)](#page-12-0), differing only in how we resolve the obstruction: whereas splitting would resolve it with queries to eliminate one element from each obstructing pair, here we resolve it by subtracting the pairwise disagreement region from the region of focus X \∆<sup>i</sup> (see Appendix [A.2.3\)](#page-28-0). This idea is also related to a technique of [Hanneke, Larsen, and Zhivotovskiy](#page-13-7) [\(2024b\)](#page-13-7) for agnostic passive learning, discussed in Appendix [A.3.](#page-30-0)

among V (4) k−1 , and f ∈ V<sup>k</sup> implies ˆer1,2 k (f) ≤ ˆer1,2 k (hˆ <sup>k</sup>) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ , extending the above concentration of ˆer1,2 k differences to functions in V (4) k−1 (with appropriate adjustment of constants in mk, m′ k ) implies er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (f ⋆ ) ≤ 2 ε<sup>k</sup> C′ . Thus, since f ⋆ and f only disagree on {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , we have

$$\begin{aligned} & \frac{1}{2}P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h^*) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \\ & \leq P(\text{ER}(f) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h^*) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) = \text{er}_P(f) - \text{er}_P(f^*) \leq 2\frac{\varepsilon_k}{C_f}. \end{aligned}$$

In other words, P(ER(h ⋆ ) ∩ {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≥ 1 2 PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − 2 ε<sup>k</sup> C′ . This is almost what we wanted, aside from having ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> in place of ∆<sup>i</sup> . We then argue P(ER(h ⋆ ) ∩ {f ̸= g} \ ∆i) ≥ P(ER(h ⋆ ) ∩ {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) + PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i), which (by the above) is at least PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) − 2 PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − 2 ε<sup>k</sup> C′ . Since both f, g ∈ Vk−1, we know PX({f ̸= g} \∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ εk, so that this lower-bound is at least PX({f ̸= g} \∆i)− ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>2</sup> −2 ε<sup>k</sup> C′ . On the other hand, for appropriate constants in mk, the condition in Step [5](#page-6-4) allows us to upper-bound ε<sup>k</sup> in terms of PX({f ̸= g}\∆i): namely, PX({f ̸= g}\∆i) ≥ ε<sup>k</sup> c for c with C <sup>2</sup> < c ≤ 3 2 ∧ C ′ 9 . Thus, we have P(ER(h ⋆ )∩ {f ̸= g} \∆i) ≥ 1 − c <sup>2</sup> − 2 c C′ PX({f ̸= g} \∆i). Since each ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> is a union of such (disjoint) {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup> regions (i < ik), β ≥ P(ER(h ⋆ ) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≥ 1 − c <sup>2</sup> − 2 c C′ PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ).

The early stopping case: The above argument for PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = O(β) hinges on having h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−1. However, since hˆ <sup>k</sup> is a more-complex function than h ⋆ , there is a chance that h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈/ V<sup>k</sup> after Step [3.](#page-6-1) For this reason, we have added the early stopping case in Step [4.](#page-6-5) By using slightly tighter concentration inequalities than used to update Vk, this step effectively *tests* that hˆ <sup>k</sup> is not *so* much better than all concepts in Vk−<sup>1</sup> that h <sup>⋆</sup> might have been removed. Thus, if we make it past Step [4,](#page-6-5) we maintain h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V<sup>k</sup> so that the above argument applies on the next round. On the other hand, in the event that this test fails, we have effectively verified that hˆ <sup>k</sup> is at least *slightly better* than all concepts in Vk−<sup>1</sup> (including h ⋆ ), and we can safely return hˆ <sup>k</sup> in this case.

Overall behavior: The effective overall behavior of the algorithm is to *isolate* in the region ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> the most-challenging part of the error estimation problem, due to the high variance (diameter) of the error differences in that region. It then allocates a disproportionately larger number of queries S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> to this region, toward estimating the error rates there. By comparing with the function hˆ <sup>k</sup> (which separately optimizes errors in pairwise difference regions {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) in the definition of Vk, we can maintain that ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> never grows larger than O(β), so that the number of queries in S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> does not grow excessively large. The remaining region X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> enjoys the property that the set Vk−<sup>1</sup> has diameter ≤ εk, so that we can easily estimate error differences in this region by a uniform Bernstein inequality. Altogether, after at most N = O log ε rounds, this achieves the objective of ε excess error rate, while using a number of queries as stated in the query complexity bound in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0) The formal proof is given in Appendix [E.](#page-36-0)

# 5 Conclusions and Summary of the Appendices

This work resolves a long-standing open question of central importance to the theory of active learning, proving that *every* concept class benefits from active learning in the non-realizable case. Quantitatively, we establish a new sharp upper bound on the optimal query complexity, with leading term that is smaller than that of passive learning by a factor proportional to the best-in-class error rate.

The appendices include the formal proofs, along with additional contents. Appendix [A](#page-17-0) presents a thorough summary of related work and background on the theory of active learning, as well as other works with techniques related to those used here. Appendix [C](#page-33-1) presents remaining minutiae for the definition of Aavid, along with a more-detailed version of Theorem [3,](#page-4-0) including formal claims regarding the number of *unlabeled* examples. Appendix [E](#page-36-0) presents the formal proof of Theorem [3.](#page-4-0) Appendix [F](#page-51-0) presents distribution-dependent refinements of Theorem [3,](#page-4-0) which replace the star number s with certain P-dependent complexity measures: variants of the disagreement coefficient. We further argue that the disagreement coefficient θ<sup>P</sup> (ε), as originally defined by [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0), provably *cannot* be attained as a replacement for s in the lower-order term (by any algorithm), while on the other hand <sup>A</sup>avid *does* achieve a lower-order term O˜(θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) <sup>2</sup>d). We also present subregion-based refinements of the algorithm and analysis, based on techniques of [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1). Appendix [G](#page-68-0) presents extensions (*multiclass* classification, *stream-based* active learning), along with several open questions and future directions.

# A Survey of the Theory of Active Learning and Other Related Work

There is at this time quite an extensive literature on the theory of active learning. We refer the interested reader to the surveys of [Hanneke](#page-13-1) [\(2014\)](#page-13-1), [Dasgupta](#page-12-1) [\(2011\)](#page-12-1), and the 2019 ICML tutorial of [Hanneke and Nowak](#page-13-8) [\(2019\)](#page-13-8) for detailed discussions of classic works in this literature. In this section, we present a brief survey of the subject, with particular emphasis on the parts most-closely related to the present work.

## A.1 A Brief Historical Overview

The literature on active learning has a long history, dating back at least to the classical works on *experiment design* in statistics [\(Peirce,](#page-15-7) [1879;](#page-15-7) [Fisher,](#page-12-6) [1935\)](#page-12-6), wherein the analogous setting to active learning is referred to as *sequential design* (e.g., [Wald,](#page-16-5) [1947;](#page-16-5) [DeGroot,](#page-12-7) [1962;](#page-12-7) [Fedorov,](#page-12-8) [1972;](#page-12-8) [Atkinson and Donev,](#page-10-7) [1992;](#page-10-7) [Efromovich,](#page-12-9) [2007;](#page-12-9) [Zhang and Oles,](#page-16-6) [2000;](#page-16-6) [Paninski,](#page-15-8) [2005;](#page-15-8) [Lewi, Butera,](#page-14-6) [and Paninski,](#page-14-6) [2009;](#page-14-6) [Bull,](#page-11-3) [2013;](#page-11-3) [Naghshvar and Javidi,](#page-15-9) [2013;](#page-15-9) [Chaudhuri, Kakade, Netrapalli, and](#page-11-4) [Sanghavi,](#page-11-4) [2015\)](#page-11-4). Active learning has also been an important subject within the machine learning literature from the very beginning (e.g., [Popplestone,](#page-15-10) [1969;](#page-15-10) [Simon and Lea,](#page-15-11) [1974;](#page-15-11) [Buchanan,](#page-11-5) [1976;](#page-11-5) [Smith, Mitchell, Chestek, and Buchanan,](#page-15-12) [1977;](#page-15-12) [Mitchell,](#page-14-7) [1979\)](#page-14-7). Below we briefly mention some of the background of the subject in the *learning theory* literature, before giving detailed background of the literature on agnostic active learning.

Membership Queries: In the learning theory literature, the idea of active learning also appeared as a natural variant of the problem of *Exact learning with queries*. Specifically, in this setting, supposing there is an unknown *target concept* h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup>, the objective of the learner is to *exactly identify* h ⋆ . To achieve this goal, the learner has access to an oracle (who knows h ⋆ ), to which it may pose queries of a given type. The most relevant such queries (to the present work) are *membership queries*: namely, it may construct any x ∈ X and query for the value h ⋆ (x) (in later works in machine learning, this is sometimes known as *query synthesis*). Early discussion of this framework and corresponding algorithmic principles appear in the seminal work of [Mitchell](#page-14-7) [\(1979\)](#page-14-7). General analyses of the number of queries necessary and sufficient to identify h ⋆ (i.e., the *query complexity* of Exact learning) were developed in the works of [Angluin](#page-10-8) [\(1987\)](#page-10-8); [Hegedüs](#page-14-8) [\(1995\)](#page-14-8); [Hellerstein, Pillaipakkamnatt, Raghavan,](#page-14-9) [and Wilkins](#page-14-9) [\(1996\)](#page-14-9); [Nowak](#page-15-13) [\(2008,](#page-15-13) [2011\)](#page-15-14); [Hopkins, Kane, Lovett, and Mahajan](#page-14-10) [\(2020\)](#page-14-10), and a related average-case analysis was developed by [Dasgupta](#page-12-10) [\(2004\)](#page-12-10).

Closer to the setting considered in the present work, the idea of learning with membership queries has also been extensively studied in the context of PAC learning in the realizable case. In that setting, the learner observes i.i.d. samples (X<sup>i</sup> , Yi) with unknown distribution P, under the assumption that there exists an unknown target concept h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> with er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) = 0. The learner is additionally permitted to make membership queries for this concept h ⋆ , with the goal of producing a predictor hˆ having er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ ε with high probability 1 − δ. While most of the literature on PAC learning with membership queries has focused on the benefits of such queries for the *computational* complexity of learning (e.g., [Valiant,](#page-16-7) [1984;](#page-16-7) [Baum,](#page-10-9) [1991;](#page-10-9) [Jackson,](#page-14-11) [1997\)](#page-14-11), the literature also contains several works on the number of samples and queries for learning in this setting (e.g., [Eisenberg and Rivest,](#page-12-11) [1990;](#page-12-11) [Eisenberg,](#page-12-12) [1992;](#page-12-12) [Seung, Opper, and Sompolinsky,](#page-15-15) [1992;](#page-15-15) [Turán,](#page-15-16) [1993;](#page-15-16) [Kulkarni, Mitter, and](#page-14-3) [Tsitsiklis,](#page-14-3) [1993;](#page-14-3) [Diakonikolas, Kane, and Ma,](#page-12-13) [2024\)](#page-12-13).

Modern Active Learning with Label Queries: While the early literature on PAC learning with membership queries included several strong positive results (exhibiting advantages in both query complexity and computational complexity compared to learning from i.i.d. samples alone), when researchers implemented these algorithms and tried to use them for practical machine learning with a human labeler as the oracle, they found that the instances x ∈ X queried by the learner often turned out to be rather nonsensical, unnatural, or borderline cases between two labels (e.g., [Baum and Lang,](#page-10-10) [1992\)](#page-10-10). As such, human labelers were unable to provide useful answers to the queries, leading to poor performance of the learning algorithm. To address this issue, researchers turned to studying algorithms whose queries are restricted to only *natural* instances x ∈ X , which in most works (with a few notable exceptions, e.g., [Awasthi, Feldman, and Kanade,](#page-10-11) [2013\)](#page-10-11) essentially means x in the support of the marginal distribution PX : i.e., the types of examples that might occur naturally in the population. To actualize this restriction, researchers proposed a simple variant of active learning (which has become the standard framework in the literature, and is now essentially synonymous

with the term *active learning*), in which there are i.i.d. samples (X1, Y1), . . . ,(Xm, Ym) from an unknown distribution P, but the learner initially only observes the *unlabeled* examples X<sup>i</sup> , and can *query* to observe individual labels Y<sup>i</sup> (in a *sequential* fashion, so that it observes the label Y<sup>i</sup> of its previous query before selecting the next query X<sup>i</sup> ′ ) [\(Cohn, Atlas, and Ladner,](#page-11-6) [1994;](#page-11-6) [Freund, Seung,](#page-12-14) [Shamir, and Tishby,](#page-12-14) [1997;](#page-12-14) [Tong and Koller,](#page-15-0) [2001\)](#page-15-0). Such queries can typically be answered by human experts, being of the same type as used for data annotation in standard supervised machine learning. In this scenario, the unlabeled examples X<sup>i</sup> are typically assumed to be available in abundance, while obtaining the labels Y<sup>i</sup> is considered comparably more *expensive* (relying on the effort of a human expert), so that the primary objective is to minimize the number of *label queries* needed to achieve a given accuracy of a learned predictor hˆ. This is the setting studied in the present work.

The theoretical literature on this subject has origins in early works discussing algorithmic principles based on version spaces [\(Mitchell,](#page-14-7) [1979;](#page-14-7) [Cohn, Atlas, and Ladner,](#page-11-6) [1994\)](#page-11-6). Many of the early works providing actual bounds on the query complexity focused on showing improvements over passive learning for special scenarios, such as linear classifiers under distribution assumptions (e.g., [Freund,](#page-12-14) [Seung, Shamir, and Tishby,](#page-12-14) [1997;](#page-12-14) [Dasgupta, Kalai, and Monteleoni,](#page-12-15) [2005;](#page-12-15) [Har-Peled, Roth, and](#page-13-9) [Zimak,](#page-13-9) [2007;](#page-13-9) [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford,](#page-10-5) [2006;](#page-10-5) [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang,](#page-10-12) [2007;](#page-10-12) [Balcan](#page-10-2) [and Long,](#page-10-2) [2013;](#page-10-2) [Gonen, Sabato, and Shalev-Shwartz,](#page-13-10) [2013;](#page-13-10) [Wang and Singh,](#page-16-8) [2016;](#page-16-8) [Cavallanti,](#page-11-7) [Cesa-Bianchi, and Gentile,](#page-11-7) [2011;](#page-11-7) [Dekel, Gentile, and Sridharan,](#page-12-16) [2012\)](#page-12-16). This was followed by a boom of general-case analyses, providing general theories analyzing the query complexity for any concept class (e.g., [Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-11) [2007a,](#page-13-11)[b,](#page-13-0) [2009b,](#page-13-3)[a,](#page-13-12) [2011,](#page-13-13) [2012,](#page-13-14) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Dasgupta, Hsu,](#page-12-17) [and Monteleoni,](#page-12-17) [2007;](#page-12-17) [Balcan, Hanneke, and Vaughan,](#page-10-13) [2010;](#page-10-13) [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009;](#page-11-1) [Koltchinskii,](#page-14-5) [2010;](#page-14-5) [Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014;](#page-16-1) [El-Yaniv and Wiener,](#page-12-18) [2012;](#page-12-18) [Wiener, Hanneke,](#page-16-9) [and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015;](#page-16-9) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015;](#page-13-2) [Hanneke, Karbasi, Moran, and Velegkas,](#page-13-15) [2024a\)](#page-13-15), some of which are discussed in more detail below.

Agnostic PAC Learning: The PAC learning framework has also been extended to allow *nonrealizable* distributions P, that is, removing the restriction that infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) = 0. This framework was abstractly formulated in the classic work of [Vapnik and Chervonenkis](#page-16-4) [\(1974\)](#page-16-4), with interest in the computer science literature initiated by the works of [Haussler](#page-14-12) [\(1992\)](#page-14-12); [Kearns, Schapire, and Sellie](#page-14-13) [\(1994\)](#page-14-13). Since such non-realizable distributions P might not allow for predictors hˆ with er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ ε, the objective in this framework changes to merely achieving a *relatively* low error rate compared to the best error rate achievable by concepts in the class C. More precisely, we aim to produce a predictor hˆ which, with probability at least 1 − δ, satisfies er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε. The goal is to achieve this objective, for *every* distribution P, without *any* restrictions. This framework is termed *agnostic PAC learning*, to emphasize that we do not assume any special knowledge of P when designing such a learning algorithm [\(Kearns, Schapire, and Sellie,](#page-14-13) [1994\)](#page-14-13).

While an agnostic learning algorithm should achieve this objective for *every* distribution P, this need not restrict the *analysis* of such learners to consider only the worst case over all P. In particular, in the present work, we are primarily interested in analyzing the number of queries necessary and sufficient for agnostic active learning, as a function of the *best-in-class* error rate infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h), known as a *first-order* query complexity bound. Precisely, as introduced in Section [2,](#page-1-1) for every ε, δ, β ∈ (0, 1), we denote by QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) the minimax optimal first-order query complexity: that is, the minimal Q ∈ <sup>N</sup> for which there exists an active learning algorithm <sup>A</sup><sup>a</sup> such that (for a sufficiently large number m of unlabeled examples), for every distribution P with infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β, with probability at least 1 − δ, <sup>A</sup><sup>a</sup> makes at most Q queries and returns a predictor hˆ satisfying er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε. While, in principle, this definition of QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) admits learners which explicitly depend on knowledge of β, we will find that the optimal query complexity is achievable (up to constant factors and lower-order terms) simultaneously for all β by an active learner which does *not* require knowledge of β. Such a learner is said to be *adaptive* to β. In particular, such a learner is therefore an agnostic PAC learner, and the β restriction only enters in its analysis.

The Passive Learning Baseline: Since the predictor hˆ produced by an active learning algorithm is based on its queried subset of a given set of i.i.d. examples (X<sup>i</sup> , Yi), the natural quantity for comparison is the number of *i.i.d. labeled* examples necessary to obtain the same accuracy: i.e., the sample complexity of standard supervised learning, which in this literature is termed *passive*

*learning*. [<sup>5</sup>](#page-19-0) Recall from Section [2](#page-1-1) that we denote by Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) the minimax optimal sample complexity of passive learning: i.e., the minimal n such that there exists a passive learning algorithm <sup>A</sup><sup>p</sup> that, for every P with infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β, for S ∼ P <sup>n</sup> and hˆ <sup>n</sup> = <sup>A</sup>p(S), guarantees with probability at least 1 − δ that er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ <sup>n</sup>) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε. Since we can always design an active learner that simply queries the first n examples and runs a passive learner Ap, we clearly always have QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) ≤ Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>). Thus, the main question of interest is whether QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) is *strictly smaller* than Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>), and if so, by how much. Lower bounds of [Vapnik and](#page-16-4) [Chervonenkis](#page-16-4) [\(1974\)](#page-16-4); [Devroye and Lugosi](#page-12-4) [\(1995\)](#page-12-4) establish that

$$\mathcal{M}_p(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta, \mathbb{C}) = \Omega\left(\frac{\beta}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right), \quad (4)$$

recalling that d denotes the *VC dimension* of C [\(Vapnik and Chervonenkis,](#page-16-3) [1971;](#page-16-3) see Definition [4](#page-32-0) of Appendix [B\)](#page-32-1). The classic analysis of [Vapnik and Chervonenkis](#page-16-4) [\(1974\)](#page-16-4) further established this lower bound can nearly be achieved by the simple method of *empirical risk minimization*, i.e., hˆ <sup>n</sup> = argminh∈<sup>C</sup> ˆerS(h), providing an upper bound Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = O β ε 2 d log ε + log δ + ε d log ε + log δ . This has since been refined in various ways, such as via localized chaining arguments (e.g., [Giné and Koltchinskii,](#page-13-16) [2006\)](#page-13-16). Most recently, [Hanneke, Larsen, and Zhivotovskiy](#page-13-7) [\(2024b\)](#page-13-7) proved an upper bound Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = O β ε d + log δ + O˜ 1 ε d + log 1 δ , matching the lower bound [\(4\)](#page-19-1) up to log factors in the lower-order term (the problem of removing these remaining log factors remains open at this time). The algorithm achieving this is *improper*, meaning its returned hˆ <sup>n</sup> is not necessarily an element of C, and [Hanneke, Larsen, and Zhivotovskiy](#page-13-7) [\(2024b\)](#page-13-7) in fact show that for some concept classes C improperness is *necessary* to match the lower bound [\(4\)](#page-19-1) in the lead term, as all proper learners incur an extra log β factor. In the special case of β = 0 (the *realizable case*), the lower bound [\(4\)](#page-19-1) was shown to be achievable by [Hanneke](#page-13-4) [\(2016a\)](#page-13-4) (also necessarily via an improper learner), so that Mp(ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ ε d + log δ . The lower bound [\(4\)](#page-19-1) will therefore serve as a suitable baseline for gauging whether the query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) of active learning is smaller than the sample complexity Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) of passive learning.

The Need for Distribution-dependent Analysis in Realizable Active Learning: Much of the early work on active learning focused on the *realizable case*, i.e., the special case β = 0. In this special case, it was quickly observed by [Dasgupta](#page-12-10) [\(2004,](#page-12-10) [2005\)](#page-12-0) that there are some concept classes (e.g., *thresholds* **1**[a,∞) on <sup>R</sup>) where active learning offers strong improvements over passive learning, and other concept classes (e.g., *intervals* **1**[a,b] on <sup>R</sup>) where the (distribution-free) minimax query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, C) offers *no significant improvements* over passive learning. The essential advantage in the former case arises from a kind of "binary search" behavior, where the "uncertainty" is being sequentially reduced by a careful choice of queries. In contrast, the essential challenge in the latter case is the problem of "searching in the dark" for a small-but-important region: e.g., the optimal concept is 1 for a single unknown x<sup>i</sup> among some x1, . . . , x1/ε, and PX = Uniform({x1, . . . , x1/ε}). It turns out this *hard* scenario is embedded in many concept classes of interest, a fact which was formalized and quantified by [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) in the *star number* complexity measure (Definition [2\)](#page-3-1) discussed below. Such concept classes C naturally exhibit a lower bound QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω 1 ε . Even worse, consider a scenarios where the optimal concept can be 1 for any d points x<sup>i</sup> among x1, . . . , xd/(2ε) , and PX = Uniform({x1, . . . , xd/(2ε)}). [Hanneke and](#page-13-2) [Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) show this scenario has QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω d ε , so that QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, C) has the same joint dependence on (d, ε) as passive learning Mp(ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ ε d + log δ , only offering

<sup>5</sup> Since the active learner also has access to the remaining (unqueried) i.i.d. *unlabeled examples* Xi, it is also natural to compare to the related framework of *semi-supervised* learning, in which a learner has access to some number n of i.i.d. labeled examples with distribution P and additionally some larger number m of i.i.d. *unlabeled* examples with distribution PX [\(Chapelle, Scholkopf, and Zien,](#page-11-8) [2006\)](#page-11-8). While, under some favorable conditions, the labeled sample complexity n of semi-supervised learning can be smaller than that of strictly-supervised passive learning (see [Balcan and Blum,](#page-10-14) [2010\)](#page-10-14), the lower bounds on the (distribution-free) sample complexity of passive learning discussed in this work remain valid for the labeled sample complexity of semi-supervised learning (regardless of how many unlabeled examples are available), so that for the purpose of comparison in the present work, the distinction between supervised and semi-supervised passive learning as a baseline is not important, and we will simply compare to passive supervised learning for simplicity.

an improvement in the dependence on δ (where δ thus only affects the *unlabeled* sample complexity). Moreover, as noted by [Hanneke](#page-13-1) [\(2014\)](#page-13-1), similar scenarios[<sup>6</sup>](#page-20-0) are embedded (with d = Θ(VC(C))) in most concept classes C of interest in learning theory (e.g., linear classifiers in R 3d , and axis-aligned rectangles in R 2d ), so that such classes also exhibit no significant improvements over passive learning in their (distribution-free minimax) realizable-case query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, C).

Motivated by the fact that such *hard* scenarios are embedded in many concept classes of interest, [Dasgupta](#page-12-0) [\(2005\)](#page-12-0) suggested that, for such concept classes, the only viable way to understand the potential advantages of active learning is to focus on *distribution-dependent* analysis, toward identifying special scenarios where active learning algorithms offer improvements over passive learning, by formulating appropriate assumptions on the distribution P. This narrative quickly caught on in the literature, with a variety of distribution-dependent analyses and general P-dependent complexity measures being proposed to analyze certain active learning strategies under various restrictions on the realizable distribution P [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-11) [2007a](#page-13-11)[,b,](#page-13-0) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang,](#page-10-12) [2007;](#page-10-12) [Balcan and Long,](#page-10-2) [2013;](#page-10-2) [Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014;](#page-16-1) [El-Yaniv and Wiener,](#page-12-18) [2012;](#page-12-18) [Wiener,](#page-16-9) [Hanneke, and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015\)](#page-16-9). We discuss several of these in detail below.

Active Learning in the Non-realizable Case: Given the above narrative, when approaching the analysis of active learning in the *non-realizable* case (β > 0), it might at first seem perfectly reasonable to expect that for many concept classes <sup>C</sup> the query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) might *not* be much smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>). As such, the literature on agnostic active learning has largely focused on *extending* the distribution-dependent analyses from the realizable case to the agnostic setting (e.g., [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford,](#page-10-5) [2006,](#page-10-5) [2009;](#page-10-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b,](#page-13-0)[a,](#page-13-11) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011,](#page-13-13) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Balcan and Hanneke,](#page-10-15) [2012;](#page-10-15) [Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014;](#page-16-1) [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015\)](#page-16-9). These upper bounds conformed to the accepted narrative, in that they offer improvements under some distributions, but for most classes C, in the worst case over distributions P (with infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β) they essentially revert to the passive sample complexity Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>).

However, since this narrative was born from analysis of the *realizable case*, there was no actual reason to believe it should remain valid in the non-realizable case. In particular, there remained an intriguing possibility that there could perhaps be *other advantages* of active learning *specific to the non-realizable case*: that is, beyond the "binary search" type advantages known from the realizable case (as captured by the complexity measures proposed for realizable-case analysis). Some hints that such additional advantages may exist appear in the works of [Efromovich](#page-12-9) [\(2007\)](#page-12-9) and [Hanneke](#page-13-2) [and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) studying certain special scenarios (noise models more-restrictive than the agnostic setting), which found that active learning can also be useful for *adaptively identifying noisy regions* (i.e., regions of X's where P(Y |X) is close to <sup>1</sup> 2 ) and allocating queries appropriately to compensate for this noisiness without wasting excessive queries in less-noisy regions (as passive learning would). This additional advantage, specific to the non-realizable case, offered quantitative advantages over passive learning under the specific conditions studied in those works (e.g., [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015](#page-13-2) showed improvements in query complexity under certain noise models, namely Tsybakov noise and Benign noise, for all classes C, including those with the "searching in the dark" scenario embedded in them). However, these works left open the question of whether such advantages can be observed also in the more-challenging *agnostic* setting.
