The extension to the agnostic case is not at all clear, since in this setting (unlike the special noise models in the works above) the source of non-realizability is not only the noisiness of the P(Y |X) label distribution, but also *model misspecification*: i.e., it is possible to have β > 0 even when P(Y |X) ∈ {0, 1}, if the *Bayes classifier* is not in C, in which

<sup>6</sup>The hard scenario embedded in these classes is slightly more structured. Namely, for any d ∈ <sup>N</sup> and infinite X , partition X into disjoint infinite subsets X1, . . . , Xd, and define C<sup>d</sup> = {h : ∀i ≤ d, P x∈X<sup>i</sup> h(x) ≤ 1}, which has VC(Cd) = d. For such classes <sup>C</sup>d, [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) show that QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>d) = Ω d ε (as an aside, it is a straightforward exercise to show a matching upper bound for this class). For homogeneous linear classifiers in R 3d , we can construct the X<sup>i</sup> sets as *circles* in disjoint subspaces: i.e., X<sup>i</sup> is all (z1, . . . , z3d) ∈ <sup>R</sup> 3d s.t. all coordinates z<sup>j</sup> = 0 except z 2 3(i−1)+1 +z 2 3(i−1)+2 = 1 and z3(i−1)+3 = 1 (to allow for non-homogeneous linear classifiers on these circles, each controlled using 3 distinct weights in the 3d-dimensional linear classifier); the classifiers with boundary tangent to these circles witness the d singleton problems in Cd. For axis-aligned rectangles in R 2d , we can construct the X<sup>i</sup> sets as diagonal *lines* in disjoint 2-dimensional subspaces: i.e., X<sup>i</sup> is all (z1, . . . , z2d) ∈ <sup>R</sup> <sup>2</sup><sup>d</sup> with all z<sup>j</sup> = 0 except z2(i−1)+1 ∈ [0, 1] and z2(i−1)+2 = 1 − z2(i−1)+1; as each X<sup>i</sup> can be classified by a 2-dimensional rectangle based on 2 distinct coordinates, the classifiers with a single *corner* intersecting each of these diagonal lines then witness the d singleton problems in Cd.

case the idea of adapting to "noisiness" of labels is no longer a useful framing of the problem. (The present work finds the appropriate re-framing of this capability, replacing the notion of "noisiness" by the *variance* of the excess-error estimation problem, and identifies algorithmic principles for isolating such high-variance regions, constituting the AVID principle).

Quantifying the Query Complexity (Realizable Case): As discussed above, motivated by negative results for distribution-free analysis in the realizable case, numerous works have studied the query complexity of active learning under restrictions on the distribution P. These distribution-dependent analyses are often expressed in terms of abstract P-dependent complexity measures intended to capture favorable conditions for active learning, compared to passive learning. Quantitatively, in the realizable case (β = 0), such P-dependent query complexity bounds are typically expressed in the form c<sup>P</sup> (ε) · d · polylog εδ for some P-dependent complexity measure c<sup>P</sup> (ε). Examples of such complexity measures include the splitting index [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005\)](#page-12-0), disagreement coefficient [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b,](#page-13-0) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011\)](#page-13-13), empirical extended teaching dimension [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-11) [2007a\)](#page-13-11), and a subregion variant of the disagreement coefficient [\(Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014\)](#page-16-1), among others (e.g., [El-Yaniv](#page-12-18) [and Wiener,](#page-12-18) [2012;](#page-12-18) [Hanneke,](#page-13-14) [2012,](#page-13-14) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015;](#page-16-9) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). Some of these were accompanied by related minimax lower bounds holding for any fixed PX marginal distribution [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-11) [2007a;](#page-13-11) [Balcan and Hanneke,](#page-10-15) [2012\)](#page-10-15). We discuss several of these P-dependent analyses and c<sup>P</sup> (ε) complexity measures in detail in Appendix [A.2.](#page-23-0)

The works of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2); [Hanneke](#page-13-5) [\(2016b,](#page-13-5) [2024\)](#page-13-6) later showed that all of these proposed complexity measures c<sup>P</sup> (ε) have worst-case values (i.e., sup<sup>P</sup> c<sup>P</sup> (ε) over realizable P) *precisely equal* a complexity measure s therein termed the *star number* of C (Definition [2\)](#page-3-1), a quantity which abstractly formalizes and quantifies the extent to which the aforementioned "searching in the dark" scenario is embedded in a given concept class C. Thus, the star number *unifies* all of these complexity measures in the case of distribution-free analysis. Moreover, [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) also show the star number sharply characterizes the optimal distribution-free query complexity in the realizable case: namely,

$$\mathfrak{s} \wedge \left( \frac{1}{\varepsilon} + \mathbf{d} \right) \lesssim \mathbb{Q}\mathbb{C}_a(\varepsilon, \delta; 0, \mathbb{C}) \lesssim \left( \mathfrak{s} \wedge \frac{\mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon} \right) \log\left( \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right) \quad (5)$$

(see [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015,](#page-13-2) for more-detailed bounds). [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) also show the upper and lower bounds in [\(5\)](#page-21-0) represent a *nearly-sharp* dependence on (d, s): that is, there exist concept classes <sup>C</sup>d,<sup>s</sup> and <sup>C</sup>d,<sup>s</sup> of any given VC dimension d and star number s ≥ d for which QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>d,<sup>s</sup> ) = Θ s ∧ <sup>ε</sup> + d and QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>d,s) = Θ s ∧ d ε . Thus, the bounds in [\(5\)](#page-21-0) are essentially *unimprovable* (up to a log factor) without introducing additional complexity measures for the class <sup>C</sup>. In particular, this also means any upper bound on QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, C) depending on C only via d and s can be no smaller than Ω s ∧ d ε .

The bounds in [\(5\)](#page-21-0) imply that QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, C) admits an improved dependence on ε compared to Mp(ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ 1 ε d + log 1 δ *if and only if* s < ∞. While there exist some interesting concept classes C with finite star number (e.g., threshold classifiers on R, decision stumps on R p ), it turns out *most* concept classes of interest in learning theory have infinite (or very large) star number (e.g., s = ∞ for linear classifiers on R p , p ≥ 2). Thus, the general bounds in [\(5\)](#page-21-0) quantitatively reflect the fact (already observed in several cases by [Dasgupta,](#page-12-10) [2004,](#page-12-10) [2005\)](#page-12-0) that we should typically *not* expect any significant benefits of active learning in the realizable case to be reflected in the (distribution-free minimax) query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; 0, C). Moreover, as mentioned above, the concept classes <sup>C</sup>d,<sup>s</sup> with s = ∞ witnessing near-sharpness of the upper bound in [\(5\)](#page-21-0) are also embedded in many concept classes of interest in learning theory (see footnote [6\)](#page-20-0), further strengthening the lower bound to Ω d ε for such classes, thus matching the sample complexity Mp(ε, δ; 0, <sup>C</sup>) of passive learning in all dependencies (except δ).

Quantifying the Query Complexity (Agnostic Case): Turning to the agnostic case (β ≥ 0), [Kääriäinen](#page-14-2) [\(2006\)](#page-14-2) established a general lower bound QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Ω β ε <sup>2</sup> log δ , later strengthened by [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford](#page-11-1) [\(2009\)](#page-11-1) to

$$\mathbb{Q}C_a(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta, \mathbb{C}) = \Omega\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( d + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right)\right). \quad (6)$$

Comparing this to the sample complexity of passive learning (discussed above), namely <sup>M</sup>p(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) = Θ β ε d + log δ + Θ˜ 1 ε d + log 1 δ , we see that in the regime β ≫ √ ε, the best improvement we can hope for from active learning would be to replace the factor β with β 2 : i.e., squaring the dependence on the best-in-class error rate infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h). In the regime β ≲ √ ε, the realizable-case lower bound from [\(5\)](#page-21-0) becomes relevant (the realizable case being a special case, since clearly infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> = 0 satisfies the condition infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β), which may be thought of as a *lower-order* additive term.

The work of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford](#page-10-5) [\(2006\)](#page-10-5) initiated the study of upper bounds on the query complexity in the agnostic case, showing that the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1) can be matched in the special cases of *threshold* classifiers (concepts **<sup>1</sup>**[a,∞) on <sup>X</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>R</sup>), and (in the regime <sup>β</sup> <sup>≲</sup> ε/√ d) matched up to a factor d for homogeneous linear classifiers under PX uniform in an origin-centered ball, extending these well-known examples from the realizable case. This analysis was generalized to *all* concept classes by [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0), expressing a query complexity bound of the form O˜ c<sup>P</sup> (β + ε)d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1, where the factor <sup>c</sup><sup>P</sup> (<sup>β</sup> <sup>+</sup> <sup>ε</sup>) is based on a <sup>P</sup>-dependent complexity measure θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) therein termed the *disagreement coefficient* (Definition [25\)](#page-51-1). In particular, the bound of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) matches the lower bound of [Kääriäinen](#page-14-2) [\(2006\)](#page-14-2); [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta,](#page-11-1) [and Langford](#page-11-1) [\(2009\)](#page-11-1) up to logs only when θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = O˜(1). The latter holds for threshold classifiers, and for other classes under restrictions on P, but in many other cases θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) can be as large as <sup>1</sup> β+ε due to the "searching in the dark" problem discussed above; in such cases, the query complexity upper bound of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) is no smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>). Numerous later works (some discussed in detail below) discovered refinements and alternative P-dependent complexity measures used to express upper bounds on the query complexity [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-11) [2007a;](#page-13-11) [Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni,](#page-12-17) [2007;](#page-12-17) [Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011,](#page-13-13) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014;](#page-16-1) [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015\)](#page-16-9). However, like the bound of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0), all of these results establish query complexity upper bounds of the form O˜ c<sup>P</sup> (β + ε)d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 for some <sup>P</sup>-dependent complexity measure <sup>c</sup><sup>P</sup> (<sup>β</sup> <sup>+</sup> <sup>ε</sup>), all of which have the property that, in the "searching in the dark" type scenarios discussed above, the value c<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) ≥ 1 β+ε , so that in such scenarios these upper bounds are all no smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>).

As in the realizable case, these various analysis were later all unified under worst-case analysis over P by the *star number* in the work of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2). Indeed, these complexity measures c<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) are in fact the same family of complexity measures alluded to above for the realizable case. As such, by the aforementioned result of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2), they all satisfy sup<sup>P</sup> c<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = s∧ 1 β+ε . Thus, the upper bounds established by these works, all being of the form O˜ c<sup>P</sup> (β + ε)d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1, unify to a single upper bound of the form <sup>O</sup>˜ <sup>s</sup> <sup>∧</sup> 1 β+ε d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 in the worst case over distributions P (satisfying infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β). In particular, this also means they all fail to imply any improvements over the sample complexity of passive learning Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) in the worst case over such distributions P, for any concept class C with s = ∞. [<sup>7</sup>](#page-22-0) This is significant, since (as discussed above) most commonly-studied concept classes have s = ∞, including, for instance, linear classifiers in R p , p ≥ 2. On the other hand, the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1), of the form Ω β ε d + log 1 δ , has no such factor s ∧ 1 β+ε . The natural question is therefore which of these can be strengthened: the upper bound or lower bound.

The above gap has a qualitative significance. If the lower bound could be strengthened to match the upper bound, it would mean that (as in the realizable case) there are classes where active learning offers no advantage in its minimax query complexity compared to passive learning. On the other hand, if the upper bound can be strengthened to match the lower bound, it would mean that (unlike the realizable case) the query complexity of active learning is *always* smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning in the agnostic setting: i.e., for *every* concept class. The problem of resolving this gap has remained open until now. In the present work, we completely resolve this question,

<sup>7</sup>One can also show that this is not merely a result of loose analysis. The algorithms (prior to the present work) can be made to behave similarly to passive learners (meaning they query almost indiscriminately) in some scenarios constructed on large star sets, resulting in a number of queries <sup>β</sup> <sup>ε</sup><sup>2</sup> .

strengthening the upper bound to match the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1), thereby establishing that active learning is *always* better than passive learning in the agnostic case, providing an improvement by squaring the dependence on the best-in-class error rate in the leading term: i.e., replacing β with β . Establishing this upper bound requires a new principle for active learning, specific to the agnostic setting, which we develop in this work (termed AVID, for *Adaptive Variance Isolation by Disagreements*).

Before proceeding with the presentation of our results, we first provide, in the next subsection, a detailed survey of several of the prior works mentioned in the above brief historical summary.

# A.2 Detailed Description of Relevant Techniques in the Prior Literature

In this subsection, we provide further details of relevant works in the literature. Due to the vastness and diversity of the literature on the theory of active learning, we will not provide an exhaustive survey here, instead focusing on the techniques and results most-relevant to the present work.

# A.2.1 Disagreement-based Active Learning

By-far the most well-studied technique in the literature on the theory of active learning is *disagreementbased* active learning. A disagreement-based active learner is given as input the sequence X1, X2, . . . , X<sup>m</sup> of unlabeled examples. It maintains (either explicitly or implicitly) a set V ⊆ <sup>C</sup> of *surviving concepts* (known as a *version space*), with a guarantee that the *best-in-class* concept[<sup>8</sup>](#page-23-1) h ⋆ is always retained in V . To choose its query points, it finds the next unlabeled example X<sup>i</sup> in the sequence for which ∃f, g ∈ V with f(Xi) ̸= g(Xi), and queries for the label Y<sup>i</sup> : or more succinctly, it queries the next X<sup>i</sup> ∈ DIS(V ), where

$$\text{DIS}(V) := \{x \in \mathcal{X} : \exists f, g \in V, f(x) \neq g(x)\},$$

denotes the *region of disagreement* of V . It then updates the set V of surviving concepts based on this new information (or, in some variants, it performs this update only periodically, rather than after every query). This is abstractly summarized in the following outline (where Step 4 can be instantiated in various ways, as discussed below).

Algorithm Outline: Disagreement-based Active Learning Input: Unlabeled data X1, . . . , X<sup>m</sup> Output: Classifier hˆ 0. Initialize V = C 1. For i = 1, 2, . . . , m 2. If X<sup>i</sup> ∈ DIS(V ) 3. Query for label Y<sup>i</sup> 4. Update V 5. Return any hˆ ∈ V

The idea is that, if we seek to return a concept hˆ ∈ V with small er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) − er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ), then for any X<sup>i</sup> ∈/ DIS(V ), since all surviving concepts *agree* on the classification of X<sup>i</sup> , the label Y<sup>i</sup> would provide no information that would help with this goal, so we do not bother querying for this label: that is, such a Y<sup>i</sup> cannot help to estimate the *relative* performances er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g) of concepts f, g ∈ V , since regardless of Y<sup>i</sup> , we have **1**[f(Xi) ̸= Y<sup>i</sup> ] − **1**[g(Xi) ̸= Y<sup>i</sup> ] = 0. In contrast, the next X<sup>i</sup> ∈ DIS(V ) in the sequence is a random sample from PX (·|DIS(V )), so that for any f, g ∈ V , **1**[f(Xi) ̸= Y<sup>i</sup> ] − **1**[g(Xi) ̸= Y<sup>i</sup> ] is an unbiased estimate of the difference of error rates under the conditional distribution P(·|DIS(V ) × {0, 1}), which (again since f, g agree outside DIS(V )) is proportional to er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g). By reasoning about uniform concentration of these estimates, we can define an update rule for V in Step 4 that never removes the best-in-class concept h <sup>⋆</sup> while pruning sub-optimal concepts from V (where the resolution of this pruning improves as i grows).

In the *realizable case*, since we always have h ⋆ (Xi) = Y<sup>i</sup> , the updates to V in Step 4 can simply remove all concepts incorrect on a queried example (X<sup>i</sup> , Yi): that is, V ← {h ∈ V : h(Xi) = Yi} (called the *version space*), which always retains h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V . The algorithmic principle of disagreementbased queries, and corresponding reasoning about correctness and potential advantages, was already

<sup>8</sup>The theory easily generalizes to cases where the infimum infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) is not attained, in which case define h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> to have er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) sufficiently close to the infimum, e.g., as in [\(10\)](#page-36-1) below.

identified in the early work of [Mitchell](#page-14-7) [\(1979\)](#page-14-7) (for the membership queries model).[<sup>9</sup>](#page-24-0) The precise form expressed above (sequentially checking unlabeled examples for disagreements) was first explicitly studied by [Cohn, Atlas, and Ladner](#page-11-6) [\(1994\)](#page-11-6), and in their honor, this realizable-case technique is referred to as CAL in the literature. As for its theoretical analysis, the original works of [Mitchell](#page-14-7) [\(1979\)](#page-14-7); [Cohn, Atlas, and Ladner](#page-11-6) [\(1994\)](#page-11-6) include the observation that h ⋆ is retained in V , and [Cohn,](#page-11-6) [Atlas, and Ladner](#page-11-6) [\(1994\)](#page-11-6) additionally include some discussion of generalization. However, the formal analysis of the query complexity of this technique in the PAC framework only began with the later work of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford](#page-10-5) [\(2006\)](#page-10-5) (bounding the query complexity for some specific concept classes), and the general analysis of the technique (applicable to any concept class) began with the works of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b,](#page-13-0) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011\)](#page-13-13); [Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni](#page-12-17) [\(2007\)](#page-12-17).

The idea of disagreement-based active learning was first extended to the agnostic setting (β ≥ 0) by [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford](#page-10-5) [\(2006\)](#page-10-5), with an instantiation of the above outline they called the A<sup>2</sup> algorithm (for *Agnostic Active*). The main idea in A<sup>2</sup> is to instantiate the update to V in Step 4 using uniform concentration inequalities. In their original version, they specifically define UB(h) and LB(h) as high-probability uniform upper and lower bounds on er<sup>P</sup> (·|DIS(<sup>V</sup> )×{0,1})(h) based on the queries from DIS(V ) since the last update to V (where they only update V periodically in their algorithm, so that the queried examples since the last update are i.i.d. samples from P(·|DIS(V ) × {0, 1})). They then define the update as V ← {h ∈ V : LB(h) ≤ minh′∈<sup>V</sup> UB(h ′ )}. The idea is that they wish to remove a concept h from V if there is another concept h ′ ∈ V whose *upper bound* UB(h ′ ) on its error rate is smaller than the *lower bound* LB(h) on the error rate of h. In particular, since h, h′ agree on all x /∈ DIS(V ), we have

$$\text{er}_P(h) - \text{er}_P(h') \propto \text{er}_{P(\cdot|\text{DIS}(V) \times \{0,1\})}(h) - \text{er}_{P(\cdot|\text{DIS}(V) \times \{0,1\})}(h') \geq \text{LB}(h) - \text{UB}(h'),$$

and hence a concept h can be removed from V *only if* er<sup>P</sup> (h) − er<sup>P</sup> (h ′ ) > 0 for some h ′ ∈ V , meaning that h is verifiably suboptimal, guaranteeing that the algorithm always retains h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V . Conversely, by querying the examples in DIS(V ), we are improving the concentration inequalities UB(h), LB(h), so that suboptimal concepts are removed from V , which has two benefits: (1) we are converging to a set of relatively low-error concepts (important for the final error guarantee), and (2) by reducing V we are potentially also reducing DIS(V ), focusing the algorithm's queries to more-informative samples and decreasing the query complexity.

The original analysis of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford](#page-10-5) [\(2006\)](#page-10-5) included the above correctness guarantee (i.e., the algorithm maintains h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V ), along with a general guarantee that the A<sup>2</sup> algorithm returns an hˆ with er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) + ε, with a number of queries never significantly worse than that of passive learning. Also, as a sort of *proof of concept* illustrating the potential benefits of A<sup>2</sup> in a simple example, they also quantified the query complexity advantages in the special case of *threshold* classifiers (concepts **<sup>1</sup>**[a,∞) on X <sup>=</sup> <sup>R</sup>), showing a bound <sup>O</sup>˜ β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 for that class (matching the lower bound of [Kääriäinen,](#page-14-2) [2006\)](#page-14-2). They also studied the special case of learning homogeneous linear classifiers under a uniform distribution in an origin-centered ball in R d , focusing on the regime <sup>β</sup> <sup>≲</sup> ε/√ d, for which they showed the query complexity is O˜ d log ε log δ .

The first *general* analyses (i.e., applicable to *any* concept class) of the query complexity of active learning in the agnostic setting were given in the works of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b](#page-13-0)[,a\)](#page-13-11). In particular, [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) analyzed the A<sup>2</sup> disagreement-based active learning algorithm, providing a general query complexity bound expressed in terms of a new complexity measure therein termed the *disagreement coefficient*. Specifically, for r > 0, denoting by B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r) = {h ∈ C : PX (x : h(x) ̸= h ⋆ (x)) ≤ r} the h ⋆ -centered r-ball (under L1(PX )), the disagreement coefficient is defined as

$$\theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon) := \sup_{r > \beta + \varepsilon} \frac{P_X(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_X}(h^*, r)))}{r} \vee 1.$$

The intuitive interpretation of the relevance of this quantity is that, as the algorithm progresses, the set V of surviving concepts will become closer and closer to h ⋆ (up until a distance O(β + ε)), so that the probability of querying decreases as PX (DIS(V )) ≤ PX (DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r))) for an appropriate r decreasing as the number of queries grows.

[Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) proves that, for any <sup>C</sup> and P, for β = er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ), the A<sup>2</sup> algorithm succeeds after a number of queries O˜ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + 1. This matches the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1) of [Kääriäinen](#page-14-2)

<sup>9</sup>[Mitchell](#page-14-7) [\(1979\)](#page-14-7) also discusses some reasonable extensions of version spaces to the non-realizable case.

[\(2006\)](#page-14-2); [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford](#page-11-1) [\(2009\)](#page-11-1) up to logs whenever θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = O˜(1). In particular, [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) bounds θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) for a number of scenarios (C, P), including showing that this general query complexity upper bound recovers the examples of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and](#page-10-5) [Langford](#page-10-5) [\(2006\)](#page-10-5): θ<sup>P</sup> (β +ε) ≤ 2 for threshold classifiers, and θ<sup>P</sup> (β +ε) = O( √ d) for homogeneous linear classifiers under a uniform distribution on an origin-centered sphere (thus also removing the constraints on β, ε from the result of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford,](#page-10-5) [2006\)](#page-10-5). However, [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) also found θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) can sometimes be as large as <sup>1</sup> β+ε , particularly for the "searching in the dark" scenarios discussed above, in which case the query complexity bound is no smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning.

Subsequently, [Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni](#page-12-17) [\(2007\)](#page-12-17) refined the dependence on θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) in this bound (analyzing a different disagreement-based algorithm), replacing θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) <sup>2</sup> with θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε). Their technique also identifies a principle enabling more-practical implementation of disagreementbased active learning, expressing the algorithm as a *reduction to empirical risk minimization* (ERM). In general (even with A<sup>2</sup> ), we can always maintain the set V *implicitly* (i.e., without storing this large object V explicitly), by simply maintaining the set of *constraints* that define it, which then enable us to perform the various operations (e.g., checking whether X<sup>i</sup> ∈ DIS(V ), or computing minh′∈<sup>V</sup> UB(h)) via constraint satisfaction or constrained optimization problems. The algorithm of [Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni](#page-12-17) [\(2007\)](#page-12-17) takes this a step further, expressing such operations as (effectively) unconstrained optimization problems, or in other words, calls to an ERM subroutine (i.e., an algorithm which returns a concept in C minimizing the number of mistakes on any given labeled data set). Specifically, they store two labeled data sets Q<sup>i</sup> , L<sup>i</sup> , where Q<sup>i</sup> are the queried examples so far (up to round i) and L<sup>i</sup> are the unqueried examples so far (with *inferred* labels). On round i, they consider the concepts h 1 , h<sup>0</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> of minimal ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h) subject to ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h) = 0 and h 1 (Xi) = 1, h 0 (Xi) = 0, if they exist (noting that such concepts can each be obtained by a single call to an ERM oracle with appropriately high weight, or repetition, of the Li−<sup>1</sup> and (X<sup>i</sup> , y) examples). If ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h ) and ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h 0 ) are of similar sizes, they query for Y<sup>i</sup> and add it to Qi−<sup>1</sup> to get Q<sup>i</sup> (letting L<sup>i</sup> = Li−1), and otherwise they take an *inferred* label yˆ<sup>i</sup> = argmin<sup>y</sup> ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h y ) and add (X<sup>i</sup> , yˆi) to Li−<sup>1</sup> to get L<sup>i</sup> (letting Q<sup>i</sup> = Qi−1). Note that this is equivalent to maintaining a set V of all concepts h ∈ <sup>C</sup> having ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h) = 0 and ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h) of similar size to minh′ ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h ′ ) among all h ′ ∈ <sup>C</sup> with ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h ′ ) = 0, and querying X<sup>i</sup> iff X<sup>i</sup> ∈ DIS(V ). Thus, this algorithm can also be viewed as a disagreement-based active learner (and this connection is made explicit in the analysis of [Dasgupta,](#page-12-17) [Hsu, and Monteleoni,](#page-12-17) [2007\)](#page-12-17). (Notably, subsequent works of [Beygelzimer, Hsu, Langford, and Zhang,](#page-11-9) [2010;](#page-11-9) [Hsu,](#page-14-14) [2010](#page-14-14) even further simplified this technique by dropping the Li−<sup>1</sup> constraints, obtaining similar query complexity bounds).

The specific quantification of the "similar sizes" criterion for the difference of empirical error rates comes from uniform Bernstein-style concentration inequalities (related to the uniform Bernstein inequality stated in Lemma [7](#page-35-0) of Appendix [D](#page-34-0) below). In particular, letting Si−<sup>1</sup> = {(X<sup>j</sup> , Y<sup>j</sup> ) : j < i}, among all pairs of concepts h, h′ ∈ <sup>C</sup> with ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h) = ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h ′ ) = 0, since they *agree* on examples in Li−1, we have

$$\hat{\text{er}}_{Q_{i-1}}(h) - \hat{\text{er}}_{Q_{i-1}}(h') \propto \hat{\text{er}}_{Q_{i-1} \cup L_{i-1}}(h) - \hat{\text{er}}_{Q_{i-1} \cup L_{i-1}}(h') = \hat{\text{er}}_{S_{i-1}}(h) - \hat{\text{er}}_{S_{i-1}}(h'),$$

so that we can make use of concentration inequalities for estimating differences of error rates from the i.i.d. data set Si−<sup>1</sup> (e.g., as in Lemma [7\)](#page-35-0). Reasoning inductively, if ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h ⋆ ) = 0, such a concentration inequality guarantees that among all h ∈ <sup>C</sup> with ˆer<sup>L</sup>i−<sup>1</sup> (h) = 0, ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h ⋆ ) can never be too much larger than ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h), so that if the algorithm does not query X<sup>i</sup> (meaning ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h <sup>1</sup>−yˆ<sup>i</sup> ) is much larger than ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h <sup>y</sup>ˆ<sup>i</sup> )), the corresponding label yˆ<sup>i</sup> must be h ⋆ (Xi), so that adding (X<sup>i</sup> , yˆi) to L<sup>i</sup> retains that ˆer<sup>L</sup><sup>i</sup> (h ⋆ ) = 0. Conversely, if ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h ) and ˆerQi−<sup>1</sup> (h ) are of similar sizes, then the algorithm has effectively verified that there exist concepts h, h′ ∈ <sup>C</sup> with er<sup>P</sup> (h) and er<sup>P</sup> (h ′ ) rather close to er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) which nevertheless disagree on X<sup>i</sup> (h(Xi) ̸= h ′ (Xi)), and querying for Y<sup>i</sup> and adding (X<sup>i</sup> , Yi) to Q<sup>i</sup> then strengthens the concentration of the ˆerQ<sup>i</sup> estimates, to help further distinguish among such small-error concepts in subsequent rounds.

[Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni](#page-12-17) [\(2007\)](#page-12-17) analyzed the query complexity of this algorithm, showing that it guarantees er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ )+ε after a number of queries O˜ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε)d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1. Compared to the original analysis of [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0), this improves the bound in its dependence on θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε), reducing from quadratic θ<sup>P</sup> (β +ε) 2 to linear θ<sup>P</sup> (β +ε). Again, the conclusion is that the algorithm's query complexity matches the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1) of [Kääriäinen](#page-14-2) [\(2006\)](#page-14-2); [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and](#page-11-1)

[Langford](#page-11-1) [\(2009\)](#page-11-1) up to logs whenever θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = O˜(1); however, again, in scenarios (C, P) with θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = Ω 1 β+ε , the bound offers no improvements over the sample complexity of passive learning.

The above techniques, and corresponding analysis in terms of the disagreement coefficient, seeded a vast literature, with many variations on the technique, analysis, and complexity measures, and many examples of scenarios (C, P) for which θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) can be favorably bounded. This branch of the literature is collectively referred to as *disagreement-based active learning* (see e.g., the works of [Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011,](#page-13-13) [2012,](#page-13-14) [2014,](#page-13-1) [2016b;](#page-13-5) [Balcan, Hanneke, and Vaughan,](#page-10-13) [2010;](#page-10-13) [Hsu,](#page-14-14) [2010;](#page-14-14) [El-Yaniv and Wiener,](#page-12-18) [2012;](#page-12-18) [Friedman,](#page-12-19) [2009;](#page-12-19) [Mahalanabis,](#page-14-15) [2011;](#page-14-15) [Koltchinskii,](#page-14-5) [2010;](#page-14-5) [Wang,](#page-16-10) [2011;](#page-16-10) [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009;](#page-11-1) [Beygelzimer, Hsu, Langford, and Zhang,](#page-11-9) [2010;](#page-11-9) [Raginsky and Rakhlin,](#page-15-17) [2011;](#page-15-17) [Ailon, Begleiter, and Ezra,](#page-10-16) [2014;](#page-10-16) [Huang, Agarwal, Hsu, Langford, and](#page-14-16) [Schapire,](#page-14-16) [2015;](#page-14-16) [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015;](#page-16-9) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-17) [2010,](#page-13-17) [2015,](#page-13-2) [2019;](#page-13-18) [Yan,](#page-16-11) [Chaudhuri, and Javidi,](#page-16-11) [2018,](#page-16-11) [2019;](#page-16-12) [Gelbhart and El-Yaniv,](#page-12-20) [2019;](#page-12-20) [Cortes, DeSalvo, Gentile, Mohri,](#page-11-10) [and Zhang,](#page-11-10) [2019a;](#page-11-10) [Cortes, DeSalvo, Gentile, Mohri, and Zhang,](#page-11-11) [2019b,](#page-11-11)[c,](#page-11-12) [2020;](#page-11-13) [DeSalvo, Gentile,](#page-12-21) [and Thune,](#page-12-21) [2021;](#page-12-21) [Shayestehmanesh,](#page-15-18) [2020;](#page-15-18) [Puchkin and Zhivotovskiy,](#page-15-5) [2022\)](#page-15-5). A detailed summary of this line of work is presented in the survey of [Hanneke](#page-13-1) [\(2014\)](#page-13-1).

In the context of distribution-free analysis, [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) showed that sup<sup>P</sup> θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = s ∧ 1 β+ε , where s is the *star number* of C (Definition [2\)](#page-3-1), and where the sup is over realizable distributions P (so that, in particular, they satisfy the condition er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) ≤ β). Thus, in terms of their implications for the distribution-free query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C), these P-dependent analyses of disagreement-based active learning simplify to a bound of the form QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) = O˜ <sup>s</sup> <sup>∧</sup> β+ε d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1. In particular, such bounds are capable of providing improvements in distribution-free query complexity over the sample complexity of passive learning Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) if and only if s < ∞ (which, as discussed above, is a rather strong restriction). This contrasts with Theorems [1,](#page-3-0) [3,](#page-4-0) which provide improvements for *all* concept classes C, regardless of whether s is finite or infinite. (The role of s in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) is merely in refining the *lower-order* term in the special case that s < ∞). In this sense, Theorems [1,](#page-3-0) [3](#page-4-0) are *most* interesting for classes C with s = ∞, since no previous techniques provide improvements over passive learning for such classes.

We note that, while the AVID Agnostic algorithm (Figure [1\)](#page-6-0) itself should *not* be regarded as a disagreement-based active learner (as its primary advantage over passive learning is *not* based on the restriction of queries to DIS(V )), elements of disagreement-based learning have been incorporated into it for the purpose of the refined *lower-order* term in the upper bound in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0) Specifically, the choice to query examples in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> in Step [2](#page-6-2) (and similarly Step [9\)](#page-6-3) restricts to queries in Dk−<sup>1</sup> = DIS(Vk−1). This restriction is directly responsible for the lower-order term in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) being of the form O˜ s ∧ 1 ε d rather than O˜ d ε as in Theorem [1.](#page-3-0) On the other hand, for the purpose of the lead term, this incorporation of disagreement-based queries is unnecessary, and indeed Theorem [1](#page-3-0) remains valid *without* this aspect of the algorithm: that is, in Steps [2](#page-6-2) and [9,](#page-6-3) if we simply query all of S 1 k \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , the algorithm still achieves the query complexity bound stated in Theorem [1](#page-3-0) with its lower-order term O˜ d ε .

The argument leading to the refined lower-order term in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) makes use of reasoning directly rooted in the analysis of disagreement-based methods via the disagreement coefficient (Lemma [22\)](#page-46-0), and indeed we present P-dependent refinements of this lower-order term directly expressed in terms of θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) in Appendix [F.2.](#page-53-0) In particular, we show (Corollary [28\)](#page-55-0) the lower-order term O˜ s ∧ ε d can be replaced by O˜ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2d , yielding an overall P-dependent query complexity bound O β 2 ε 2 d + log 1 δ + O˜ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2d . we further argue, in Appendix [F.1,](#page-52-0) that it is *not* possible (by any algorithm) to reduce this lower-order term to O˜(θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε)d) or even O˜(θ<sup>P</sup> (0)d), though we do show that other intermediate forms of the term are achievable, such as O˜ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε)d β+ε ε .

# A.2.2 Subregion-based (Margin-based) Active Learning

Shortly after the work of [Balcan, Beygelzimer, and Langford](#page-10-5) [\(2006\)](#page-10-5), which included an analysis of homogeneous linear classifiers under a uniform distribution, [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang](#page-10-12) [\(2007\)](#page-10-12) proposed a refinement of disagreement-based active learning specific to linear classifiers. Rather than querying every example in the region of disagreement DIS(V ), they identified a *subregion*

R ⊆ DIS(V ) which suffices for the purpose of estimating differences of error rates er<sup>P</sup> (f) − er<sup>P</sup> (g) among f, g ∈ V . The key idea is to choose R so that any f, g ∈ V have PX ({f ̸= g} \ R) small, so that R captures *most* of the disagreements between concepts f, g ∈ V that are far apart. In their case, since they were specifically focusing on homogeneous linear classifiers (i.e., concepts hw(x) = **1**[⟨w, x⟩ ≥ 0] on X = <sup>R</sup> d ) under PX uniform in an origin-centered ball, they could describe this region R as a *slab* around the boundary of a current hypothesis hwˆ: that is, R = x ∈ R d : |⟨w, x ˆ ⟩| ≤ b for an appropriate width b (which decreases over time as the algorithm progresses). In other words, the algorithm queries examples X<sup>i</sup> with *low margin* under the current hypothesis wˆ. As such, this technique is referred to as *margin-based active learning*. They analyzed this technique for the realizable case and under a specialized noise condition (Tsybakov noise), and found it provides advantages over disagreement-based learning: in the realizable case, improving the query complexity from d 3/2 · polylog 1 εδ to d · polylog 1 εδ (matching the query complexities achieved by earlier works [Freund, Seung, Shamir, and Tishby,](#page-12-14) [1997;](#page-12-14) [Dasgupta, Kalai, and Monteleoni,](#page-12-15) [2005;](#page-12-15) [Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005\)](#page-12-0), while allowing for some robustness to non-realizable distributions P (albeit not fully agnostic). The technique was later extended in various ways, including studying adaptivity to certain noise parameters [\(Wang and Singh,](#page-16-8) [2016\)](#page-16-8) and generalizing beyond the uniform distribution, to general isotropic log-concave or s-concave distributions [\(Balcan and Long,](#page-10-2) [2013;](#page-10-2) [Balcan and](#page-10-17) [Zhang,](#page-10-17) [2017\)](#page-10-17).

This idea was extended to general concept classes C and distributions P, including the agnostic setting, in the work of [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1). Again the idea is to identify a region R ⊆ DIS(V ) for which concepts f, g ∈ V have only small disagreements outside R: PX ({f ̸= g} \ R) ≤ η, for a small η. Rather than an explicit region R (as in margin-based active learning), they simply choose a subset of the unlabeled examples via a linear program, which they show (in the analysis) can be related to an optimal choice of such a region. We discuss this technique in detail in Appendix [F.3.](#page-60-0)

The implication of this refinement of disagreement-based learning is a P-dependent query complexity bound, stated in terms of a subregion-based refinement of the disagreement coefficient, defined as follows (adopting some simplifications from [Hanneke,](#page-13-5) [2016b\)](#page-13-5). As above, define the r-ball B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r) = {h ∈ C : PX (x : h(x) ̸= h ⋆ (x)) ≤ r} for r > 0. Also, for η ≥ 0, define

$$\Phi_{P_X}(\mathbf{B}_{P_X}(h^*, r), \eta) := \inf \left\{ P_X(R) : \sup_{h \in \mathbf{B}_{P_X}(h^*, r)} P_X(\{h \neq f\} \setminus R) \leq \eta, R \subseteq \mathcal{X}, f: \mathcal{X} \rightarrow \{0, 1\} \right\},$$

where R and f are restricted to be measurable. Finally, for ε ≥ 0, define the subregion disagreement coefficient (Definition [31\)](#page-61-0) as

$$\varphi_P(\varepsilon, \eta) := \sup_{r > \eta + \varepsilon} \frac{\Phi_{P_X}(\mathbf{B}_{P_X}(h^*, r), (r - \eta)/c)}{r} \vee 1,$$

for an appropriate universal constant c > 1. The technique of [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) provides a P-dependent query complexity bound of the form O˜ φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 2β)d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1. In particular, it follows from the definitions that φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 2β) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (2β + ε) (see Appendix [F.3\)](#page-60-0), and [Zhang and](#page-16-1) [Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) discuss some examples where the gap is large. Thus, this represents a refinement of the query complexity bounds for disagreement-based active learning discussed above.

As a primary example where φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 2β) ≪ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε), consider again the scenario of homogeneous linear classifiers on R <sup>d</sup> under PX an isotropic log-concave distribution (as considered in the marginbased active learning works of [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang,](#page-10-12) [2007;](#page-10-12) [Balcan and Long,](#page-10-2) [2013](#page-10-2) discussed above). In this scenario, [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) show that φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 2β) = O log β ε (based on concentration arguments from [Balcan and Long,](#page-10-2) [2013\)](#page-10-2). Thus, in this scenario, the query complexity bound of [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) is O˜ d β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1. In contrast, [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0) showed <sup>θ</sup><sup>P</sup> (<sup>β</sup> <sup>+</sup> <sup>ε</sup>) = Ω√ d ∧ β+ε for PX the uniform distribution on an origin-centered sphere (a special case of isotropic log-concave), so that the query complexity bounds for disagreement-based active learning are roughly d 3/2 β ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 , hence are suboptimal by a factor √ d.

That said, in the context of distribution-free analysis, it is unclear whether there are advantages from this subregion technique. Specifically, [Hanneke](#page-13-5) [\(2016b\)](#page-13-5) showed that sup<sup>P</sup> φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 0) = s ∧ 1 ε (where the sup is restricted to realizable distributions P), which matches the worst-case value of θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) (established by [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). In [\(54\)](#page-62-0) of Appendix [F.3,](#page-60-0) we further extend this to φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, η) (using the fact that φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, η) ≥ φ<sup>P</sup> (η + ε, 0)), establishing that (for ε, η ≥ 0 with η + ε ≤ 1)

$$\sup_P \varphi_P(\varepsilon, \eta) = \mathfrak{s} \wedge \frac{1}{\eta + \varepsilon},$$

where again the sup is restricted realizable distributions P. Thus, the implication of the Pdependent query complexity bound of [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) for bounding the distributionfree query complexity QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) is merely to recover the same query complexity bound O˜ <sup>s</sup> <sup>∧</sup> β+ε d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 already known to hold for disagreement-based active learning. In particular, this means that the above query complexity bound of [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) is capable of providing improvements in the distribution-free query complexity of active learning, compared to the sample complexity Mp(ε, δ; β, <sup>C</sup>) of passive learning, *if and only if* s < ∞ (again, a rather strong restriction). Again, this contrasts with Theorems [1,](#page-3-0) [3,](#page-4-0) which provide improvements for *all* concept classes C, regardless of s, with s merely influencing refinements in the lower-order term in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0)

In Appendix [F.3,](#page-60-0) we give a refinement of the AVID Agnostic algorithm, which adopts this subregion technique (in combination with the AVID principle). We show this Subregion-AVID Agnostic algorithm achieves a P-dependent refinement of the lower-order term compared to the original AVID Agnostic algorithm. For instance, one implication of this refinement is replacing the term O˜ s ∧ ε d in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) with O˜ φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 5β) 2d , yielding a P-dependent query complexity bound O β ε d + log δ + O˜ φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 5β) 2d . It follows from an example in Appendix [F.1](#page-52-0) that the above quadratic dependence φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 5β) 2 cannot be reduced to φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 5β) (or even φ<sup>P</sup> (0, 0)) without introducing additional factors, though we also establish intermediate forms of the term, such as O˜ φ<sup>P</sup> (ε, 5β)d β+ε ε .

# A.2.3 Other Topics and Techniques in the Theory of Active Learning

In addition to disagreement-based active learning and its subregion-based refinement, and query complexity bounds for the agnostic setting, the active learning literature also contains numerous other techniques and topics. Though these ideas are not directly used in the present work, we briefly survey them here for completeness, and in some cases, to discuss connections to the results and techniques of the present work. For brevity, we omit most of the formal definitions, algorithms, and precise statements of the results, rather summarizing the essential ideas, and referring the interested reader to the original works for the precise results and details (some of which are also surveyed by [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014](#page-13-1) in detail).

The Splitting Index: The earliest general theory of active learning, providing query complexity bounds applicable to any concept class and realizable-case distribution, was proposed by [Dasgupta](#page-12-0) [\(2005\)](#page-12-0). That work proposes a (C, P)-dependent complexity measure called the *splitting index* ρ ∈ [0, 1], based on the property that, for any γ > ε and any finite set of pairs (f, g) ∈ C <sup>2</sup> with PX (f ̸= g) > γ, there will likely be an unlabeled example X<sup>i</sup> for which, regardless of whether Y<sup>i</sup> is 0 or 1, we are guaranteed that at least a ρ fraction of the pairs (f, g) will have at least one function incorrect on (X<sup>i</sup> , Yi) (see [Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005](#page-12-0) for the precise definition). The idea is that ρ measures a notion of *progress*, from querying such an X<sup>i</sup> , toward reducing the *diameter* of the version space below γ. The pairs (f, g) in the version space having PX (f ̸= g) > γ are the *obstructions* to reducing the diameter of the version space. The above definition guarantees there will be an example X<sup>i</sup> we can query such that, regardless of which label Y<sup>i</sup> is returned, discarding all inconsistent concepts from the version space results in a reduction in the set of such obstructing pairs, leaving at most a 1 − ρ fraction of them. If we start with an α-cover V of the concept class C of size roughly α −d (with α < ε small enough to guarantee all queried labels agree with some h ∈ V ), we would require at most O d ρ log α such queries to eliminate *all* obstructing pairs, and thus reduce the diameter of the version space below γ. We can then decrease γ by a constant factor and repeat, until the diameter is below ε, at which time we can return any surviving concept, yielding a query complexity roughly O d ρ log 1 α log 1 ε .

The splitting index also provides a lower bound Ω 1 ρ on the realizable-case query complexity (where, in this case, ρ can be PX -dependent, but should be h ⋆ -independent; see [Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Balcan](#page-10-15)

[and Hanneke,](#page-10-15) [2012;](#page-10-15) [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014](#page-13-1) for precise statements). This is particularly interesting due to being PX -dependent, and yet still providing near-matching upper and lower bounds (in contrast, other quantities such as the disagreement coefficient and subregion-based refinement only provide upper bounds, and provably cannot yield general PX -dependent lower bounds). Since the above results reveal the PX -dependent query complexity can be well-captured by the splitting index, whereas [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) have shown the optimal *distribution-free* realizable-case query complexity is characterized by the *star number* (Definition [2\)](#page-3-1), it is also natural to study the relation between these quantities. Toward this end, [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) have in fact shown that these quantities are *equivalent* in the context of distribution-free analysis: namely, sup<sup>P</sup> j ρ k = min s, ε .

The splitting index analysis also provides another interesting feature, which is perhaps missing from other works on active learning in the realizable case: it quantifies a *trade-off* between the query complexity and the number of *unlabeled* examples. This is reflected in the above (imprecise) definition in the part that requires that such a ρ-splitting example X<sup>i</sup> is likely to exist in the unlabeled data (this is made precise in [Dasgupta,](#page-12-0) [2005](#page-12-0) by another parameter τ reflecting the probability of obtaining such an example). Using a larger number of unlabeled examples can increase the likelihood of including an example X<sup>i</sup> that eliminates a larger fraction of pairs, so that the splitting index ρ can grow larger (hence decreasing the query complexity) for larger unlabeled sample sizes. This improvement from having larger unlabeled sample sizes is not reflected in other complexity measures proposed in the literature, and in such cases the splitting-based query complexity bounds can be substantially smaller than those based on these other complexities, such as the disagreement coefficient (see [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014](#page-13-1) for explicit comparisons). It is worth noting that such trade-offs are not known to be possible in the agnostic setting.

While the original work of [Dasgupta](#page-12-0) [\(2005\)](#page-12-0) was developed for the realizable case, subsequent works have explored extensions to non-realizable settings under restrictions on the types of non-realizability. Specifically, [Balcan and Hanneke](#page-10-15) [\(2012\)](#page-10-15); [Hanneke](#page-13-1) [\(2014\)](#page-13-1); [Tosh and Hsu](#page-15-19) [\(2020\)](#page-15-19) have extended the theory to allow for so-called *Massart noise*, wherein it is assumed P(Y = h ⋆ (X)|X) − 2 is everywhere positive and bounded away from 0. The extension of the splitting technique to that setting merely requires that we only remove a concept from consideration upon having sufficiently many errors on queried examples. The resulting query complexity bounds are then similar to the above.

To date, the splitting technique has not been extended to the *agnostic* setting in any meaningful way (e.g., to obtain query complexity bounds which could not also be obtained by, say, running disagreement-based active learning with an (ε/2)-cover of the concept class). The agnostic setting presents significant challenges for this technique, due to the ρ-splitting examples X<sup>i</sup> being possibly in ER(h ⋆ ) for the best-in-class concept h ⋆ , meaning such examples cannot be trusted as the sole source of information for pruning suboptimal concepts; see the scenario in Appendix [F.1](#page-52-0) (which is constructed therein for a different reason, but also illustrates this issue).

We may remark that the AVID principle, developed in the present work and employed in Aavid, has certain aspects that are intriguingly reminiscent of the splitting technique of [Dasgupta](#page-12-0) [\(2005\)](#page-12-0). As in splitting, Aavid aims to reduce the *diameter* of a set V of surviving concepts. Toward this end, again as in splitting, it identifies *obstructing pairs*: f, g ∈ V with PX (f ̸= g) > εk, where ε<sup>k</sup> is the desired diameter guarantee at that stage. However, the main difference is in how such obstructions are addressed in the algorithm. While the splitting technique would attempt to resolve this obstruction by *querying* to eliminate at least one of f, g for many such obstructing pairs, Aavid instead simply *removes* (isolates) the region {f ̸= g} from the space X (adding it to the ∆ region), and estimates error rates separately in ∆ and X \ ∆. Thus, in this aspect, the algorithmic principle underlying <sup>A</sup>avid is considerably different from splitting. Nevertheless, this common focus on addressing pairs (f, g) obstructing the reduction of the diameter presents an intriguing connection, which might potentially warrant further exploration.

Empirical Teaching Dimension: Another approach to agnostic active learning, based on principles seemingly distinct from disagreement-based methods, was proposed in the work of [Hanneke](#page-13-11) [\(2007a\)](#page-13-11). The technique there is inspired by early work on Exact learning with membership queries in the realizable case by [Hegedüs](#page-14-8) [\(1995\)](#page-14-8); [Hellerstein, Pillaipakkamnatt, Raghavan, and Wilkins](#page-14-9) [\(1996\)](#page-14-9), which found interesting connections between active learning and the complexity of *machine teaching* [\(Goldman and Kearns,](#page-13-19) [1995\)](#page-13-19). [Hanneke](#page-13-11) [\(2007a\)](#page-13-11) extends those ideas to the PAC setting, starting with an upper bound for the realizable case, based on a PX -dependent complexity measure τ (ε) therein

termed the *extended teaching dimension growth function*: for an i.i.d.-PX data set S of size <sup>1</sup> ε , τ (ε) (roughly) represents the minimal size of a subsample which induces the same version space (for any fixed target concept h ⋆ ). The main technique is to find sets of τ (ε) unlabeled examples for which the labels are guaranteed to significantly reduce the *number* of concepts in (a finite cover of) the version space. The work also presents a realizable-case lower bound based on a modified variant of this complexity measure.

[Hanneke](#page-13-11) [\(2007a\)](#page-13-11) further extends the upper bound to the non-realizable case, establishing an upper bound O˜ τ (β + ε)d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + 1. In particular, this matches the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1) of [Kääriäinen](#page-14-2) [\(2006\)](#page-14-2); [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford](#page-11-1) [\(2009\)](#page-11-1) up to logs when τ (β + ε) = O˜(1). [Hanneke](#page-13-11) [\(2007a\)](#page-13-11) provides examples where τ (β + ε) is bounded, including the class of thresholds (concepts **1**[a,∞) on X = <sup>R</sup>) and axis-aligned rectangles (of at least some volume) under restrictions on PX ; however, as with the previously discussed complexity measures, τ (β + ε) can be as large as <sup>1</sup> β+ε for the "searching in the dark" type scenarios discussed above, in which case the above query complexity bound is no smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning. Indeed, as with the complexity measures discussed above, results of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) imply that, taking the worst case over distributions, τ (ε) becomes equivalent to the *star number* (Definition [2\)](#page-3-1): sup<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> τ (ε) ≈ min s, 1 ε .

Variants of this τ (ε) complexity measure were later further analyzed (for several example scenarios, and more-generally, in relation to the disagreement coefficient), under the name *version space compression set size*, and (interestingly) have also been found useful for studying *disagreement-based* active learning, by [El-Yaniv and Wiener](#page-12-3) [\(2010,](#page-12-3) [2012\)](#page-12-18); [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv](#page-16-9) [\(2015\)](#page-16-9); [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2); [Hanneke](#page-13-5) [\(2016b\)](#page-13-5); [Hanneke and Kontorovich](#page-13-20) [\(2021\)](#page-13-20).

Restricted Noise Models: Besides the study of first-order agnostic query complexity guarantees QC<sup>a</sup> (ε, δ; β, C) (the subject of the present work), the theory of active learning additionally includes many works on query complexity guarantees holding under other conditions or parameterizations, or in other words, under various *noise models*. Here we briefly survey some of this literature.

The most-similar noise model to that studied in the present work is the *benign noise* setting [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b\)](#page-13-3), which differs from the agnostic setting only in that it makes the additional assumption that infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) = inf<sup>h</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h), where the infimum on the right hand side is over *all* measurable functions h : X → {0, 1} (not necessarily in C). In other words, the benign noise setting assumes the best-in-class error β = infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) is also the *Bayes risk* of P: i.e., the error rate of the function x 7→ **1** -P(Y = 1|X = x) ≥ 2 . Since the distributions used to establish the lower bound [\(4\)](#page-19-1) for passive learning satisfy the benign noise condition, this still serves as a suitable comparison point for the query complexity of active learning. Similarly, the distributions used to establish the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1) for active learning also satisfy benign noise, and therefore the lower bound [\(6\)](#page-21-1) also holds in the benign noise setting. Notably, in the special case of benign noise, [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) have shown a result analogous to the present work: the optimal first-order query complexity of active learning is O˜ d β ε <sup>2</sup> + min s, d ε . In particular, comparing to [\(4\)](#page-19-1), this means, under the benign noise assumption, the query complexity of active learning is always better than the sample complexity of passive learning. That work posed the question of whether such improvements are also attainable in the more-challenging *agnostic* setting, a question which the present work answers positively. Notably, the above result for benign noise is even slightly sharper in the lower-order term, compared to our Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (which has ds rather than s); I conjecture this ds can also be reduced to s in the agnostic setting. There are interesting connections or analogies between the algorithm used by [Hanneke and](#page-13-2) [Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) and the AVID principle developed in the present work, and we discuss these connections in Appendix [A.3](#page-30-0) below. However, one noteworthy point is that Aavid requires vastly fewer *unlabeled* examples to obtain the query complexity guarantee, compared to the method of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2), so that the present work also offers some benefits over the known techniques for the benign noise setting as well.

# A.3 Background of the AVID Principle

Having surveyed much of the related work on agnostic active learning above, we conclude our discussion of related work by discussing previous works in the learning theory literature containing ideas related to our main technique (the AVID principle).

Arguably the main innovation involved in this work is the decomposition of the space X into regions X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , and augmenting the predictor hˆ <sup>k</sup> to be a (shallow) *decision list* of concepts from C. One key inspiration for the main idea underlying the technique is rooted in the works of [Bousquet](#page-11-2) [and Zhivotovskiy](#page-11-2) [\(2021\)](#page-11-2); [Puchkin and Zhivotovskiy](#page-15-5) [\(2022\)](#page-15-5) on prediction with an *abstention* option (evaluated with the *Chow loss*). Interestingly, this continues a long precedent of finding useful connections and cross-inspirations between active learning and prediction with abstentions [\(Mitchell,](#page-14-7) [1979;](#page-14-7) [El-Yaniv and Wiener,](#page-12-3) [2010,](#page-12-3) [2012;](#page-12-18) [Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014,](#page-16-1) e.g.,). Specifically, [Bousquet](#page-11-2) [and Zhivotovskiy](#page-11-2) [\(2021\)](#page-11-2); [Puchkin and Zhivotovskiy](#page-15-5) [\(2022\)](#page-15-5) consider methods exhibiting a kind of *transition time*, in which they determine that, for some f, g ∈ C, *abstaining* in a the pairwise disagreement region {x : f(x) ̸= g(x)}, and predicting with f in its complement, comes out to have smaller Chow loss than the overall loss of the best h ∈ C. Some reasoning very much analogous to this (and directly inspired by it) can be found in one of the base cases of the arguments in the present paper (namely, concerning the "early stopping" case in the algorithm), in which we find that in the case of early stopping (Step [4\)](#page-6-5), we can find f, g ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> and h1, h<sup>2</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup>, such that predicting with h<sup>1</sup> in {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (rather than abstaining), with f in {f = g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , and with h<sup>2</sup> in ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , produces a *smaller* overall error rate in compared to the best concept h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup>. Of course, the algorithm and analysis here contain many additional pieces on top of this, but it is interesting that this connection to learning with abstentions still remains present at the core (though it is noteworthy that this connection is qualitatively different from the usual one, in that here we are not replacing abstentions with queries, but rather that a part of the analysis inspires part of our analysis). We remark that this analysis of learning with abstentions by [Bousquet and Zhivotovskiy](#page-11-2) [\(2021\)](#page-11-2); [Puchkin and Zhivotovskiy](#page-15-5) [\(2022\)](#page-15-5) was also inspirational for an active learning method in the work of [Zhu and Nowak](#page-16-13) [\(2022\)](#page-16-13) (though the aim in that work is different from the present work, and the setting is generally not comparable to ours).

At a high level, we can view the technique as also analogous to an idea of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) developed for the *benign noise* model: namely, the restriction of the agnostic setting to the case the *Bayes classifier* h ⋆ Bayes(x) 7→ **1**[P(Y = 1|X = x) ≥ 1/2] is in the concept class <sup>C</sup>. [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) prove a query complexity bound for this special case which matches Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (and indeed, refines the lower-order term's sd dependence to simply s). In that context, since the h ⋆ Bayes ∈ <sup>C</sup>, the only source of non-realizability is in the *noisiness* of the conditional label distribution Y |X. Thus, if an active learner could *repeatedly query* a given X<sup>t</sup> to receive *multiple* conditionally independent samples of Y<sup>t</sup> given Xt, it could use the majority vote of these samples to effectively *de-noise* the label of Xt, thereby identifying h ⋆ Bayes(Xt). This strategy only fails if P(Y = 1|X = Xt) is very close to <sup>1</sup> 2 , in which case this de-noising would require too many queries to be worthwhile, particularly since such noisy examples have very little effect on the *excess* error rate er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) − er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ Bayes). As such, if the active learner cannot identify the optimal label within some number of queries, it should *abandon* the example X<sup>t</sup> and move on. Of course, in the model of active learning studied in this work, and in the work of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2), an active learner cannot actually obtain multiple conditionally independent copies of the label Yt. However, by appropriate discretization of the space X based on the structure of the concept class C, [Hanneke](#page-13-2) [and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) are able to *approximate* this idealized behavior. The resulting algorithm effectively *adapts* to the noisiness of the labels of examples X<sup>t</sup> within the equivalence classes induced by this discretization, allocating more queries to the noisier (high-label-variance) regions (and abandoning the regions it finds to be too noisy). In that sense, the high-level idea behind the AVID principle is similar in nature. The goal is to isolate the regions where learning is more challenging, due to higher variance in error difference estimation, and allocate disproportionately more queries to these regions. Of course, in the agnostic case, this is made much more challenging, since the source of non-realizabilityy is not merely label noise, but also model misspecification (i.e., h ⋆ Bayes ∈/ <sup>C</sup>) so that de-noising the examples may sometimes have little benefit (e.g., it is even possible to have β > 0 while P(Y = 1|X) ∈ {0, 1}). As such, the AVID principle necessarily makes greater use of the structure of the concept class to isolate such regions of high variance in error difference estimation.

It is worth mentioning that other works on active learning have also considered decomposing the space X into subregions and learning separately in each region (e.g., [Cortes, DeSalvo, Gentile, Mohri,](#page-11-10) [and Zhang,](#page-11-10) [2019a;](#page-11-10) [Cortes, DeSalvo, Gentile, Mohri, and Zhang,](#page-11-11) [2019b,](#page-11-11) [2020\)](#page-11-13). However, we note that these works retain the above issue of having a query complexity of the form c(β)d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> for a complexity measure c(β) (as discussed in Section [2\)](#page-1-1) such that, in the worst case over distributions P

(respecting the β constraint) the results become ultimately no smaller than the sample complexity of passive learning.

The idea of decomposing a predictor into a decision list based on pairwise disagreement regions has an even closer parallel in the recent work of [Hanneke, Larsen, and Zhivotovskiy](#page-13-7) [\(2024b\)](#page-13-7), which removes a log factor from the lead term in the (first-order) sample complexity of *passive* learning, thereby obtaining an optimal lead term of Θ β ε d + log δ . The overall approach in that work is in many ways similar to the technique in the present work, though with some important differences in the actual algorithms. In particular, since the interest in that work is merely removing a factor log 1 β , it essentially suffices for the algorithm to reduce the best-in-class *error rate* in a region X \ ∆ down to <sup>β</sup> log(1/β) (for PX (∆) = O(β)), so that a uniform Bernstein inequality for the error rate of ERM implies the desired result in that region X \ ∆, and a uniform convergence analysis of ERM under the conditional distribution given ∆ implies the desired result in the region ∆. In contrast, our interest in the present work is a factor of β in the lead term, with a lower-order term of size O˜ d ε , and to achieve this our algorithm aims to reduce (below ε) the *diameter* of a set V<sup>k</sup> of surviving concepts, in a region X \ ∆ (with PX (∆) = O(β)). We achieve this via uniform estimation of error differences, using an appropriate number of samples from these two regions, while precisely controlling the schedule of decreases of this diameter in the algorithm (in part by increasing the ∆ region as needed to maintain this schedule of diameter decreases). Nevertheless, the essential inspiration and strategy behind these two algorithms are notably related, perhaps indicating that the AVID principle might in fact be a widely useful idea.

# B Additional Definitions and Notation

We provide additional definitions and notation required for the formal analysis. A fundamental quantity in statistical learning theory is the *VC dimension* [\(Vapnik and Chervonenkis,](#page-16-3) [1971\)](#page-16-3), which plays an important role in characterizing the optimal query complexity (and optimal sample complexity of passive learning). It is defined as follows.

Definition 4. *For any concept class* C*, the* VC dimension *of* C*, denoted by* VC(C)*, is defined as the supremum* n ∈ <sup>N</sup> ∪ {0} *for which there exists a sequence* {x1, . . . , xn} ∈ X <sup>n</sup> *such that* {(h(x1), . . . , h(xn)) : h ∈ <sup>C</sup>} = {0, 1} <sup>n</sup> *(i.e., all* 2 <sup>n</sup> *classifications are realizable by* <sup>C</sup>*).*

For brevity, in all results, proofs, and discussion below (where C is clear from the context), we will simply denote by d := VC(C). In all statements below, we suppose d < ∞ (see Appendix [G\)](#page-68-0). Also note that, by our assumption that |C| ≥ 3 (see footnote [1\)](#page-1-0), we always have d ≥ 1.

Additional Notation and Conventions: For any distribution P on X × {0, 1}, denote by P<sup>X</sup> the marginal distribution on X . Throughout, we refer to any sequence S ∈ (X × {0, 1}) ∗ as a *data set*. For any x ∈ <sup>R</sup>, it will be convenient to define log(x) = ln(max{x, e}), and for x > 0 we define log(x/0) = x/0 = ∞ and 0 log(x/0) = 0. For a, b ∈ <sup>R</sup>∪ {∞}, we use a∧b or min{a, b} to denote the minimum of a and b, and a∨b or max{a, b} to denote the maximum of a and b. We will make use of standard big-O notation (O, Ω, Θ effectively hide universal constant factors, while O˜, Θ˜ effectively hide log factors) to simplify theorem statements. The precise constant and log factors will always be made explicit in the formal proofs. We also adopt a convention regarding conditional probabilities: all claims involving conditional probabilities given a random variable should be interpreted as holding almost surely (i.e., for a *version* of the conditional probability), such as when claiming that an event holds with conditional probability at least 1 − δ given a random variable X. We also continue the notational conventions introduced in Section [4,](#page-4-1) such as ER(h), DIS(C ′ ), {f ̸= g}, overloading set notation to treat A ⊆ X as notationally interchangeable with its labeled extension A × {0, 1}, extending notation for set-intersection to allow intersections with sequences, and defining empirical estimates Pˆ <sup>S</sup>(A) = |S ∩ A|/|S|. See Section [4](#page-4-1) for details of these conventions.

Measurability: We remark that, formally speaking, an active learning algorithm can be defined simply as a measurable function A : (X × {0, 1}) <sup>m</sup> × X → {0, 1}: that is, taking as input an i.i.d. data set S = {(X<sup>i</sup> , Yi)}i≤<sup>m</sup> and an independent test point X and evaluating to a prediction A(S, X) ∈ {0, 1}. In this view, the number of *queries* is merely bookkeeping, keeping track of the dependences of this function on the labels Y<sup>i</sup> . For simplicity of presentation, we have adopted the common colloquialism of referring to the function hˆ *returned* by <sup>A</sup>(S), which in this view simply refers to the function <sup>A</sup>(S, ·), so that er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) is simply the conditional expectation <sup>E</sup>[**1**[A(S, X) ̸= Y ]|S]. The measurability of the algorithms A defined in this work follows from measurability of the individual operations involved in their execution[<sup>10</sup>](#page-33-2) under the standard measuretheoretic assumptions on (X , C) specified in footnote [1.](#page-1-0) To simplify the presentation, we do not explicitly discuss this in the proofs.

# C The Query Complexity of the AVID Agnostic Algorithm

This section presents a detailed version of Theorem [3,](#page-4-0) bounding the query complexity of the AVID Agnostic algorithm. Recall the definition of the algorithm and notation from Section [4.](#page-4-1) Before stating the theorem, we first discuss a few additional technical aspects of the algorithm omitted from the high-level description in Section [4,](#page-4-1) starting with an explicit specification of the quantities involved. Let c0, c<sup>1</sup> be universal constants, defined by Lemmas [7](#page-35-0) and [8](#page-35-1) of Appendix [D.](#page-34-0) We define[<sup>11</sup>](#page-33-3) C = 11 <sup>10</sup> , C ′′ = 200C 8−5C<sup>3</sup> 2 , and C ′ = √ C′′ <sup>16</sup> . For a given ε, δ ∈ (0, 1) (arguments to <sup>A</sup>avid), as in Section [4](#page-4-1) we let N = log<sup>C</sup> 2 ε , and for k ∈ <sup>N</sup>, let ε<sup>k</sup> = C 1−k , and we then define <sup>m</sup><sup>k</sup> := l 300C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>d</sup> log C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> + log 1 δ m. The algorithm adaptively allocates data subsets <sup>S</sup> 1 k , S 2 k , S 3 k,i, S 4 k during its execution, as described in Section [4.1.](#page-5-1) Recall that S 1 k , S 3 k,i, and S 4 k are all of size m<sup>k</sup> (for any k, i for which they exist). The data subset S 2 k is of size m′ k , formally defined as follows. For the value i<sup>k</sup> and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> as defined in the algorithm at the time that S 2 k is allocated (either in Step [2](#page-6-2) for some value of k, or in Step [9,](#page-6-3) in which case let k = N + 1), letting pˆ<sup>k</sup> := 2Pˆ S<sup>4</sup> k (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), define m′ k := l C ′′c 2 <sup>1</sup>pˆ<sup>k</sup> ε k <sup>d</sup> + log 4(3+N−k) δ m.

We remark that, for simplicity of presentation, we have described the algorithm without explicitly discussing what happens if the algorithm *runs out* of unlabeled examples while allocating examples to subsets S 2 k , S 3 k,i. In this event, the algorithm can simply halt and return an arbitrary predictor <sup>h</sup>ˆ, as the analysis will account for this event in the δ failure probability. To avoid excessive clutter, we do not explicitly mention this case in the description of the algorithm or allocation of data subsets used therein (i.e., we explicitly discuss this only in the analysis, and indeed only in the final part of the proof; see the discussion at the start of Appendix [E\)](#page-36-0).

The following theorem provides a bound on the query complexity achieved by Aavid along with a bound on the unlabeled data set size sufficient to achieve it. This result represents a detailed version of the upper bound in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) of Section [4](#page-4-1) (in particular, Theorems [1](#page-3-0) and [3](#page-4-0) are immediate implications of this result). The constant factors in the big-O will be made explicit in the formal proof. The proof is given in Appendix [E.](#page-36-0)

Theorem 5 (Query Complexity of AVID Agnostic). *For any concept class* C *with* VC(C) < ∞*, letting* d = VC(C)*, for every distribution* P *on* X × {0, 1}*, letting* β = infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h)*, for any* ε, δ ∈ (0, 1)*, if the algorithm* <sup>A</sup>avid *is executed with parameters* (ε, δ)*, with any number* m ≥ M(ε, δ; β) *of*

<sup>10</sup>The only part requiring some care in this regard is the definition of hˆ<sup>k</sup> in [\(3\)](#page-6-9), where formally we require that, given Vk−1, ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , m′ <sup>k</sup>, the function (S 1 k, S<sup>2</sup> <sup>k</sup>, x) 7→ <sup>h</sup>ˆk(x) should be a measurable function; such a measurable function can be shown to exist assuming C (and therefore Vk−1) satisfies the conditions of footnote [1,](#page-1-0) following straightforwardly from arguments of [\(Dudley,](#page-12-5) [1999\)](#page-12-5).

<sup>11</sup>For simplicity of presentation, the constant C plays two major roles in the algorithm. First, it controls the schedule of *diameter* guarantees ε<sup>k</sup> in the algorithm. Second, it controls certain constant factors in uniform concentration guarantees employed in the proof (Lemma [10\)](#page-38-0). If we were to separate these roles, into C<sup>1</sup> and C2, respectively, the two values exhibit a trade-off. In particular, we can admit a schedule ε<sup>k</sup> = C 1−k for *any* choice of 1 < C<sup>1</sup> < 2 by an appropriately large choice of C ′′ (diverging as C<sup>1</sup> → 2) and corresponding C<sup>2</sup> > 1 sufficiently close to 1. The source of this 2 limitation is the multiplicative factor in Lemma [20](#page-45-0) which, in the limit as C ′′ → ∞ and C<sup>2</sup> → 1, becomes <sup>2</sup> 2−C<sup>1</sup> . We also remark that we have defined constants that enable the cleanest presentation of the algorithm and analysis. We leave the issue of optimizing the constants to minimize the query complexity for future work.

*i.i.d.-*P *examples, for a value* M(ε, δ; β) *(defined in Lemma [24\)](#page-49-0) satisfying*

$$M(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta) = O\left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right)\right) = \tilde{O}\left(\frac{\beta \mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon^2} + \frac{\mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon}\right),$$

*then with probability at least* 1 − δ*, the returned predictor* hˆ *satisfies* er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε *and the algorithm makes a number of queries at most* Q(ε, δ; β) *(defined in Lemma [23\)](#page-47-0) satisfying*

$$\begin{aligned}Q(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta) &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \min\left\{ \mathfrak{s} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right\} \left( \mathbf{d} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) \\ &= \tilde{O}\left(\frac{\beta^2 \mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon^2} + \left( \mathfrak{s} \wedge \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right) \mathbf{d}\right).\end{aligned}$$

Remark on adaptivity to β: We emphasize that the algorithm *does not need to know* β in its execution (i.e., it *adaptively* achieves the above query complexity bound *for all* β). A more subtle point worth noting is that we can also *run* the algorithm without *ourselves* knowing β (to choose m), since the guarantee on query complexity holds for *any* unlabeled sample size m ≥ M(ε, δ; β). For instance, if we run the algorithm with a β-independent number of unlabeled examples m = Θ˜ 1 ε 2 d log ε + log δ , the query complexity bound Q(ε, δ; β) would remain valid as stated in Theorem [5.](#page-33-0) Additionally, in the proof (see Lemma [24\)](#page-49-0), we show that, in a sense, even the *unlabeled* sample complexity M(ε, δ; β) is achieved *adaptively*, since the algorithm (with no knowledge of β) only actually uses the first (at most) M(ε, δ; β) unlabeled examples in the sequence. This is itself an interesting feature.
