In particular, if we consider an alternative setting where, rather than getting the unlabeled data altogether at the start, the algorithm can adaptively *sample* new unlabeled examples X<sup>i</sup> ∼ P<sup>X</sup> one-at-a-time during execution (i.e., it has access to an *unlabeled example oracle*, which it can use to construct the data subsets S 1 k , S 2 k , S 3 k,i, S 4 k , during execution), the analysis establishes that the algorithm will succeed while adaptively sampling at most M(ε, δ; β) unlabeled examples (and querying at most Q(ε, δ; β) of them), all *without* knowing β (or anything else about P).

# D Concentration Inequalities

This section presents a number of useful concentration inequalities, essential to the analysis. We begin with the classic *multiplicative Chernoff bound* [\(Chernoff,](#page-11-14) [1952;](#page-11-14) [Bernstein,](#page-11-15) [1924\)](#page-11-15). We will find the following particular form to be useful; since this is slightly different from the more-typical statements of Chernoff bounds, we include a brief explanation of how this result is derived from the more-standard exponential form.

Lemma 6 (Multiplicative Chernoff bound). *Fix any* p ∈ [0, 1] *and* n ∈ <sup>N</sup>*, and let* B1, . . . , B<sup>n</sup> *be i.i.d.* Bernoulli(p) *random variables. Let* B¯ := <sup>1</sup> n P<sup>n</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 B<sup>i</sup> *. For any* δ ∈ (0, 1)*, with probability at least* 1 − δ*, the following both hold:*

$$p \leq \max\left\{2\bar{B}, \frac{8}{n} \ln\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)\right\},$$

$$\bar{B} \leq \max\left\{2p, \frac{6}{n} \ln\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)\right\}.$$

Proof. We include a brief explanation, based on more well-known exponential forms of the Chernoff bound: namely, <sup>P</sup>(B <¯ (1/2)p) ≤ e −np/8 and <sup>P</sup>(B >¯ 2p) ≤ e −np/3 (see e.g., [Zhang,](#page-16-14) [2023\)](#page-16-14).

For the first inequality in the lemma, we note that it trivially holds if p < <sup>8</sup> n ln 2 δ , and otherwise, if p ≥ 8 n ln 2 δ , then by the above exponential tail bound, we have <sup>P</sup>(B <¯ (1/2)p) ≤ e <sup>−</sup>np/<sup>8</sup> ≤ δ 2 . For the second claimed inequality, note that it trivially holds if <sup>6</sup> n ln δ ≥ 1, so let us focus on the case <sup>6</sup> n ln δ < 1. Note that for p ′ ∈ [0, 1] and B′ 1 , . . . , B′ n i.i.d. Bernoulli(p ′ ), and B¯′ = 1 n P<sup>n</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 <sup>B</sup>′ i , for any x ∈ <sup>R</sup> the value of <sup>P</sup>(B¯′ > x) is non-decreasing in p ′ . Thus, letting p ′ = max p, <sup>3</sup> n ln 2 δ ≥ p, this monotonicity (together with the second exponential tail bound above) implies <sup>P</sup>(B >¯ 2p ′ ) ≤ <sup>P</sup>(B¯′ > 2p ′ ) ≤ e <sup>−</sup>np′/<sup>3</sup> ≤ δ 2 . The lemma then follows by the union bound, so that both of these inequalities hold simultaneously with probability at least 1 − δ. ■

We will also rely heavily on *uniform* concentration inequalities. Toward stating these, we first introduce additional useful notation.

VC dimension of collections of sets: As is standard in the literature, we overload the definition of *VC dimension* [\(Vapnik and Chervonenkis,](#page-16-3) [1971\)](#page-16-3) to also allow for collections of sets. Formally, for any non-empty set Z and any non-empty A ⊆ 2 <sup>Z</sup> (i.e., a collection of subsets of Z), the VC dimension of A, denoted by VC(A), is the supremum n ∈ N ∪ {0} for which there exists Z ⊆ Z with |Z| = n such that {Z ∩ A : A ∈ A} = 2<sup>Z</sup> (i.e., it is possible to pick out any subset of Z by intersection with an appropriate A ∈ A). Equivalently, VC(A) is the VC dimension (Definition [4\)](#page-32-0) of the *indicator functions* {**1**<sup>A</sup> : A ∈ A}.

Uniform concentration term: For any non-empty set Z and any non-empty A ⊆ 2 <sup>Z</sup> , for any n ∈ <sup>N</sup> and δ ∈ (0, 1), define (for a universal constant c<sup>0</sup> defined by Lemma [7](#page-35-0) below)

$$\varepsilon(n, \delta; \mathcal{A}) := \frac{c_0}{n} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) \log\left(\frac{n}{\text{VC}(\mathcal{A})}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right). \quad (7)$$

The following result represents a uniform variant of the classic *Bernstein inequality* (or Bennett inequality) [\(Bernstein,](#page-11-15) [1924;](#page-11-15) [Bennett,](#page-11-16) [1962\)](#page-11-16). It can be derived from results proven by [Vapnik and](#page-16-4) [Chervonenkis](#page-16-4) [\(1974\)](#page-16-4) (see [Hanneke and Kpotufe,](#page-13-21) [2022](#page-13-21) for an explicit derivation, via a layered application of Massart's lemma and Bousquet's inequality). We additionally include implications providing a uniform variant of multiplicative Chernoff bounds, which are easily derived from the stated uniform Bernstein inequality (taking B = ∅).

Lemma 7 (Uniform Bernstein and multiplicative Chernoff bounds). *There is a finite universal constant* c<sup>0</sup> > 1 *for which the following holds. Fix any* n ∈ <sup>N</sup>*,* δ ∈ (0, 1)*, any non-empty set* Z*, and any set* A ⊆ 2 <sup>Z</sup> *with* VC(A) < ∞*.* [<sup>12</sup>](#page-35-2) *Define* ε(n, δ; A) *as in* [\(7\)](#page-35-3)*. Fix any distribution* P *on* Z *and let* Z = {Z1, . . . , Zn} ∼ P <sup>n</sup> *(i.i.d.* P *random variables). For any measurable set* A ⊆ Z*, define its* empirical probability PˆZ(A) := <sup>1</sup> n P<sup>n</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 **1**[Z<sup>i</sup> ∈ A]*. With probability at least* 1 − δ*, every* A, B ∈ A ∪ {∅} *satisfy the following (where* A ⊕ B := (A \ B) ∪ (B \ A) *denotes the symmetric difference)*

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| (\hat{P}_Z(A) - \hat{P}_Z(B)) - (P(A) - P(B)) \right| \\ & \leq \sqrt{\min\{P(A \oplus B), \hat{P}_Z(A \oplus B)\}\varepsilon(n, \delta; \mathcal{A}) + \varepsilon(n, \delta; \mathcal{A})}. \end{aligned}$$

*Moreover, for any* ε > 0 *and* α ∈ (0, 1) *satisfying* ε(n, δ; A) ≤ α 2 4 ε*, the above inequality immediately yields the following implications:* ∀A ∈ A*,*

$$\begin{aligned}\hat{P}_z(A) \geq \varepsilon &\implies P(A) > (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon, & \text{or equivalently, } P(A) \leq (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon &\implies \hat{P}_z(A) < \varepsilon \\ P(A) \geq \varepsilon &\implies \hat{P}_z(A) > (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon, & \text{or equivalently, } \hat{P}_z(A) \leq (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon &\implies P(A) < \varepsilon.\end{aligned}$$

We also make use of a uniform concentration inequality which refines the classic uniform convergence bound q 1 n VC(A) + log 1 δ of [Talagrand](#page-15-6) [\(1994\)](#page-15-6) in the case that S A has small measure under P. The lemma is well-known in the literature, and follows immediately from expectation bounds based on chaining involving an *envelope* function (e.g., Theorem 2.14.1 of [van der Vaart and Wellner,](#page-16-15) [1996\)](#page-16-15) together with Bousquet's inequality [\(Bousquet,](#page-11-17) [2002\)](#page-11-17) to achieve high probability. For completeness, we provide a brief direct proof, by simply applying the uniform convergence bound of [Talagrand](#page-15-6) [\(1994\)](#page-15-6) to the samples from the conditional distribution given a set D ⊇ S A.

Lemma 8. *There is a finite universal constant* c<sup>1</sup> ≥ 1 *for which the following holds. Let* A *be as in Lemma [7,](#page-35-0) and suppose* D ⊆ Z *is a measurable set such that* ∀A ∈ A*,* A ⊆ D*. Then for the same quantities as Lemma [7,](#page-35-0) if* P(D) ≥ 9 n ln 4 δ *, then with probability at least* 1 − δ*,* ∀A ∈ A

$$|\hat{P}_Z(A) - P(A)| \leq c_1 \sqrt{\frac{P(D)}{n}} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right).$$

<sup>12</sup>We suppose standard mild measure-theoretic restrictions on A and the σ-algebra of Z, from empirical process theory: namely, the image-admissible Suslin condition [\(Dudley,](#page-12-5) [1999\)](#page-12-5).

Proof. Note that the samples in Z ∩ D are conditionally i.i.d. P(·|D) given |Z ∩ D|. For each A ∈ A, denote by PˆZ(A|D) := PˆZ∩D(A) (or 0 if |Z ∩ D| = 0). Applying the uniform convergence bound of [Talagrand](#page-15-6) [\(1994\)](#page-15-6) to the samples in Z ∩ D under the conditional distribution given |Z ∩ D|, together with the law of total probability, yields that, with probability at least 1 − δ 2 , ∀A ∈ A,

$$|\hat{P}_Z(A|D) - P(A|D)| \leq c_1 \sqrt{\frac{1}{|Z \cap D|}} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) + \log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right) \right), \quad (8)$$

for a finite universal constant c ′ <sup>1</sup> ≥ 1. Moreover, by Bernstein's inequality (see Theorem 2.10 of [Boucheron, Lugosi, and Massart,](#page-11-18) [2013\)](#page-11-18), with probability at least 1 − δ 2 ,

$$|\hat{P}_z(D) - P(D)| \leq \sqrt{\frac{2P(D)}{n}} \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right) + \frac{1}{n} \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right) \leq 2\sqrt{\frac{P(D)}{n}} \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right), \quad (9)$$

where the last inequality is due to the assumption that P(D) ≥ 9 n ln 4 δ . By the union bound, these two events occur simultaneously with probability at least 1 − δ. Suppose this occurs. In particular, by the assumption that P(D) ≥ 9 n ln 4 δ , [\(9\)](#page-36-2) further implies

$$\frac{1}{n} |Z \cap D| = \hat{P}_Z(D) \geq P(D) - 2\sqrt{\frac{P(D)}{n}} \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right) \geq \frac{1}{3} P(D),$$

so that the right hand side of [\(8\)](#page-36-3) is at most

$$c'_1 \sqrt{\frac{3}{nP(D)}} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) + \log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right) \right).$$

Combining this with [\(8\)](#page-36-3) and [\(9\)](#page-36-2) implies that ∀A ∈ A, since A ⊆ D,

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \hat{P}_z(A) - P(A) \right| = \left| \hat{P}_z(D) \hat{P}_z(A|D) - P(D) P(A|D) \right| \\ & \leq P(D) \left| \hat{P}_z(A|D) - P(A|D) \right| + \hat{P}_z(A|D) 2 \sqrt{\frac{P(D)}{n}} \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right) \\ & \leq c_1 \sqrt{\frac{3P(D)}{n}} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) + \log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right) \right) + 2 \sqrt{\frac{P(D)}{n}} \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right) \\ & \leq c_1 \sqrt{\frac{P(D)}{n}} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right), \end{aligned}$$

where c<sup>1</sup> := c ′ 1 √ 6 + 2p ln(4e) (recalling log(x) := ln(x ∨ e)). ■

# E Proof of Theorem [5:](#page-33-0) Query Complexity of the AVID Agnostic Algorithm

The formal proof of Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) given at the end of this section, will be built up from a sequence of lemmas, roughly following the outline presented in Section [4.2.](#page-7-0)

Throughout this section, we fix an arbitrary concept class C (with d := VC(C) < ∞) and distribution P on X × {0, 1}, let β = infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h), fix any ε, δ ∈ (0, 1) (where ε, δ are inputs to the AVID algorithm), let (X1, Y1),(X2, Y2), . . . be independent P-distributed examples, and we let all values (N, εk, mk, m′ k , etc.) be defined as in Appendix [C,](#page-33-1) based on these values ε, δ, and the examples (X1, Y1),(X2, Y2), . . .. Also let h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> denote any concept with

$$\text{er}_P(h^*) < \inf_{h \in \mathbb{C}} \text{er}_P(h) + \frac{\varepsilon}{10^4}. \quad (10)$$

For full generality, we do not assume there exists a minimizer achieving the infimum on the right hand side; rather, any choice of h ⋆ satisfying this *near*-minimality property will suffice for our purposes in the analysis below.

To simplify the proof, we will establish the sequence of lemmas under a scenario where the algorithm is executed with an *inexhaustible* source of examples (for the adaptive allocation of data subsets): i.e., an infinite sequence (X1, Y1),(X2, Y2), . . . of independent P-distributed examples. However, it will follow from these lemmas that, with high probability, the algorithm only depends on a *finite* prefix (X1, Y1), . . . ,(Xm, Ym), for a sufficiently large m = M(ε, δ; β) as in Theorem [5](#page-33-0) (see Lemma [24\)](#page-49-0). At the end of the section, when combining the lemmas into a formal proof of Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) we will return to the standard setting where the algorithm has access *only* to such a finite prefix. In that context, the event that the algorithm attempts to access any examples (Xt, Yt) with t > m will be accounted for as part of the allowed δ-probability failure event, and thus (as mentioned in Appendix [C\)](#page-33-1) in such a case the algorithm can simply halt and return an arbitrary predictor hˆ. As mentioned in the remark following Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) the fact that the algorithm *adaptively* decides how many unlabeled examples to use is itself an interesting feature, as it means the algorithm can be considered adaptive to β even in its use of unlabeled examples.

Before proceeding with the proof, we first introduce some convenient notation regarding the values of k and i encountered in the algorithm. If the algorithm returns in Step [9,](#page-6-3) denote by K := N + 1, and otherwise, let K be the maximum value of k reached in the '*For*' loop in the algorithm; we argue in Lemma [10](#page-38-0) below that the algorithm terminates eventually, with high probability, so that this latter case coincides with the case of returning in Step [4,](#page-6-5) with K being the value of k on which this occurs. Let K := {1, . . . , K ∧ N}: that is, the set of values of k encountered in the '*For*' loop in the algorithm. Also, for each k ∈ K, denote by I<sup>k</sup> the values of i encountered by the algorithm on round k; in particular, for k < K, I<sup>k</sup> = {ik, . . . , ik+1}. In the case K = N + 1, for convenience also denote by I<sup>N</sup> +1 := {iN +1}.

We begin with a lemma which motivates our choice of sample size m<sup>k</sup> for S 1 k , S 3 k,i, S 4 k . Recall <sup>m</sup><sup>k</sup> := l 300C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>d</sup> log C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> + log δ m. Also recall our convention (adopted throughout this work) of treating sets D ⊆ X as notationally interchangeable with their labeled extension D × {0, 1}, such as in A ∩ D or A \ D for A ⊆ X × {0, 1}.

Lemma 9. *Fix any set* D ⊆ X *and define a family of subsets of* X × {0, 1}:

$$\mathcal{A} = \left\{ ((\text{ER}(f) \cap \{f = g\}) \cup (\text{ER}(h) \cap \{f \neq g\})) \setminus D : f, g, h \in \mathbb{C} \right\} \cup \left\{ (\{f \neq g\} \times \{0, 1\}) \setminus D : f, g \in \mathbb{C} \right\} \cup \left\{ \text{ER}(h) \setminus D : h \in \mathbb{C} \right\}.$$

*For any* n ∈ N *and* δ ′ ∈ (0, 1)*, let* ε(n, δ′ ; A) *be defined as in* [\(7\)](#page-35-3)*. For each* k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1}*, letting* δ<sup>k</sup> := δε<sup>2</sup> k+3 <sup>72</sup> *, it holds that*

$$\varepsilon(m_k, \delta_k; \mathcal{A}) < \frac{\varepsilon_k}{C''}. \quad (11)$$

Proof. We begin by bounding VC(A), as needed to evaluate ε(mk, δk; A). Define the following families of subsets of X × {0, 1}:

$$\begin{aligned}\mathcal{A}_0 &:= \{\text{ER}(h) : h \in \mathbb{C}\} \cup \{\emptyset, \mathcal{X} \times \{0, 1\}\}, \\ \mathcal{A}_1 &:= \{\{f \neq g\} \times \{0, 1\} : f, g \in \mathbb{C}\} \cup \{\mathcal{X} \times \{0, 1\}\}, \\ \mathcal{A}_2 &:= \{((A \setminus C) \cup (B \cap C)) \setminus D : A, B \in \mathcal{A}_0, C \in \mathcal{A}_1\}.\end{aligned}$$

First note that A ⊆ A2. To see this, note that for any f, g, h ∈ <sup>C</sup>, taking A = ER(f), B = ER(h), C = {f ̸= g}×{0, 1}, we have that ((ER(f)∩{f = g})∪(ER(h)∩{f ̸= g}))\D = ((A\C)∪(B∩ C)) \ D ∈ A2. Similarly, for any f, g ∈ <sup>C</sup>, taking A = ∅, B = X × {0, 1}, C = {f ̸= g} × {0, 1} reveals ({f ̸= g} × {0, 1}) \ D = ((A \ C) ∪ (B ∩ C)) \ D ∈ A2. Finally, for h, f ∈ <sup>C</sup>, taking A = ER(h), B = ∅, C = {f ̸= f}×{0, 1} = ∅ reveals ER(h)\D = ((A\C)∪(B∩C))\D ∈ A2. Next we bound VC(A2). It is immediate from the definition that VC({ER(h) : h ∈ <sup>C</sup>}) = d. Moreover, this implies VC(A0) ≤ d + 2 [\(Vidyasagar,](#page-16-16) [2003,](#page-16-16) Lemma 4.11). Also note that A<sup>1</sup> ⊆ {A ⊕ B : A, B ∈ A0}, where A ⊕ B := (A \ B) ∪ (B \ A) is the symmetric difference: that is, trivially (X × {0, 1})⊕ ∅ = X × {0, 1}, and for any f, g ∈ C, {f ̸= g} × {0, 1} = ER(f)⊕ER(g). Thus, any element of A<sup>2</sup> can be expressed as a fixed function of four sets A, B, A′ , B′ ∈ A0: namely (A, B, A′ , B′ ) 7→ ((A \ (A′ ⊕ B′ )) ∪ (B ∩ (A′ ⊕ B′ ))) \ D. Based on this fact, well-known results about the effect of such combinations on the VC dimension imply VC(A2) = O(VC(A0)): explicitly, Theorem 4.5 of [Vidyasagar](#page-16-16) [\(2003\)](#page-16-16) implies VC(A2) ≤ 25VC(A0) ≤ 25(d + 2). By the

assumption that |C| ≥ 3 (footnote [1\)](#page-1-0) we know d ≥ 1, so that 25(d + 2) ≤ 75d. Altogether, we have VC(A) ≤ 75d.

With this in mind, we may note that (also using that d ≥ 1 and C ′′ ≥ 9C 3 )

$$\begin{aligned} m_k &\geq \frac{300C''c_0}{\varepsilon_k} \left( d \log\left(\frac{C''c_0}{\varepsilon_k}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \\ &\geq \frac{150C''c_0}{\varepsilon_k} \left( d \log\left(\frac{C''c_0}{\varepsilon_k}\right) + \log\left(\frac{9C^3}{\delta\varepsilon_k}\right) \right) \\ &\geq \frac{2C''c_0}{\varepsilon_k} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) \log\left(\frac{C''c_0}{\varepsilon_k}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_k}\right) \right). \end{aligned} \quad (12)$$

In particular, if VC(A) ≥ 1, then by Corollary 4.1 of [Vidyasagar](#page-16-16) [\(2003\)](#page-16-16), [\(12\)](#page-38-1) implies

$$m_k > \frac{C'' c_0}{\varepsilon_k} \left( \text{VC}(\mathcal{A}) \log \left( \frac{m_k}{\text{VC}(\mathcal{A})} \right) + \log \left( \frac{1}{\delta_k} \right) \right). \quad (13)$$

Moreover, if VC(A) = 0, then recalling we define 0 log(1/0) = 0, [\(12\)](#page-38-1) trivially implies [\(13\)](#page-38-2) in this case as well. Thus, regardless of the value of VC(A), by definition of ε(mk, δk; A), the claim in [\(11\)](#page-37-0) follows from [\(13\)](#page-38-2). ■

We continue the proof with a lemma conveniently summarizing several uniform concentration bounds which are useful in various places throughout the rest of the proof. In particular, the lemma focuses on concentration inequalities in the X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> region of focus of the learning algorithm. It will therefore be convenient to explicitly define the portion of the functions in V (4) k−1 specific to this region: namely, for every k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, define[<sup>13</sup>](#page-38-3)

$$V_{k-1}^{(3)} := \{f\mathbb{1}_{\{f=g\}} + h\mathbb{1}_{\{f \neq g\}} : f, g \in V_{k-1}, h \in \mathbb{C}\}.$$

Lemma 10. *On an event* E<sup>0</sup> *of probability at least* 1 − 4 *, for every* k ∈ {1, . . . , K}*, it holds that*

$$\begin{aligned} & \forall h, h' \in V_{k-1}^{(3)}, \quad \left| \left( \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h) \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h') \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \right) \right. \\ & \quad \left. - (P(\text{ER}(h) \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h') \setminus \Delta_{i_k})) \right| \\ & < \sqrt{P_X(\{h \neq h'\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \frac{\varepsilon_k}{C''}} + \frac{\varepsilon_k}{C''}, \end{aligned} \quad (14)$$

*and for every* k ∈ K *and every* i ∈ Ik*,* ∀f, g ∈ <sup>C</sup>*,*

$$\hat{P}_{k,i}^3(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) \geq \varepsilon_{k+2} \implies P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) > \varepsilon_{k+3} \quad (15)$$

$$\hat{P}_{S_{k,i}}(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) \leq \varepsilon_{k+2} \implies P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) < \varepsilon_{k+1}, \quad (16)$$

*and moreover,* max I<sup>k</sup> ≤ εk+3 *. In particular, the latter implies the algorithm eventually terminates (in Step [9](#page-6-3) if* K = N + 1*, or in Step [4](#page-6-5) if* K ≤ N*).*

Proof. Consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having a non-zero probability of k ≤ K. Let δ<sup>k</sup> be as in Lemma [9.](#page-37-1) Recall that the data set S 1 k is independent of all data involved in rounds k ′ < k in the algorithm, whereas the event k ≤ K and (in this event) the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> are entirely determined by data involved in rounds k ′ < k. Thus, even conditioned on the event that k ≤ K and and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , the data set S 1 k remains conditionally i.i.d.-P. Therefore, letting A<sup>k</sup> denote the set A as defined in Lemma [9](#page-37-1) with D = ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , applying the uniform Bernstein inequality (Lemma [7](#page-35-0) in Appendix [D\)](#page-34-0) with this A<sup>k</sup> under the conditional distribution given the event k ≤ K and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> implies that, with conditional probability at least 1 − δ<sup>k</sup> given the event k ≤ K and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , it holds that ∀A, B ∈ Ak,

$$\left| \left( \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(A) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(B) \right) - \left( P(A) - P(B) \right) \right| \leq \sqrt{P(A \oplus B)\varepsilon(m_k, \delta_k; \mathcal{A}_k)} + \varepsilon(m_k, \delta_k; \mathcal{A}_k). \quad (17)$$

<sup>13</sup>Since this work focuses on binary classification, V (3) k−1 can equivalently be stated as {Maj(f, g, h) : f, g ∈ Vk−1, h ∈ C}, where Maj(f, g, h)(x) = **1**[f(x) + g(x) + h(x) ≥ 2] is the majority vote function. The definition of V (3) k−1 above expresses a more-general form, which, as we discuss in Section [G,](#page-68-0) also extends to *multiclass* classification.

By the law of total probability, on an event E0,k of probability at least 1 − δk, if k ≤ K (and thus ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and A<sup>k</sup> are defined) then [\(17\)](#page-38-4) holds ∀A, B ∈ Ak.

In particular, on the event E0,k, supposing k ≤ K, if we consider any h, h′ ∈ V (3) k−1 , then for the sets A = ER(h) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ∈ A<sup>k</sup> and B = ER(h ′ ) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ∈ Ak, we may note that the symmetric difference A ⊕ B = (ER(h) ⊕ ER(h ′ )) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> = ({h ̸= h ′} × {0, 1}) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , so that together with [\(11\)](#page-37-0) of Lemma [9,](#page-37-1) [\(17\)](#page-38-4) implies

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \left( \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h) \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h') \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \right) - \left( P(\text{ER}(h) \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h') \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \right) \right| \\ & < \sqrt{P_X(\{h \neq h'\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \frac{\varepsilon_k}{C''}} + \frac{\varepsilon_k}{C''}. \end{aligned} \quad (18)$$

To arrive at the claim in [\(14\)](#page-38-5), we merely note that for any f, g, f′ , g′ ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> and h, h′ ∈ <sup>C</sup>, letting DL(f, g, h) := f**1**{f=g} + h**1**{f̸=g} and DL(f ′ , g′ , h′ ) := f ′**1**{<sup>f</sup> ′=g ′} + h ′**1**{<sup>f</sup> ′̸=g ′}, for any x ∈ X \ Dk−1, we have g(x) = f(x) = f ′ (x) = g ′ (x), so that DL(f, g, h)(x) = f(x) = f ′ (x) = DL(f ′ , g′ , h′ )(x). Thus, any h, h′ ∈ V (3) k−1 have h(x) = h ′ (x) for all x /∈ Dk−1, and therefore

$$\hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h) \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h') \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) = \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h) \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h') \setminus \Delta_{i_k}),$$

so that [\(14\)](#page-38-5) follows from [\(18\)](#page-39-0). To unify the discussion below, for any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} with probability zero of k ≤ K, also denote by E0,k the event (of probability one) that k > K.

Turning now to the claims in [\(15\)](#page-38-6) and [\(16\)](#page-38-7), consider any (k, i) having non-zero probability that k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik. Note that, since S 3 k,i is a data set of size mk, allocated from the remaining *unused* unlabeled data upon reaching Step [5](#page-6-4) with values (k, i) (noting this can happen at most once in the algorithm), the samples in S 3 k,i are conditionally i.i.d.-P given k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik, and moreover, S 3 k,i is conditionally independent of ∆<sup>i</sup> given the events that k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik. In the event that k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik, let Ak,i denote the set A as defined in Lemma [9](#page-37-1) with D = ∆<sup>i</sup> . Recalling again our definition of C = <sup>10</sup> and C ′′ ≥ 32C 5 C C−1 2 , note that for α = 1 − C , [\(11\)](#page-37-0) of Lemma [9](#page-37-1) implies ε(mk, δk; Ak,i) < ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>C</sup>′′ < α 2 4 εk+2 < α 2 4 εk+1. Therefore, applying Lemma [7](#page-35-0) of Appendix [D](#page-34-0) under the conditional distribution given the events that k ∈ K and i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> and the set ∆<sup>i</sup> , we have that with conditional probability at least 1 − δk, ∀f, g ∈ <sup>C</sup>, the set ({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) × {0, 1} ∈ Ak,i satisfies

$$\hat{P}_{k,i}^3(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) \geq \varepsilon_{k+2} \implies P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) > (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon_{k+2} = \varepsilon_{k+3}$$

and

$$\hat{P}_{S_{k,i}^3}(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) \leq \varepsilon_{k+2} = (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon_{k+1} \implies P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) < \varepsilon_{k+1}.$$

By the law of total probability, there is an event E0,k,i of probability at least 1 − δk, on which, if k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik, then the above inequalities hold ∀f, g ∈ <sup>C</sup>. To unify cases, for any (k, i) with k ≤ N and i ≤ 1/εk+3 having probability zero of satisfying k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik, also define E0,k,i as the event (of probability one) that either k /∈ K or i /∈ Ik.

We have thus established [\(14\)](#page-38-5) for all k ≤ K and [\(15](#page-38-6) - [16\)](#page-38-7) for all k ∈ K and i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> with i ≤ 1/εk+3, on the event <sup>E</sup><sup>0</sup> := T <sup>k</sup>≤N+1 <sup>E</sup>0,k ∩ T k≤N T i≤1/εk+3 E0,k,i. By the union bound, E<sup>0</sup> fails with probability at most

$$\sum_{k=1}^{N+1} \left( \delta_k + \sum_{i \leq 1 \leq \varepsilon_{k+3}} \delta_i \right) \leq \sum_{k=1}^{N+1} \left( 1 + \frac{1}{\varepsilon_{k+3}} \right) \delta_k \leq \sum_{k=1}^{N+1} \frac{2\delta_k}{\varepsilon_{k+3}} = \sum_{k=1}^{N+1} \frac{\delta}{36} \varepsilon_{k+3} < \frac{\delta}{4},$$

where the equality follows from our definition of δ<sup>k</sup> = δε<sup>2</sup> k+3 <sup>72</sup> (from Lemma [9\)](#page-37-1) and the last inequality follows from our choice of C = 11 <sup>10</sup> .

Finally, we argue that, on the event E0, for any k ∈ K, the maximum value of i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> satisfies i ≤ 1/εk+3. We argue this by induction. Specifically, we will argue that, for any k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik, PX(X \ ∆i) ≤ 1 − iεk+3. For the purpose of induction, suppose that for some k ∈ K, we have PX(X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 1 − ikεk+3 (which is trivially satisfied for k = 1, since i<sup>k</sup> = 0, which can therefore serve as a base case for induction). Taking this i<sup>k</sup> as a base case for a further nested

induction on i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> (noting that i<sup>k</sup> is the minimum element of Ik), suppose that for some i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> we have PX(X \ ∆i) ≤ 1 − iεk+3. Since probabilities are non-negative, this necessarily implies i ≤ 1/εk+3. Then note that, if i is not the maximal element of Ik, the algorithm augments ∆<sup>i</sup> in Step [7,](#page-6-10) so that ∆i+1 = ∆<sup>i</sup> ∪ {f ̸= g} for (f, g) defined in Step [6.](#page-6-6) By the criterion in Step [5,](#page-6-4) we further know that Pˆ S<sup>3</sup> k,i ({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+2. Since i ≤ 1/εk+3, the event E<sup>0</sup> implies [\(15\)](#page-38-6) holds, which therefore implies PX(∆i+1 \ ∆i) = PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+3, so that PX(X \ ∆i+1) = PX(X \ ∆i) − PX(∆i+1 \ ∆i) < 1 − (i + 1)εk+3, thus extending the inductive hypothesis. By the principle of induction, this establishes that PX(X \ ∆i) ≤ 1 − iεk+3 for every i ∈ Ik. In particular, returning to the induction on k, in the event that this k is not the maximal element of K, we have ik+1 ∈ Ik, so that PX(X \ ∆<sup>i</sup>k+1 ) ≤ 1 − ik+1εk+3 ≤ 1 − ik+1ε(k+1)+3, which therefore extends the inductive hypothesis for k. By the principle of induction, we have thus established that every k ∈ K and i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> satisfy PX(X \ ∆i) ≤ 1 − iεk+3. In particular, since probabilities are non-negative, this immediately implies any such (k, i) satisfy i ≤ 1/εk+3, as claimed. Thus, on the event E0, we have established all of the claimed inequalities: [\(14\)](#page-38-5) for all k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, and [\(15](#page-38-6) - [16\)](#page-38-7) for all k ∈ K and i ∈ Ik, which further satisfy max I<sup>k</sup> ≤ 1 εk+3 . ■

The following is an obvious implication of Lemma [10,](#page-38-0) which will be useful to state explicitly for later reference.

Lemma 11. *On the event* E0*, for every* k ∈ K *and* i ∈ Ik*, if the algorithm reaches Step [6](#page-6-6) with these values* (k, i)*, then for* f, g *as defined there,*

$$P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) > \varepsilon_{k+3}.$$

*Moreover, on the event* E0*, every* k ∈ {1, . . . , K} *with* ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ̸= ∅ *satisfies* PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > εk+2*.*

Proof. By the condition in Step [5,](#page-6-4) if the algorithm reaches Step [6](#page-6-6) then Pˆ S<sup>3</sup> k,i ({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+2. By [\(15\)](#page-38-6) of Lemma [10,](#page-38-0) on the event E0, this implies PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+3.

Turning now to the second claim, suppose again that E<sup>0</sup> occurs, and first note that this claim is trivially satisfied if ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>K</sup> = ∅. To address the remaining case, suppose ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>K</sup> ̸= ∅, and consider the minimum value k ′ ∈ {1, . . . , K} for which <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup>k′ ̸<sup>=</sup> ∅. By definition we have <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup><sup>1</sup> = ∆<sup>0</sup> <sup>=</sup> ∅, which implies we must have k ′ ≥ 2. By minimality of k ′ , we also know that the algorithm reaches Step [6](#page-6-6) at least once during round k = k ′ − 1 of the '*For*' loop, in particular with i = ik. Thus, letting (f, g) be as defined in Step [6](#page-6-6) for these values (k, i) = (k ′ − 1, ik′−1), by the first claim in the lemma, we have PX(∆<sup>i</sup>k′ ) ≥ PX(f ̸= g) = PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup>k′−<sup>1</sup> ) > εk′+2. Thus, since ∆<sup>i</sup>k′′ is non-decreasing in k ′′, and minimality of k ′ implies all k ′′ with <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup>k′′ ̸<sup>=</sup> ∅ have <sup>k</sup> ′′ ≥ k ′ , we conclude that every k ′′ ∈ {1, . . . , K} with <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup>k′′ ̸<sup>=</sup> ∅ satisfies <sup>P</sup>X(∆<sup>i</sup>k′′ ) ≥ <sup>P</sup>X(∆<sup>i</sup>k′ ) > εk′+2 ≥ <sup>ε</sup>k′′+2. ■

Next we state a bound on the diameters of Vk−<sup>1</sup> and V (3) k−1 , useful for Lemmas [15,](#page-42-0) [20,](#page-45-0) and [22.](#page-46-0)

Lemma 12. *On the event* E0*, for every* k ∈ {1, . . . , K}*,*

$$\sup_{f, g \in V_{k-1}} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \leq \varepsilon_k \quad (19)$$

$$\text{and } \sup_{\substack{f, g \in V_{k-1}^{(3)}}} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \leq 3\varepsilon_k. \quad (20)$$

Proof. Throughout this proof, we suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> holds. The inequality [\(19\)](#page-40-0) is trivially satisfied for k = 1, recalling that V<sup>0</sup> = C and ε<sup>1</sup> = C <sup>0</sup> = 1. For the remaining case, fix any k ′ ∈ {2, . . . , K} and consider the round k = k ′ − 1 in the '*For*' loop (noting that, by definition of K, we have k = k ′ − 1 ∈ K regardless of whether K = N + 1 or K ≤ N). Since k + 1 = k ′ ≤ K, we know the algorithm reaches Step [8](#page-6-11) in round k (i.e., it does not terminate early in Step [4](#page-6-5) during round k). In particular, this means the condition in Step [5](#page-6-4) fails for the value i = ik+1 = max Ik: that is, maxf,g∈V<sup>k</sup> <sup>P</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> S<sup>3</sup> k,i ({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup>k+1 ) ≤ εk+2. By [\(16\)](#page-38-7) of Lemma [10,](#page-38-0) this implies

$$\sup_{f, g \in V_{k'-1}} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_{k'}}) = \sup_{f, g \in V_k} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_{k+1}}) < \varepsilon_{k+1} = \varepsilon_{k'}.$$

To show [\(20\)](#page-40-1), let k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, and for any f, g ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> and h ∈ <sup>C</sup>, denote by DL(f, g, h) := f**1**{f=g} + h**1**{f̸=g} ∈ V (3) k−1 . Note that for any f, g, f′ , g′ ∈ Vk−1, h, h′ ∈ <sup>C</sup>, and x ∈ X , if g(x) = f(x) = f ′ (x) = g ′ (x), then DL(f, g, h)(x) = DL(f ′ , g′ , h′ )(x). Therefore,

$$P_X(\{\text{DL}(f, g, h) \neq \text{DL}(f', g', h')\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \leq P_X((\{f \neq g\} \cup \{f' \neq g'\} \cup \{f \neq f'\}) \setminus \Delta_{i_k})$$

$$\leq P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + P_X(\{f' \neq g'\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + P_X(\{f \neq f'\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \leq 3\varepsilon_k,$$

where the last inequality is by [\(19\)](#page-40-0). This completes the proof of the lemma. ■

The following Lemmas [13](#page-41-0) and [14](#page-41-1) concern concentration of empirical errors in the set S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , which will be useful in establishing guarantees on the quality of hˆ <sup>k</sup> (in Lemma [15\)](#page-42-0) and of the functions in V<sup>k</sup> (in Lemmas [16](#page-43-0) and [17\)](#page-43-1) below. We first need to argue that the pˆ<sup>k</sup> quantities approximate PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), which leads to the data sets S k being of appropriate size for concentration of empirical error rates.

Lemma 13. *There is an event* E<sup>1</sup> *of probability at least* 1− δ 4 *such that, on* E0∩E1*,* ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , K}*, the quantity* pˆ<sup>k</sup> := 2Pˆ S<sup>4</sup> k (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) *(as defined above) satisfies*

$$P_X(\Delta_{i_k}) \leq \hat{p}_k \leq 4P_X(\Delta_{i_k}). \quad (21)$$

Proof. Consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having non-zero probability that k ≤ K. Note that the execution of the algorithm does not depend on S 4 k at any time prior to Step [2](#page-6-2) of round k (or Step [9](#page-6-3) if k = N + 1), supposing this step is even reached in the algorithm (i.e., k ≤ K). Thus, since the event that k ≤ K and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> are both completely determined by events occurring prior to this first time the examples in S 4 k are used by the algorithm, we have that S 4 k is independent of these. Thus, conditioned on the event that k ≤ K and on the random variable ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , we have that for the sequence of m<sup>k</sup> examples (Xt, Yt) comprising S 4 k , the corresponding sequence of indicator random variables **1**[X<sup>t</sup> ∈ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ] are conditionally independent Bernoulli(PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> )) random variables. Therefore, applying a multiplicative Chernoff bound (Lemma [6](#page-34-1) of Appendix [D\)](#page-34-0) under the conditional distribution given the event k ≤ K and the random variable ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , together with the law of total probability, we have that on an event E1,k of probability at least 1 − δε<sup>k</sup> <sup>44</sup> , if k ≤ K, then

$$P_X(\Delta_{i_k}) \leq \max \left\{ 2\hat{P}_{S_k^4}(\Delta_{i_k}), \frac{8}{m_k} \ln \left( \frac{88}{\delta \varepsilon_k} \right) \right\}, \quad (22)$$

$$\hat{P}_{S_k^4}(\Delta_{i_k}) \leq \max\left\{2P_X(\Delta_{i_k}), \frac{6}{m_k} \ln\left(\frac{88}{\delta \varepsilon_k}\right)\right\}. \quad (23)$$

For simplicity, for any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having probability zero of k ≤ K, simply define E1,k as the event of probability one that k > K, so that the above claim also holds (vacuously) for such values k. Define an event E<sup>1</sup> = T<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 E1,k, and note that, by the union bound, E<sup>1</sup> occurs with probability at least 1 − P<sup>N</sup> +1 k=1 δε<sup>k</sup> <sup>44</sup> ≥ 1 − δ 4 .

We now argue these inequalities further imply the simpler inequalities stated in [\(21\)](#page-41-2), on the additional event E0. Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> holds, and let k ∈ {1, . . . , K}. If ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> = ∅, [\(21\)](#page-41-2) trivially holds since pˆ<sup>k</sup> = 0 = PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). To address the remaining case, suppose ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ̸= ∅. By the final claim in Lemma [11,](#page-40-2) we have PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > εk+2. Also note that, by definition of m<sup>k</sup> (and recalling |C| ≥ 2, which implies d ≥ 1), we have <sup>8</sup> m<sup>k</sup> ln 88 δε<sup>k</sup> < εk+2. In particular, these imply 2PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > 2εk+2 > 6 m<sup>k</sup> ln 88 δε<sup>k</sup> , so that the right hand side of [\(23\)](#page-41-3) equals 2PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) and hence pˆ<sup>k</sup> ≤ 4PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). Moreover, since <sup>8</sup> m<sup>k</sup> ln 88 δε<sup>k</sup> < εk+2 < PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), the "max" on the right hand side of [\(22\)](#page-41-4) cannot be achieved by the second term (as it is smaller than the quantity on the left hand side), so it must be achieved by the first term. Therefore, PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 2Pˆ S<sup>4</sup> k (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = ˆpk. ■

Using Lemma [13](#page-41-0) to bound the size of the data set S 2 k (which is based on pˆk), we are now ready to establish a concentration inequality for the error rates in the ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> region in the following lemma.

Lemma 14. *There is an event* E<sup>2</sup> *of probability at least* 1 − δ 4 *such that, on the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*,* ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , K}*,*

$$\sup_{h \in \mathbb{C}} \left| \hat{P}_{S_k^2}(\text{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_{i_k}) \right| \leq \frac{\varepsilon_k}{\sqrt{C''}}. \quad (24)$$

Proof. Consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having non-zero probability of k ≤ K. Supposing k ≤ K occurs, define a collection of sets A′ k := {ER(h) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> : h ∈ <sup>C</sup>}. Note that VC(A′ k ) ≤ d (which is immediate from the definition of VC dimension). We aim to apply Lemma [8](#page-35-1) of Appendix [D,](#page-34-0) a refinement of the uniform convergence bound of [Talagrand](#page-15-6) [\(1994\)](#page-15-6), which accounts for an *envelope* set D ⊇ S A′ k ; specifically, we instantiate the various sets and variables in Lemma [8](#page-35-1) to be Z = X × {0, 1}, n = m′ k , A = A′ k , envelope set D = ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , data set Z = S k , and confidence parameter δ/(4(3+N −k) 2 ), and we apply the lemma under the conditional distribution given the event k ≤ K and given the random variables ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and m′ k . Since the event that k ≤ K, and the random variables ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and m′ k , are all completely determined by examples allocated to data sets *before* allocating examples to the data set S 2 k , we may note that the m′ k examples comprising S 2 k are conditionally independent P-distributed random variables given the event that k ≤ K and given the random variables ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and m′ k . Thus, applying Lemma [8](#page-35-1) of Appendix [D](#page-34-0) under the conditional distribution given the event that k ≤ K and given the random variables ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and m′ k , together with the law of total probability, we have that on an event E2,k of probability at least 1 − δ 4(3+N−k) <sup>2</sup> , if k ≤ K and PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≥ 9 m′ k ln 16(3+N−k) δ , then

$$\sup_{h \in \mathbb{C}} \left| \hat{P}_{S_k^2}(\text{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_{i_k}) \right| \leq c_1 \sqrt{\frac{P_X(\Delta_{i_k})}{m'_k}} \left( \text{d} + \log \left( \frac{4(3+N-k)^2}{\delta} \right) \right). \quad (25)$$

For simplicity, for any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having zero probability of k ≤ K, let E2,k denote the event of probability one that k > K. Finally, define E<sup>2</sup> = T<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 E2,k, and note that, by the union bound, E<sup>2</sup> holds with probability at least 1 − P<sup>N</sup> +1 k=1 δ 4(3+N−k) <sup>2</sup> ≥ 1 − δ 4 P<sup>∞</sup> j=2 1 j <sup>2</sup> ≥ 1 − δ 4 .

Now suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩E<sup>1</sup> ∩E<sup>2</sup> occurs, and consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , K}. If ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> = ∅ then [\(24\)](#page-41-5) holds trivially since the left hand side of [\(24\)](#page-41-5) is then zero. To address the remaining case, suppose ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ̸= ∅. By the final claim in Lemma [11,](#page-40-2) we have PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > εk+2. Moreover, by Lemma [13](#page-41-0) we have pˆ<sup>k</sup> ≥ PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). Recalling m′ k := l C ′′c <sup>1</sup>pˆ<sup>k</sup> ε k <sup>d</sup> + log 4(3+N−k) δ m, these imply

$$m'_k > \frac{C'' c_1^2}{C' \varepsilon_{k+2}} \ln \left( \frac{4(3+N-k)^2}{\delta} \right) > \frac{9}{\varepsilon_{k+2}} \ln \left( \frac{16(3+N-k)^2}{\delta} \right),$$

where the last inequality follows from c<sup>1</sup> ≥ 1 and C ′′ ≥ 18C 4 . Thus, <sup>9</sup> m′ k ln 16(3+N−k) 2 δ < εk+2 < PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). By the definition of E2, it follows that [\(25\)](#page-42-1) holds. Moreover, since pˆ<sup>k</sup> ≥ PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), we have that m′ <sup>k</sup> ≥ C ′′c <sup>1</sup>PX(∆ik ) ε 2 k <sup>d</sup> + log 4(3+N−k) 2 δ , so that the right hand side of [\(25\)](#page-42-1) is at most √<sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> <sup>C</sup>′′ , thus establishing [\(24\)](#page-41-5). ■

Combining the concentration inequality from Lemma [14](#page-41-1) with [\(14\)](#page-38-5) of Lemma [10](#page-38-0) together with [\(20\)](#page-40-1) of Lemma [12](#page-40-3) yields a concentration inequality for the differences ˆer1,2 k (h) − ˆer1,2 k (h ′ ) among h, h′ ∈ Vk−1, recalling the definition from [\(1\)](#page-6-7):

$$\hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(h) := \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h) \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + \hat{P}_{S_k^2}(\text{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_{i_k}).$$

In fact, the implication is stronger than this, admitting functions h, h′ ∈ V (4) k−1 . In particular, for any k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, note that V (4) k−1 in [\(2\)](#page-6-8) can equivalently be defined as

$$V_{k-1}^{(4)} = \left\{ h_1 \mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}} + h_2 \mathbb{1}_{\Delta_{i_k}} : h_1 \in V_{k-1}^{(3)}, h_2 \in \mathbb{C} \right\}.$$

The following lemma provides a concentration inequality for ˆer1,2 k (h) − ˆer1,2 k (h ′ ) among functions h, h′ ∈ V (4) k−1 .

Lemma 15. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*, for every* k ∈ {1, . . . , K} *we have*

$$\sup_{h, h' \in V_{k-1}^{(4)}} \left| (\hat{\mathbf{e}}_k^{l,2}(h) - \hat{\mathbf{e}}_k^{l,2}(h')) - (\mathbf{e}_P(h) - \mathbf{e}_P(h')) \right| \leq \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'}, \quad (26)$$

*recalling that* C ′ := √ C′′ <sup>16</sup> *. Moreover,* [\(26\)](#page-42-2) *implies*

$$\operatorname{er}_P(\hat{h}_k) \leq \inf_{h \in V_{k-1}^{(4)}} \operatorname{er}_P(h) + \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'}. \quad (27)$$

Proof. Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> holds and consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , K}. Note that for any h = h1**1**X \∆ik + h2**1**∆ik ∈ V (4) <sup>k</sup>−<sup>1</sup> we have er<sup>P</sup> (h) = P(ER(h1) \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) + P(ER(h2) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) and ˆer1,2 k (h) = Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(h1) ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) + Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> k (ER(h2) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ).

Consider any h1, h′ <sup>1</sup> ∈ V (3) k−1 and any h2, h′ <sup>2</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup>, and let <sup>h</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>h</sup>1**1**X \∆ik + h2**1**∆ik and h ′ = h ′ <sup>1</sup>**1**X \∆ik + h ′ <sup>2</sup>**1**∆ik . By Lemma [14,](#page-41-1) we have ∀h ′′ <sup>2</sup> ∈ {h2, h′ <sup>2</sup>},

$$\left| \hat{P}_{S_k^2}(\text{ER}(h_2'') \cap \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h_2'') \cap \Delta_{i_k}) \right| \leq \frac{\varepsilon_k}{\sqrt{C''}}.$$

Additionally, since [\(20\)](#page-40-1) of Lemma [12](#page-40-3) implies PX({h<sup>1</sup> ̸= h ′ <sup>1</sup>} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 3εk, the inequality [\(14\)](#page-38-5) of Lemma [10](#page-38-0) implies

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h_1) \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\text{ER}(h'_1) \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - (P(\text{ER}(h_1) \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h'_1) \setminus \Delta_{i_k})) \right| \\ & < \sqrt{\frac{3\varepsilon_k^2}{C''}} + \frac{\varepsilon_k}{C''} \leq \frac{2\varepsilon_k}{\sqrt{C''}}, \end{aligned} \quad (28)$$

where the last inequality follows from C ′′ ≥ 14. Combining these with the triangle inequality (namely, |((ˆa +ˆb) − (ˆa ′ +ˆb ′ )) − ((a + b) − (a ′ + b ′ ))| ≤ |(ˆa − aˆ ′ ) − (a − a ′ )| + | ˆb − b| + | ˆb ′ − b ′ |) yields that

$$|(\hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(h) - \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(h')) - (\text{er}_P(h) - \text{er}_P(h'))| < \frac{4\varepsilon_k}{\sqrt{C''}} = \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'}.$$

To see that [\(26\)](#page-42-2) implies [\(27\)](#page-42-3), note that, by the definition of hˆ <sup>k</sup> in [\(3\)](#page-6-9), h = hˆ <sup>k</sup> has minimal ˆer1,2 k (h) among all h ∈ V (4) k−1 : that is, ∀h ∈ V (4) k−1 , ˆer1,2 k hˆ k − ˆer1,2 k h ≤ 0. Together with [\(26\)](#page-42-2), this implies that ∀h ∈ V (4) k−1 , er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k − er<sup>P</sup> h ≤ ˆer1,2 k hˆ k − ˆer1,2 k h + ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ . ■

In particular, Lemma [15](#page-42-0) immediately implies the following lemma concerning the quality of the functions h in Vk.

Lemma 16. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*,* ∀k ∈ K*,* ∀h ∈ Vk−1*, the following implications hold:*

$$h \in V_k \implies \operatorname{er}_P(h) \leq \operatorname{er}_P(\hat{h}_k) + \frac{5\varepsilon_k}{4C'}, \quad (29)$$

$$\text{er}_P(h) \leq \text{er}_P(\hat{h}_k) + \frac{3\varepsilon_k}{4C'} \implies h \in V_k. \quad (30)$$

Proof. Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> occurs and consider any k ∈ K and h ∈ Vk−1. In particular, note that we also have h ∈ V (4) k−1 , since letting f = g = h, we have h = f**1**{f=g} +h**1**{f̸=g} ∈ V (3) k−1 , and thus h = h**1**X \∆ik + h**1**∆ik ∈ V (4) k−1 .

If h ∈ Vk, then by definition of V<sup>k</sup> in Step [3](#page-6-1) we have ˆer1,2 k h − ˆer1,2 k hˆ k ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> C′ . Together with [\(26\)](#page-42-2) of Lemma [15,](#page-42-0) this implies er<sup>P</sup> h − er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>C</sup>′ + ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ = 5ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ , which establishes [\(29\)](#page-43-2).

On the other hand, if er<sup>P</sup> h − er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k ≤ 3ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ , then [\(26\)](#page-42-2) of Lemma [15](#page-42-0) implies ˆer1,2 k h − ˆer1,2 k hˆ k ≤ 3ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ + ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ = ε<sup>k</sup> C′ . Thus, any such h is retained in Vk, which establishes [\(30\)](#page-43-3). ■

The main implication of Lemma [16](#page-43-0) pertains to the early stopping case in Step [4,](#page-6-5) which we turn to next. Recall h <sup>⋆</sup> denotes an (arbitrary) concept in <sup>C</sup> with er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) < infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + <sup>ε</sup> <sup>10</sup><sup>4</sup> . In the following lemma, in addition to arguing that the predictor hˆ returned in Step [4](#page-6-5) has low excess error rate compared to h ⋆ (in fact, *negative*), this lemma also reveals a second major role of this early stopping case: it ensures that on all rounds k in which the algorithm does *not* terminate in Step [4,](#page-6-5) we retain h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk.

Lemma 17. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*, the following implications hold for every* k ∈ K*:*

- *If* Aavid *does* not *return in Step [4](#page-6-5) on round* k*, then* h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk*.*
- *If* Aavid *returns in Step [4](#page-6-5) on round* k*, then* er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k < er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ *.*

Proof. Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> occurs. We will prove the first claim by induction on k. As a base case, we trivially have h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> = V0. Now, for the purpose of induction, let k ∈ K be such that h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−1. Also note (as discussed in the proof of Lemma [16\)](#page-43-0) that this also implies h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V (4) k−1 . If h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈/ Vk, then by [\(30\)](#page-43-3) of Lemma [16,](#page-43-0) we have er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ − er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k > 3ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ . In particular, this implies that if V<sup>k</sup> ̸= ∅, then together with [\(26\)](#page-42-2) of Lemma [15,](#page-42-0) we have

$$\begin{aligned} \min_{h \in V_k} \hat{e}_k^{1,2}(h) - \hat{e}_k^{1,2}(\hat{h}_k) &\geq \min_{h \in V_{k-1}} \hat{e}_k^{1,2}(h) - \hat{e}_k^{1,2}(\hat{h}_k) \geq \min_{h \in V_{k-1}} er_P(h) - er_P(\hat{h}_k) - \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'} \\ &> er_P(h^*) - er_P(\hat{h}_k) - \frac{\varepsilon}{10^4} - \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'} > \frac{3\varepsilon_k}{4C'} - \frac{\varepsilon}{10^4} - \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'} > \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'}, \end{aligned}$$

where the last inequality follows from <sup>ε</sup> <sup>10</sup><sup>4</sup> < 2ε<sup>N</sup> <sup>10</sup><sup>4</sup> ≤ ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ . Thus, either V<sup>k</sup> = ∅ or minh∈V<sup>k</sup> ˆer1,2 k h − ˆer1,2 k hˆ k > ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ , so that either way the algorithm will return in Step [4](#page-6-5) in this case. Therefore, if the algorithm does *not* return in Step [4](#page-6-5) on round k, it must be that h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk. This completes the proof of the first claim, by the principle of induction.

Finally, we turn to the second claim. Suppose, for some k ∈ K, the algorithm returns in Step [4](#page-6-5) on round k. In particular, either k = 1, in which case h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> = Vk−1, or k > 1, in which case (since the algorithm did not return in Step [4](#page-6-5) on round k − 1) the first claim in the lemma implies h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−1. Again note that this also implies h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V (4) k−1 . If h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈/ Vk, then [\(30\)](#page-43-3) of Lemma [16](#page-43-0) implies er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ > er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k + 3ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ . Otherwise, if h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk, the condition in Step [4](#page-6-5) implies ˆer1,2 k h ⋆ − ˆer1,2 k hˆ k > ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ . Together with [\(26\)](#page-42-2) of Lemma [15,](#page-42-0) this implies

$$\text{er}_P(h^*) - \text{er}_P(\hat{h}_k) \geq \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(h^*) - \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(\hat{h}_k) - \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'} > 0.$$

Thus, in either case, we have er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ > er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k , which establishes the second claim. ■

Lemmas [15,](#page-42-0) [16,](#page-43-0) and [17](#page-43-1) together have a particularly nice implication, which, although not strictly needed for the proof of Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) is worth noting (and will be useful in Appendix [F\)](#page-51-0). Specifically, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 18. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*,* ∀k ∈ K*,*

$$V_k \subseteq \left\{ h \in \mathbb{C} : \operatorname{er}_P(h) - \operatorname{er}_P(h^*) \leq \frac{3\varepsilon_k}{2C'} \right\}.$$

Proof. Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> occurs and consider any k ∈ K. Since k − 1 < K, Lemma [17](#page-43-1) implies h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> in the case k ≥ 2, while the case k = 1 has h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V<sup>0</sup> by definition of V<sup>0</sup> = C. Together with [\(27\)](#page-42-3) of Lemma [15,](#page-42-0) this implies er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k ≤ er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ + ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ . Combined with [\(29\)](#page-43-2) of Lemma [16,](#page-43-0) we have that every h ∈ V<sup>k</sup> satisfies er<sup>P</sup> h ≤ er<sup>P</sup> hˆ k + 5ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ ≤ er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ + 3ε<sup>k</sup> 2C′ . ■

At this point, we may note that Lemmas [15](#page-42-0) and [17](#page-43-1) together completely address the error guarantee for the hˆ returned by <sup>A</sup>avid, on the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2. as summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 19. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*,* <sup>A</sup>avid *eventually terminates, and the function* hˆ *it returns satisfies* er<sup>P</sup> hˆ ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> h + ε*.*

Proof. Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> occurs. If the algorithm terminates in Step [4](#page-6-5) in some round k ∈ K, by definition we have hˆ = hˆ <sup>k</sup>, and thus Lemma [17](#page-43-1) implies er<sup>P</sup> hˆ < er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ < infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> h + ε <sup>10</sup><sup>4</sup> . On the other hand, if the algorithm does not return in Step [4](#page-6-5) on any round k ∈ K, then we have K = N + 1 (recalling Lemma [10](#page-38-0) implies the algorithm eventually terminates), so that by definition hˆ = hˆ<sup>N</sup> +1. Since in this case Lemma [17](#page-43-1) implies h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V<sup>N</sup> (and hence h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V (4) <sup>N</sup> ), [\(27\)](#page-42-3) of Lemma [15](#page-42-0) implies er<sup>P</sup> hˆ = er<sup>P</sup> <sup>h</sup>ˆ<sup>N</sup> +1 ≤ er<sup>P</sup> h ⋆ + ε<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>4</sup>C′ < infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> h + ε <sup>10</sup><sup>4</sup> + ε <sup>8</sup>C′ < infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> h + ε. ■

With the analysis of error guarantees complete, we turn now to establishing the bound Q(ε, δ; β) on the number of queries, as claimed in Theorem [5.](#page-33-0) This will be comprised of two main parts. First, we

argue that the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> never grows too large: specifically, recalling β := infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h), Lemma [20](#page-45-0) will establish that PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = O(β), which in turn allows us to bound the number of queries in S <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> on each round (in the proof of Lemma [23\)](#page-47-0). Second, in the proof of Lemma [22,](#page-46-0) we bound PX(Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ sεk, by reasoning in terms of the disagreement coefficient [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b\)](#page-13-0), relating the latter to the star number via a result of [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2). This in turn allows us to bound the number of queries in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> on each round (in the proof of Lemma [23\)](#page-47-0).

We begin with the first of these parts, stated in the following lemma. We remark that this lemma plays a special role in constraining the allowed values of the constant C, as the argument breaks down if C is taken too large. On the other hand, the proof also reveals that it is possible to decrease the factor "5" in this lemma to *any* value c > 2 by taking C > 1 appropriately close to 1 and by an appropriately large choice of the constant C ′′ (and hence C ′ ). See footnote [11](#page-33-3) for further discussion.

Lemma 20. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*, for all* k ∈ {1, . . . , K} *and* i ∈ Ik*,*

$$P_X(\Delta_i) \leq 5 \inf_{h \in \mathbb{C}} P(\text{ER}(h) \cap \Delta_i) \leq 5\beta.$$

Proof. We will argue that, on E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2, for any h<sup>0</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup>, each region ∆i+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup> (defined in Step [7\)](#page-6-10) satisfies

$$P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i) > \left(1 - \frac{C^3}{2} - \frac{5C^3}{4C^3}\right) P_X(\Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i) = \frac{1}{5} P_X(\Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i), \quad (31)$$

so that each addition to ∆<sup>i</sup> "chops off" a piece of ER(h0) of measure proportional to the increase in measure PX(∆i+1)−PX(∆i) = PX(∆i+1 \∆i). The claim in the lemma then follows immediately from [\(31\)](#page-45-1), since it holds trivially for i = 0 (recalling ∆<sup>0</sup> = ∅), and if any k ∈ {1, . . . , K} and i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> has i ≥ 1, then applying [\(31\)](#page-45-1) inductively yields

$$P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \Delta_i) = \sum_{j=0}^{i-1} P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \Delta_{j+1} \setminus \Delta_j) > \sum_{j=0}^{i-1} \frac{1}{5} P_X(\Delta_{j+1} \setminus \Delta_j) = \frac{1}{5} P_X(\Delta_i).$$

Taking the infimum over all h<sup>0</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> then implies the lemma.

We proceed now with the formal proof of [\(31\)](#page-45-1). Suppose the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> occurs, and for the purpose of analyzing the *increases* ∆i+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup> of the ∆<sup>i</sup> set (which only occur in Step [7\)](#page-6-10), consider any k ∈ K and any i ∈ I<sup>k</sup> with i < max I<sup>k</sup> (equivalently, the algorithm reaches Step [7](#page-6-10) with this (k, i)). Let (f, g) be as defined in Step [6](#page-6-6) for this (k, i) so that ∆i+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup> = {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup> .

Note that {f ̸= g} × {0, 1} = (ER(f) ∩ {f ̸= g}) ∪ (ER(g) ∩ {f ̸= g}), so that (lower-bounding 'max' by 'average')

$$\begin{aligned} & \max_{f' \in \{f, g\}} P(\text{ER}(f') \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \\ & \geq \frac{1}{2} P(\text{ER}(f) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + \frac{1}{2} P(\text{ER}(g) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \geq \frac{1}{2} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}), \end{aligned}$$

where in fact the last inequality holds with equality (since, for {0, 1} labels, (ER(f) ∩ {f ̸= g}) and (ER(g) ∩ {f ̸= g}) are disjoint). Thus, ∃f ′ ∈ {f, g} with

$$P(\text{ER}(f') \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \geq \frac{1}{2} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}).$$

Let h ′ = f ′**1**{f=g}\∆ik + h0**1**{f̸=g}\∆ik + f ′**1**∆ik and note that h ′ ∈ V (4) k−1 . Also recall that ˆer1,2 k hˆ k = minh∈<sup>V</sup> (4) k−1 ˆer1,2 k h , and hence ˆer1,2 k h ′ ≥ ˆer1,2 k hˆ k . Since we also have f ′ ∈ V (4) k−1 (as discussed in the proof of Lemma [16\)](#page-43-0), Lemma [15](#page-42-0) implies

$$\text{er}_P(f') - \text{er}_P(h') \leq \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(f') - \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(h') + \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'} \leq \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(f') - \hat{\text{er}}_k^{1,2}(\hat{h}_k) + \frac{\varepsilon_k}{4C'} \leq \frac{5\varepsilon_k}{4C'}, \quad (32)$$

where the last inequality is due to f ′ ∈ {f, g} ⊆ Vk, recalling the definition of V<sup>k</sup> in Step [3.](#page-6-1)

Moreover, by definition of f ′ and h ′ , we have

$$\begin{aligned} \text{er}_P(f') - \text{er}_P(h') &= P(\text{ER}(f') \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \\ &\geq \frac{1}{2} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}). \end{aligned}$$

Equivalently: P(ER(h0) ∩ {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≥ 1 2 PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) − er<sup>P</sup> f ′ − er<sup>P</sup> h ′ . Combining this with [\(32\)](#page-45-2), we conclude that

$$P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \geq \frac{1}{2} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \frac{5\varepsilon_k}{4C'}. \quad (33)$$

Also note that, since ∆<sup>i</sup> ⊇ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , we have

$$\Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i = \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i = (\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \setminus (\{f \neq g\} \cap \Delta_i \setminus \Delta_{i_k}),$$

so that

$$P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i) \geq P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i,k}) - P_X(\{f \neq g\} \cap \Delta_i \setminus \Delta_{i,k}). \quad (34)$$

Moreover, again since ∆<sup>i</sup> ⊇ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , we have

$$P_X(\{f \neq g\} \cap \Delta_i \setminus \Delta_{ik}) = P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{ik}) - P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i). \quad (35)$$

Combining [\(34\)](#page-46-1), [\(35\)](#page-46-2), and [\(33\)](#page-46-3) yields that

$$\begin{aligned} & P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i) \\ & \geq P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) \\ & \geq P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i) - \frac{1}{2} P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) - \frac{5\varepsilon_k}{4C'}. \end{aligned} \quad (36)$$

Lemma [12](#page-40-3) and the fact that f, g ∈ V<sup>k</sup> ⊆ Vk−<sup>1</sup> imply PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ εk, and Lemma [11](#page-40-2) implies PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+3. Together, we have

$$P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) < C^3 P_X(\{f \neq g\} \setminus \Delta_i).$$

Additionally, again since PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+3, we have that <sup>5</sup>ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>4</sup>C′ < 5C <sup>4</sup>C′ PX({f ̸= g} \ ∆i). Combining these inequalities with [\(36\)](#page-46-4), and recalling ∆i+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup> = {f ̸= g} \ ∆<sup>i</sup> , yields that

$$P(\text{ER}(h_0) \cap \Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i) > \left(1 - \frac{C^3}{2} - \frac{5C^3}{4C'}\right) P_X(\Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i).$$

Recalling that C ′ = √ C′′ <sup>16</sup> = 25C 3 <sup>16</sup>−10C<sup>3</sup> , we have <sup>C</sup> 3 <sup>2</sup> + 5C 3 <sup>4</sup>C′ = 4 5 , so that the right hand side above equals <sup>1</sup> 5 PX(∆i+1 \ ∆i), which establishes [\(31\)](#page-45-1). ■

Next we turn to the second part of the argument outlined above: bounding the number of queries in the sets S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> . We begin by stating a known fact, due to [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015,](#page-13-2) Theorem 10): namely, that the *disagreement coefficient* [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b\)](#page-13-0) is upper bounded by the *star number* [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2) (indeed, Theorem 10 of [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015](#page-13-2) shows the relation is even *sharp* in the worst case over h and distributions P ′ <sup>X</sup>).

Lemma 21 [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). *For any measurable* h : X → {0, 1}*, any distribution* P ′ <sup>X</sup> *on* X *, and any* r > 0*, defining the* r-ball centered at h *as* B<sup>P</sup> ′ X (h, r) :={h ′ ∈<sup>C</sup> : P ′ <sup>X</sup>(h ′ ̸= h) ≤ r}*, it holds that*

$$P'_X(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P'_X}(h, r))) \leq \mathfrak{s}r.$$

Toward bounding the number of queries in the sets S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> in the algorithm, the following lemma establishes a bound on PX(Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) by a straightforward application of Lemma [21](#page-46-5) to the *conditional* probabilities PX(Dk−1|X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), in combination with a diameter bound supplied by [\(19\)](#page-40-0) of Lemma [12.](#page-40-3)

Lemma 22. *On the event* E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2*, for every* k ∈ {1, . . . , K}*,* PX(Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ sεk*.*

Proof. Suppose the event E0∩E1∩E<sup>2</sup> holds and consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , K}. If PX(X \∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = 0, we trivially have that PX(Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = 0 ≤ sεk. To address the remaining case, suppose PX(X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > 0, and denote by P<sup>k</sup> := PX(·|X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). By [\(19\)](#page-40-0) of Lemma [12](#page-40-3) we have

$$\sup_{f, g \in V_{k-1}} P_k(f \neq g) \leq \frac{\varepsilon_k}{P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k})}.$$

In particular, since k − 1 < K, Lemma [17](#page-43-1) implies h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> in the case k ≥ 2, while the case k = 1 has h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ V<sup>0</sup> by definition of V<sup>0</sup> = <sup>C</sup>. Thus, the above inequality implies

$$V_{k-1} \subseteq \mathbb{B}_{P_k} \left( h^\star, \frac{\varepsilon_k}{P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k})} \right). \quad (37)$$

Together with Lemma [21,](#page-46-5) this implies

$$P_k(D_{k-1}) = P_k(\text{DIS}(V_{k-1})) \leq P_k\left(\text{DIS}\left(B_{P_k}\left(h^*, \frac{\varepsilon_k}{P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k})}\right)\right)\right) \leq \mathfrak{s} \frac{\varepsilon_k}{P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k})}. \quad (38)$$

We therefore have that

$$P_X(D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) = P_k(D_{k-1})P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \leq \mathfrak{s}\varepsilon_k.$$

■

We are now ready to state a lemma bounding the total number of queries in the algorithm, by a combination of Lemmas [13,](#page-41-0) [20,](#page-45-0) and [22](#page-46-0) together with a multiplicative Chernoff bound argument. For convenience, this lemma also supplies an upper bound on the sizes m′ k of the data sets S 2 k , which will be of further use when establishing the bound M(ε, δ; β) on the total number of unlabeled examples sufficient for the execution of the algorithm (Lemma [24](#page-49-0) below). Specifically, in the following lemma, for any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1}, denote by

$$\bar{m}'_k := \frac{25C''c_1^2\beta}{\varepsilon_k^2} \left( d + \log\left(\frac{4(3+N-k)^2}{\delta}\right) \right),$$

$$\text{and } M_2 := \frac{700C''c_1^2\beta}{\varepsilon^2} \left( d + \log\left(\frac{4e^4}{\delta}\right) \right).$$

Lemma 23. *There is an event* E<sup>3</sup> *of probability at least* 1 − δ 4 *, such that on* T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> *,* ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , K}*, the following claims hold:* m′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ <sup>m</sup>′ k *,*

$$|S_k^1 \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}| \leq 3\mathfrak{s}\varepsilon_k m_k, \quad (39)$$

$$|S_k^2 \cap \Delta_{i_k}| \leq 2P_X(\Delta_{i_k})m'_k \leq 10\beta\bar{m}'_k. \quad (40)$$

*Moreover, we have* P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 <sup>m</sup>′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ M2*, and the total number of queries by* <sup>A</sup>avid *is at most* Q(ε, δ; β)*, where*

$$\begin{aligned} Q(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta) &:= 10\beta M_2 + \min\{M_1, (3/2)\mathfrak{s}\varepsilon(N + 1)m_{N+1}\} \\ &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathfrak{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \min\left\{ \mathfrak{s} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right\} \left( \mathfrak{d} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right). \quad (41) \end{aligned}$$

Proof. By Lemma [13,](#page-41-0) on E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2, ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ pˆ<sup>k</sup> ≤ 4PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). Recall the definition of m′ k := l C ′′c 2 <sup>1</sup>pˆ<sup>k</sup> ε k <sup>d</sup> + log 4(3+N−k) δ m. Thus, if <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>∅</sup>, we have <sup>p</sup>ˆ<sup>k</sup> = 0, hence m′ <sup>k</sup> = 0, and the implication <sup>m</sup>′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ <sup>m</sup>′ k trivially follows. Otherwise, on E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2, if ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ̸= ∅, the final claim in Lemma [11](#page-40-2) implies PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > εk+2, so that together m′ k is at most

$$\left\lceil \frac{C'' c_1^2 4 P_X (\Delta_{i_k})}{\varepsilon_k^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log \left( \frac{4(3+N-k)^2}{\delta} \right) \right) \right\rceil \leq \frac{5 C'' c_1^2 P_X (\Delta_{i_k})}{\varepsilon_k^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log \left( \frac{4(3+N-k)^2}{\delta} \right) \right).$$

Since Lemma [20](#page-45-0) implies PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 5β on E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2, we conclude that m′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ <sup>m</sup>′ k .

We next turn to establishing [\(39\)](#page-47-1). Consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having non-zero probability that k ≤ K. Given that k ≤ K, note that Vk−<sup>1</sup> and ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> have no dependence on S 1 k , so that the samples in S 1 k are conditionally i.i.d.-P given the event that k ≤ K and given the random variables Vk−<sup>1</sup> and ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> . Therefore, applying a multiplicative Chernoff bound (Lemma [6](#page-34-1) of Appendix [D\)](#page-34-0) under the conditional distribution given the event k ≤ K and the random variables Vk−<sup>1</sup> and ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , with conditional probability at least 1 − δ <sup>8</sup>k(k+1) ,

$$|S_k^1 \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}| \leq 2m_k P_X(D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) + 6 \ln\left(\frac{16k(k+1)}{\delta}\right). \quad (42)$$

In particular, by the law of total probability, this implies that for every k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1}, with probability at least 1− δ <sup>8</sup>k(k+1) , if <sup>k</sup> <sup>≤</sup> <sup>K</sup> then [\(42\)](#page-47-2) holds. Letting <sup>E</sup>′ <sup>3</sup> denote the event that [\(42\)](#page-47-2) holds for every k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, by the union bound, E′ <sup>3</sup> holds with probability at least 1 − δ 8 . Combining [\(42\)](#page-47-2) with Lemma [22,](#page-46-0) we have that on the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E<sup>2</sup> ∩ E′ 3 , ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , K},

$$|S_k^1 \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}| \leq 2\mathfrak{s}\varepsilon_k m_k + 6 \ln\left(\frac{16k(k+1)}{\delta}\right) \leq 3\mathfrak{s}\varepsilon_k m_k,$$

where the rightmost inequality follows from recalling <sup>m</sup><sup>k</sup> := l 300C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>d</sup> log C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> + log 1 δ m, which satisfies <sup>ε</sup>km<sup>k</sup> <sup>≥</sup> 6 ln 16k(k+1) δ . Thus, we have established [\(39\)](#page-47-1).

We argue the left inequality in [\(40\)](#page-47-3) similarly. Consider any k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1} having non-zero probability of k ≤ K. Given k ≤ K, note that ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> has no dependence on S 2 k or m′ k , so that the m′ k samples in S 2 k are conditionally i.i.d.-P given the event k ≤ K and given the random variables ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and m′ k . Therefore, applying a multiplicative Chernoff bound (Lemma [6](#page-34-1) of Appendix [D\)](#page-34-0) under the conditional distribution given the event k ≤ K and the random variables ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> and m′ k , with conditional probability at least 1 − δ 8(3+N−k) 2 ,

$$|S_k^2 \cap \Delta_{i_k}| \leq \max \left\{ 2P_X(\Delta_{i_k}) m'_k, 6 \ln \left( \frac{16(3 + N - k)^2}{\delta} \right) \right\}. \quad (43)$$

By the law of total probability, we have that for every k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1}, with probability at least 1 − δ 8(3+N−k) <sup>2</sup> , if k ≤ K then [\(43\)](#page-48-0) holds. Letting E′′ <sup>3</sup> denote the event that [\(43\)](#page-48-0) holds for every k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, by the union bound, E′′ <sup>3</sup> holds with probability at least 1− P<sup>N</sup> +1 k=1 δ 8(3+N−k) <sup>2</sup> ≥ 1− δ 8 . Let E<sup>3</sup> = E′ <sup>3</sup> ∩ <sup>E</sup>′′ 3 , and note that, by the union bound, E<sup>3</sup> holds with probability at least 1 − δ 4 . For the remainder of the proof, let us suppose the event T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> occurs.

To arrive at the simpler claimed inequalities in [\(40\)](#page-47-3), we follow a similar argument to the final part of the proof of Lemma [14.](#page-41-1) Explicitly, we first note that for any k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, if ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> = ∅, we trivially have |S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> | = 0 = 2PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> )m′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ <sup>10</sup>βm′ k . On the other hand, if ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ̸= ∅, the final claim in Lemma [11](#page-40-2) implies PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > εk+2, and combined with Lemma [13](#page-41-0) this further implies pˆ<sup>k</sup> ≥ PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) > εk+2. Therefore, in this case,

$$2P_X(\Delta_{i_k})m'_k > \frac{2C''c_1^2}{C^4} \left( d + \ln\left(\frac{4(3+N-k)^2}{\delta}\right) \right) \geq 6 \ln\left(\frac{16(3+N-k)^2}{\delta}\right),$$

where the rightmost inequality follows from c<sup>1</sup> ≥ 1 and C ′′ ≥ 6C 4 . Thus, the left inequality in [\(40\)](#page-47-3) follows from [\(43\)](#page-48-0). The right inequality in [\(40\)](#page-47-3) follows immediately from the fact (established above) that m′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ <sup>m</sup>′ k , together with the fact (from Lemma [20\)](#page-45-0) that PX(∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 5β.

The remaining claims in the lemma follow from reasoning about convergence of the relevant series. Specifically, recalling that ε<sup>k</sup> = C 1−k , N = log<sup>C</sup> 2 ε , and C = <sup>10</sup> , we note that P<sup>N</sup> +1 k=1 ε k = 1 C<sup>2</sup>−1 C 2(N+1) − 1 ≤ 28 ε <sup>2</sup> and

$$\sum_{k=1}^{N+1} \frac{1}{\varepsilon_k^2} \ln(3 + N - k) = C^{2N} \sum_{j=0}^N C^{-2j} \ln(2 + j) \leq C^{2N} \cdot 10 \leq \frac{49}{\varepsilon^2}.$$

Recalling m′ <sup>k</sup> = 25C ′′c <sup>1</sup>β ε k d + 2 ln(3 + N − k) + ln 4 δ , we have

$$\sum_{k=1}^{N+1} \bar{m}'_k \leq \frac{25C'' c_1^2 \beta}{\varepsilon^2} \left( 28d + 2 \cdot 49 + 28 \ln\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right) \right) \leq M_2. \quad (44)$$

To obtain the query bound Q(ε, δ; β) in [\(41\)](#page-47-4), note that the total number of queries is precisely

$$\left( \sum_{k=1}^K |S_k^2 \cap \Delta_{i_k}| \right) + \left( \sum_{k=1}^K |S_k^1 \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}| \right). \quad (45)$$

By [\(40\)](#page-47-3), the first term in [\(45\)](#page-48-1) is upper bounded by 10β · P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 <sup>m</sup>′ k , and [\(44\)](#page-48-2) implies this is at most 10βM2. The second term in [\(45\)](#page-48-1) is trivially upper bounded by M<sup>1</sup> := P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 mk. Moreover, noting that εkm<sup>k</sup> is increasing in k, [\(39\)](#page-47-1) implies the second term in [\(45\)](#page-48-1) is also upper bounded by 3sεN +1 · mN +1 · (N + 1) ≤ (3/2)sε(N + 1)mN +1. Together with the definition of Q(ε, δ; β) from [\(41\)](#page-47-4), we have that the total number of queries [\(45\)](#page-48-1) is at most Q(ε, δ; β).

The bound on the asymptotic form of Q(ε, δ; β) in [\(41\)](#page-47-4) follows immediately from the definitions. Specifically, by definition of M2, we have 10βM<sup>2</sup> = O β 2 ε d + log 1 δ . Moreover, since εN +1 ≥ ε 2C , we have (3/2)sε(N + 1)mN +1 = O s log 1 ε d log 1 ε + log 1 δ , while (since each k ≤ N + 1 has ε<sup>k</sup> ≥ ε 2C ), M<sup>1</sup> = P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 m<sup>k</sup> ≤ P<sup>N</sup> +1 k=1 301C ′′c<sup>0</sup> ε<sup>k</sup> <sup>d</sup> log 2CC′′c<sup>0</sup> ε + log δ = O 1 ε d log 1 ε + log 1 δ by evaluating the geometric series. ■

As a final step before composing these lemmas into a proof of Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) we state an explicit bound on the number of unlabeled examples used by the algorithm. Much of this analysis is already implied by the above lemmas: namely, by definition, the number of examples allocated to data sets S k and S 4 k is precisely 2M<sup>1</sup> = 2P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 mk, and Lemma [23](#page-47-0) implies the number of examples allocated to data sets S 2 k is at most M2. What remains is to bound the number of examples allocated to the data sets S 3 k,i, which hinges on bounding the number of iterations of the '*While*' loop for each k. We have already noted, in Lemma [10,](#page-38-0) that max I<sup>k</sup> ≤ 1 εk+3 on the event E0, which already suffices to establish a coarse bound O˜ d ε . However, we will need a slight refinement to obtain the claimed upper bound, which will follow from a combination of Lemmas [11](#page-40-2) and [20.](#page-45-0)

Lemma 24. *On the event* T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> *, the total number of examples allocated to data sets* S 1 k *,* S 4 k *(*k ≤ N + 1*),* S 2 k *(*k ≤ K*), and* S 3 k,i *(*k ∈ K*,* i ∈ Ik*) is at most*

$$M(\varepsilon, \delta; \beta) := 3M_1 + M_2 + \frac{100C^4\beta m_N}{\varepsilon} = O\left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon^2} \left( d \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right)\right).$$

Proof. Suppose the event T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> occurs. By definition, the number of examples allocated to data sets S k and S 4 k P is m<sup>k</sup> each, for k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1}, so that the total number of such examples is N +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 <sup>2</sup>m<sup>k</sup> = 2M1. Also, by the first claim in Lemma [23,](#page-47-0) the number <sup>m</sup>′ k of examples allocated to each S 2 k data set (for k ∈ {1, . . . , K}) satisfies m′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ <sup>m</sup>′ k . Moreover, Lemma [23](#page-47-0) also establishes that P<sup>N</sup> +1 <sup>k</sup>=1 <sup>m</sup>′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ M2. Together, we have that the total number of examples allocated to data sets S 2 k is P<sup>K</sup> <sup>k</sup>=1 <sup>m</sup>′ <sup>k</sup> ≤ M2. Thus, to complete the proof of Lemma [24,](#page-49-0) it suffices to bound the total number of examples allocated to data sets S 3 k,i (k ∈ K, i ∈ Ik).

Toward this end, recall that for each k ∈ K, each S 3 k,i is of size mk, and is allocated if and when the algorithm reaches Step [5](#page-6-4) with values (k, i). Thus, if k = K (which, by the final claim in Lemma [10,](#page-38-0) occurs only if the algorithm returns in Step [4](#page-6-5) in round k), then *no* examples are allocated to any S 3 k,i sets in round k, whereas if k < K, then the number of S 3 k,i data sets allocated during round k is precisely the number of distinct values of i encountered in round k: that is, |Ik|. Moreover, note that since each time through the '*While*' loop increments i, each k ∈ K with k < K has |Ik| = ik+1 − i<sup>k</sup> + 1. It follows that the total number of examples allocated to data sets S 3 k,i in the algorithm is precisely P <sup>k</sup>∈K:k<K mk(ik+1 − i<sup>k</sup> + 1).

Next we upper bound ik+1 − i<sup>k</sup> for each k ∈ K with k < K. Specifically, for any such k, note that ∆<sup>i</sup>k+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> = S<sup>i</sup>k+1−<sup>1</sup> i=i<sup>k</sup> (∆i+1 \ ∆i), and by definition the sets ∆i+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup> are disjoint over i. Moreover, by Lemma [11](#page-40-2) and the definition of ∆i+1 in Step [7,](#page-6-10) any i ∈ {ik, . . . , ik+1 − 1} has PX(∆i+1 \ ∆i) > εk+3. Therefore,

$$P_X(\Delta_{i_{k+1}} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) = \sum_{i=i_k}^{i_{k+1}-1} P_X(\Delta_{i+1} \setminus \Delta_i) \geq (i_{k+1} - i_k)\varepsilon_{k+3}.$$

On the other hand, by Lemma [20,](#page-45-0) PX(∆<sup>i</sup>k+1 \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ PX(∆<sup>i</sup>k+1 ) ≤ 5β. Combining these inequalities, we conclude that (ik+1 − ik) ≤ 5C <sup>3</sup>β ε<sup>k</sup> . Combined with the facts that m<sup>k</sup> ≤ mN and

P <sup>k</sup>∈K:k<K m<sup>k</sup> ≤ M1, altogether we have

$$\sum_{k \in \mathcal{K}: k < K} m_k(i_{k+1} - i_k + 1) \leq M_1 + m_N \sum_{k=1}^N \frac{5C^3\beta}{\varepsilon_k} \leq M_1 + \frac{100C^4\beta m_N}{\varepsilon},$$

where the last inequality follows by evaluating the geometric series and recalling εN ≥ ε 2C . This completes the proof that the total number of examples allocated to data sets S 1 k , S 3 k,i, S k , S 4 k is at most M(ε, δ; β). The claimed asymptotic form of M(ε, δ; β) follows immediately from the definitions of the quantities involved: namely, by definition, 3M<sup>1</sup> = Θ ε d log ε + log δ , <sup>M</sup><sup>2</sup> = Θ β ε 2 d + log 1 δ , and (since εN ≥ ε 2C ) 100C <sup>4</sup>βm<sup>N</sup> <sup>ε</sup> = Θ β ε 2 d log 1 ε + log 1 δ . ■

We are now ready to combine the above lemmas into a complete proof of Theorem [5.](#page-33-0)

Proof of Theorem [5.](#page-33-0) By the union bound, the event T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> has probability at least 1 − δ. By Lemma [24,](#page-49-0) on T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> , <sup>A</sup>avid uses at most M(ε, δ; β) (as defined in the lemma) of the examples in the sequence; in particular, this means that if we were to run the algorithm with a finite sequence (X1, Y1), . . . ,(Xm, Ym), for any <sup>m</sup> ≥ <sup>M</sup>(ε, δ; β), then on the event T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> , the behavior of the algorithm (e.g., queries, returned hˆ) is identical to the *idealized* setting the above lemmas were established under (where there is an unlimited supply of examples), and hence the claims in the above lemmas remain valid. Thus, for any sample size m ≥ M(ε, δ; β), on the event T<sup>3</sup> <sup>j</sup>=0 E<sup>j</sup> , by Lemma [19](#page-44-0) we have er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε, and by Lemma [23](#page-47-0) the total number of queries is at most Q(ε, δ; β) as defined therein. ■

A remark on intersecting with Dk−<sup>1</sup> in ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> : We remark that Theorem [5](#page-33-0) remains valid if we restrict either (or both) h1, h<sup>2</sup> to be in Vk−<sup>1</sup> in the definition [\(2\)](#page-6-8) of V (4) k−1 . The entire proof remains valid (applying the same change to V (3) k−1 ), with the only exception being the first inequality in Lemma [20,](#page-45-0) which should then replace C by Vk−1.
