This change is of no consequence to the second inequality in the lemma since the proof of Lemma [17](#page-43-1) in fact implies that h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> holds simultaneously (on E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2) for *all* functions h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> satisfying [\(10\)](#page-36-1) (and hence infh∈Vk−<sup>1</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) = β). Moreover, with this restriction to require h<sup>2</sup> ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> in V (4) k−1 , we can extend the intersection with Dk−<sup>1</sup> to the ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> region: that is, instead of querying all of S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (in Step [2\)](#page-6-2) or S 2 <sup>N</sup> +1 ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>N</sup> +1 (in Step [9\)](#page-6-3), we can instead merely query the subset S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (in Step [2\)](#page-6-2) or S 2 <sup>N</sup> +1 ∩ DN ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>N</sup> +1 (in Step [9\)](#page-6-3). With this change, we must then also modify the definition of ˆer1,2 k (h) in [\(1\)](#page-6-7) to ˆer1,2 k (h) := Pˆ S<sup>1</sup> k (ER(h) ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) + Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> (ER(h) ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). The argument in Lemma [15](#page-42-0) extends to this modified definition of ˆer1,2 k (h), since (as in the proof of [\(14\)](#page-38-5) in Lemma [10\)](#page-38-0) we are only interested in error *differences*, which, for h, h′ ∈ Vk−1, satisfy Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> k (ER(h)∩Dk−1∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> )−Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> k (ER(h ′ )∩Dk−1∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) = Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> k (ER(h)∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> )−Pˆ S<sup>2</sup> k (ER(h ′ )∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). Indeed, with additional modifications to the proof, we can then even slightly refine the query complexity analysis, since if we replace the sets ER(h) ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> with ER(h) ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> in Lemma [14,](#page-41-1) the *envelope* set in the application of Lemma [8](#page-35-1) in the proof of Lemma [14](#page-41-1) can be chosen as Dk−1∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> , so that we can refine the definition of pˆ<sup>k</sup> to 2Pˆ S<sup>4</sup> (Dk−1∩∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> )+O(εk). However, since these changes concern only the leading term <sup>β</sup> 2 ε d + log δ in Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) which is already optimal (perfectly matching the lower bounds of [Kääriäinen,](#page-14-2) [2006;](#page-14-2) [Beygelzimer, Dasgupta, and Langford,](#page-11-1) [2009\)](#page-11-1), they are completely inconsequential to the theorem. We have therefore stated the algorithm without these modifications, for simplicity. However, this modified variant would be interesting in the context of P-dependent analysis, where it can lead to refinements to the leading term in the upper bound under certain favorable distributions. We leave the investigation of such refinements as an interesting direction for future work (focusing our P-dependent analysis in Appendix [F](#page-51-0) on refining the *lower-order* term).

# F Distribution-Dependent Analysis

In addition to analysis based on the star number [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2), the active learning literature includes a variety of *distribution-dependent* complexity measures which have been used to analyze the query complexity in various contexts (see Appendix [A\)](#page-17-0). In this section, we will add to this line of work a distribution-dependent analysis of Aavid which replaces the star number s in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) by a (never-larger) distribution-dependent quantity (Theorem [27\)](#page-54-0), which can be further upper-bounded in terms of a simpler and more-familiar quantity: namely, a quadratic θ 2 dependence in the *disagreement coefficient* [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b;](#page-13-0) Definition [25](#page-51-1) below). We also show (in Appendix [F.1\)](#page-52-0) that it is *not possible* (by *any* algorithm) to obtain a lower-order term which replaces the star number in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) with the disagreement coefficient θ itself, so that the aforementioned θ <sup>2</sup> quadratic dependence generally cannot be reduced to linear (without introducing other factors). We will also present (in Appendix [F.3\)](#page-60-0) a slight refinement of Aavid, which replaces the region of disagreement Dk−<sup>1</sup> by a carefully-chosen *subregion*, following the technique of [Zhang and](#page-16-1) [Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1); [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang](#page-10-12) [\(2007\)](#page-10-12), which yields a corresponding refinement of the distribution-dependent query complexity bound. For instance, in the case of learning homogeneous linear classifiers under a uniform (or isotropic log-concave) distribution, this recovers a known query complexity bound O˜ d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + d (and indeed, improves log factors in the lead term compared to prior works).

The Disagreement Coefficient: In the context of *agnostic* active learning, the most commonly-used P-dependent complexity measure is the *disagreement coefficient*, introduced by [Hanneke](#page-13-0) [\(2007b\)](#page-13-0), defined as follows.

Definition 25. *For any concept class* C *and distribution* PX *on* X *, for any measurable function* f : X → {0, 1}*, for any* ε ≥ 0*, the* disagreement coefficient*, denoted by* θ<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> ,f (ε)*, is defined as*

$$\theta_{P_x, f}(\varepsilon) := \sup_{r > \varepsilon} \frac{P_X(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_x}(f, r)))}{r} \vee 1,$$

*where* B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (f, r) := {h ∈ C : PX (h ̸= f) ≤ r} *denotes the* r-ball centered at f*, and* DIS(C ′ ) := {x ∈ X : ∃h, h′ ∈ <sup>C</sup> ′ , h(x) ̸= h ′ (x)} *denotes the* region of disagreement *(as in Section [4\)](#page-4-1). For any distribution* P *on* X × {0, 1} *and* ε > 0*, for* h <sup>⋆</sup> *as in* [\(10\)](#page-36-1)*,* [<sup>14</sup>](#page-51-2) *define* θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) := θ<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> ,h<sup>⋆</sup> (ε)*.*

There are many works establishing bounds on the disagreement coefficient for commonly-studied classes C under various restrictions on the distribution P (see [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014,](#page-13-1) for a detailed summary). As discussed in Appendix [A,](#page-17-0) the disagreement coefficient commonly appears in analyses of the query complexity of *disagreement-based* active learning methods (e.g., [Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b,](#page-13-0) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011,](#page-13-13) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni,](#page-12-17) [2007\)](#page-12-17). Since the lower-order term in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) arises from the analysis of queries in the region of disagreement DIS(Vk−1) of Vk−1, one might naturally wonder whether we can replace s with θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) in the upper bound in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0) [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) have shown that sup<sup>P</sup> θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) = s ∧ ε for ε ∈ (0, 1], which implies that if we could replace s by θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) it would indeed represent a distribution-dependent refinement of the upper bound in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0) However, it turns out this is *not possible* (by *any* algorithm) for some classes C, as we demonstrate by an example in Appendix [F.1.](#page-52-0) Following this, in Appendix [F.2,](#page-53-0) we find that it is possible to achieve a lower-order term O˜(dθ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2 ), and indeed this is achieved by <sup>A</sup>avid. This quadratic dependence unfortunately means the upper bound is sometimes loose (i.e., sometimes larger than that in Theorem [3\)](#page-4-0). However, as an intermediate step, we also establish a query complexity bound (Theorem [27\)](#page-54-0) expressed in terms of a modified disagreement coefficient which is *never larger* than the s-dependent query complexity bound in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (though which is more difficult to evaluate due to a more-involved definition).

<sup>14</sup>When h ⋆ is not uniquely defined, in principle we can define θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) as the *infimum* value among all choices of such h ⋆ . It is also possible to define h ⋆ as an ε-independent fixed function, even when er<sup>P</sup> (h) does not have a minimizer in C, by choosing it as an element of the L1(PX )-closure of C having er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) = infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h): see [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-14) [2012\)](#page-13-14) for a proof that such an h ⋆ always exists when VC(C) < ∞. In particular, with such an h ⋆ , the limiting value θ<sup>P</sup> (0) := θ<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> ,h<sup>⋆</sup> (0) is also well-defined.

# F.1 Impossibility of Replacing s with θ<sup>P</sup> (ε)

In this section, we present an example demonstrating that *no algorithm* can achieve a P-dependent query complexity bound which replaces s by θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0)

An Example: Consider the following concept class (see [Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b,](#page-13-0) for a related construction). Let X = Z (the integers) and define a concept class

$$\mathbb{C}_{ts} := \{\mathbb{1}_{\{-t\} \cup [t, \infty)} : t \in \mathbb{N}\}.$$

In other words, each h ∈ <sup>C</sup>ts defines a *threshold* classifier on the *positive* integers and a *singleton* classifier on the *negative* integers, and the position −t of the singleton point mirrors the position t of the threshold boundary point.

Fix any ε, β ∈ (0, 1/3), denote by n = 1−2β 2ε , and for simplicity suppose n ∈ N. Define a marginal distribution PX on X as follows: ∀x ∈ {1, . . . , n}, PX ({x}) = <sup>2</sup><sup>β</sup> n and PX ({−x}) = 2ε. Note that this completely specifies PX . Now define a family of probability distributions P1, . . . , Pn: for each t ∈ {1, . . . , n}, P<sup>t</sup> has marginal distribution PX on X and conditional distribution ∀x ∈ X

$$P_t(Y = 1 | X = x) = \begin{cases} 1, & \text{if } x = -t \\ 0, & \text{if } x \in \{-1, \dots, -n\} \setminus \{-t\} \\ \frac{1}{2}, & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}$$

In particular, note that each P<sup>t</sup> satisfies infh∈Cts er<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (h) = β and h ⋆ is uniquely equal **1**{−t}∪[t,∞) . [15](#page-52-1)

Upper-bounding θ<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (ε): For any Pt, we will argue θ<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (ε) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> ,h<sup>⋆</sup> (0) = O( 1 β ). For any r > 0, if h ∈ B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r), then letting k<sup>h</sup> := |{h ̸= h <sup>⋆</sup>} ∩ {1, . . . , n}|, since each x ∈ {1, . . . , n} has PX ({x}) = <sup>2</sup><sup>β</sup> n , we must have <sup>k</sup><sup>h</sup> <sup>≤</sup> <sup>k</sup><sup>r</sup> := j rn 2β k . Since h and h <sup>⋆</sup> both implement threshold functions in this region {1, . . . , n}, the k<sup>h</sup> elements in {h ̸= h <sup>⋆</sup>} ∩ {1, . . . , n} are a contiguous segment: either {t, . . . , t + k<sup>h</sup> − 1} or {t − kh, . . . , t − 1}. In either case, we have {h ̸= h <sup>⋆</sup>} ∩ {1, . . . , n} ⊆ {t − kr, . . . , t + k<sup>r</sup> − 1} ∩ {1, . . . , n}. Moreover, since h = **1**{−<sup>t</sup> ′}∪[t ,∞) for some t ′ ∈ <sup>N</sup>, this further implies {h ̸= h <sup>⋆</sup>} ∩ {−1, . . . , −n} ⊆ {−(t − kr), . . . , −(t + kr)} ∩ {−1, . . . , −n}. Since DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r)) is just the union of these {h ̸= h <sup>⋆</sup>} regions among all h ∈ B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r), we have

$$\begin{aligned} & \text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_X}(h^*, r)) \cap \{-n, \dots, -1, 1, \dots, n\} \\ & \subseteq (\{-(t - k_r), \dots, -(t + k_r)\} \cap \{-1, \dots, -n\}) \cup (\{t - k_r, \dots, t + k_r - 1\} \cap \{1, \dots, n\}) \\ & \implies P_X(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_X}(h^*, r))) \leq (2k_r + 1)2\varepsilon + 2k_r \frac{2\beta}{n} \leq \frac{r(1 - 2\beta)}{\beta} + 2\varepsilon + 2r. \end{aligned}$$

We also note that any r < 2ε has B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r) = {h <sup>⋆</sup>}. Altogether,

$$\theta_{P_t}(\varepsilon) \leq \theta_{P_x, h^*}(0) \leq \sup_{r \geq 2\varepsilon} \frac{r((1-2\beta)/\beta) + 2\varepsilon + 2r}{r} = \frac{1-2\beta}{\beta} + 3 = O\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right).$$

Lower-bounding the query complexity: On the other hand, we will argue that the query complexity is Ω( <sup>1</sup> ε ) under the assumption P ∈ {P1, . . . , Pn}. Note that every h : X → {0, 1} has the *same* value of Pt(ER(h) \ {−1, . . . , −n}) = β. Together with the definition of P<sup>t</sup> in {−1, . . . , −n}, this implies any h : X → {0, 1} with {h ̸= h <sup>⋆</sup>}∩{−1, . . . , −n} ̸= ∅ has er<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (h)−er<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (h ⋆ ) ≥ 2ε. Thus, the problem of learning, to an excess error ε (under the assumption that P ∈ {P<sup>t</sup> : t ∈ {1, . . . , n}}) is equivalent to the problem of *identifying* the value t ∈ {1, . . . , n} for which the distribution P = Pt: that is, if an algorithm returns <sup>h</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> with er<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (hˆ) − er<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (h ⋆ ) ≤ ε, the unique x ∈ {1, . . . , n} for which hˆ(−x) = 1 satisfies x = t. Moreover, in the active learning problem defined by these distributions, for every x /∈ {−1, . . . , −n}, the conditional distribution Pt(Y = 1|X = x) of responses to queries for examples at x is *invariant* to t, so that such queries reveal no information about which

<sup>15</sup>Indeed, these distributions P<sup>t</sup> even satisfy the stronger *benign noise* property: i.e., infh∈Cts er<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (h) = Bayes risk (a setting studied by [Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b;](#page-13-3) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). Thus, the argument in this section further implies the impossibility of using θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) in the lower-order term under benign noise.

t has P = Pt, and hence without loss of generality we can restrict to active learning algorithms that do not query outside {−1, . . . , −n}. The unlabeled examples also reveal no such information, since all P<sup>t</sup> have the same marginal distribution PX . Altogether, the active learning problem for this set of distributions is information-theoretically no easier (in terms of query complexity) than the problem of actively identifying a *singleton* classifier on {−1, . . . , −n} in the realizable case under marginal Uniform({−1, . . . , −n}). [<sup>16</sup>](#page-53-1) It is well known that the minimax query complexity of this latter problem (with confidence parameter δ = 1/3) is Ω(n) = Ω( <sup>1</sup> ε ) [\(Dasgupta,](#page-12-10) [2004,](#page-12-10) [2005;](#page-12-0) [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2), which therefore serves as a lower bound on the minimax query complexity for P ∈ {P<sup>t</sup> : t ∈ {1, . . . , n}}: that is, for every active learning algorithm, there exists P ∈ {P<sup>t</sup> : t ∈ {1, . . . , n}} for which, with probability at least δ, it either makes Ω( <sup>1</sup> ε ) queries or returns <sup>h</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> with er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) <sup>&</sup>gt; infh∈Cts er<sup>P</sup> (h) + <sup>ε</sup>.

Conclusion that θ<sup>P</sup><sup>t</sup> (ε) is not achievable in the lower-order term: From the above arguments, we can conclude that for the class <sup>C</sup>ts, it is *not possible* to replace s by θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) (or indeed θ<sup>P</sup> (0)) in the upper bound of Theorem [3](#page-4-0) to obtain a P-dependent refinement of the upper bound. Formally, for *every* active learning algorithm guaranteeing that, for every P with infh∈Cts er<sup>P</sup> (h) ≤ β, with probability at least <sup>2</sup>/<sup>3</sup> (i.e., <sup>δ</sup> = 1/3), it returns <sup>h</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> with er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈Cts er<sup>P</sup> (h) + <sup>ε</sup>, there exists a distribution P satisfying this for which θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) ≤ 1−2β <sup>β</sup> + 3 = O( 1 β ), yet with probability at least 1/3, the algorithm makes a number of queries Ω( <sup>1</sup> ε ). We have argued this conclusion for any choices of ε, β ∈ (0, 1/3) (with n ∈ N for simplicity). In particular, for ε ≪ β ≪ √ ε (e.g., β ≈ ε 2/3 ), such a distribution P has <sup>β</sup> ε <sup>2</sup> + θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) = <sup>β</sup> ε <sup>2</sup> + O( β ) ≪ <sup>1</sup> ε , so that replacing s with θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) *cannot* yield a valid query complexity bound (holding for all P) for *any* active learning algorithm. Indeed, we have established that this conclusion also holds for θ<sup>P</sup> (0) (as defined in footnote [14\)](#page-51-2).

We will see in Corollary [28](#page-55-0) of Appendix [F.2](#page-53-0) that <sup>A</sup>avid *does* achieve an upper-bound O˜ dθ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2 on the lower-order term: a *quadratic* dependence on the disagreement coefficient. This conclusion is compatible with the above scenario, since <sup>β</sup> ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 <sup>β</sup><sup>2</sup> = Ω 1 ε for the full range of β, ε.

#### F.2 Replacing s with θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2

Appendix [F.1](#page-52-0) implies the disagreement coefficient θ<sup>P</sup> (ε), as defined in Definition [25,](#page-51-1) cannot be used as a P-dependent substitute for the star number s in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (at least, not with a *linear* dependence). In this section, we will argue that the AVID Agnostic algorithm Aavid *does* achieve a P-dependent lower-order term which is at most *quadratic* in the disagreement coefficient: namely, O˜(dθ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2 ). We will argue this by first establishing a P-dependent refinement of Theorem [5](#page-33-0) based on a modified disagreement coefficient (Definition [26\)](#page-54-1) which is *never larger* than the star number. While this quantity itself is often more-difficult to calculate, compared to the original disagreement coefficient θ<sup>P</sup> (ε), fortunately it is always upper bounded by O β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2 . In particular, this means that for any P with θ<sup>P</sup> (0) < ∞, the asymptotic dependence on ε, δ in the lower-order term in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) can be reduced to polylog εδ .

Specifically, the modified disagreement coefficient we consider can be expressed as the value θ<sup>P</sup>∆,h<sup>⋆</sup> (ε) produced under a *restriction* of PX to a subregion X \ ∆ of size at least 1 − O(β). Toward stating the definition, we first extend Definition [25](#page-51-1) to allow for general measures µ: that is, for any measure µ on X and measurable f : X → {0, 1}, define Bµ(f, r) := {h ∈ <sup>C</sup> : µ(h ̸= f) ≤ r},

<sup>16</sup>Formally, for any active learning algorithm <sup>A</sup>, under distributions P ∈ {P<sup>t</sup> : t ∈ {1, . . . , n}}, we can convert A into an active learner A ′ for realizable-case singletons under Uniform({−1, . . . , −n}) with at most the query complexity of A under such distributions P. Specifically, given any number m of i.i.d. unlabeled examples X1, . . . , X<sup>m</sup> ∼ Uniform({−1, . . . , −n}), define independent random variables (also independent of X1, . . . , Xm) B1, . . . , B<sup>m</sup> ∼ Bernoulli(2β), X ′ 1, . . . , X′ <sup>m</sup> ∼ Uniform({1, . . . , n}), and Y ′ <sup>1</sup> , . . . , Y ′ <sup>m</sup> ∼ Bernoulli( <sup>1</sup> ). For each i ≤ m, let X ′′ <sup>i</sup> = X<sup>i</sup> if B<sup>i</sup> = 0 and X ′′ <sup>i</sup> = X ′ i if B<sup>i</sup> = 1. Then A ′ runs A with unlabeled data X ′′ <sup>1</sup> , . . . , X′′ <sup>m</sup>; whenever <sup>A</sup> queries an X ′′ <sup>i</sup> with B<sup>i</sup> = 0, <sup>A</sup> ′ queries for the label Y<sup>i</sup> of X<sup>i</sup> and gives this as a response to the query, and whenever A queries an X ′′ <sup>i</sup> with B<sup>i</sup> = 1, <sup>A</sup> ′ gives Y ′ <sup>i</sup> as a response to the query. Note that the corresponding data sequence and responses observed by A are indeed identical to running A under P = Pt, where −t is the singleton location for the realizable-case singleton problem Pt(·|{−1, . . . , −n}). Thus, the query complexity of A ′ identifying the t for the realizable-case singletons distribution Pt(·|{−1, . . . , −n}) is at most that of A identifying this t when P = Pt.

and for ε ≥ 0 define θµ,f (ε) := supr>ε µ(DIS(Bµ(f,r))) r ∨1. We then consider the following definition: a *region-excluded disagreement coefficient*.

Definition 26. *For any distribution* P *on* X × {0, 1} *and any measurable* ∆ ⊆ X *, define a measure* A 7→ P∆(A) := PX (A \ ∆)*. For any* ε, τ ≥ 0*, for* h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> *as in* [\(10\)](#page-36-1) *(under* P*),*[<sup>17</sup>](#page-54-2) *define*

$$\theta_P(\varepsilon; \tau) := \sup_{\Delta \subseteq \mathcal{X}: P_X(\Delta) \leq \tau} \theta_{P_\Delta, h^*}(\varepsilon).$$

We can equivalently define θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) as the disagreement coefficient under a worst-case *conditional* distribution PX (·|X \ ∆): that is,

$$\theta_P(\varepsilon; \tau) = \sup_{\Delta \subseteq \mathcal{X}: P_X(\Delta) \leq \tau} \theta_{P_X(\cdot | \mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta), h^*(\varepsilon/P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta))}, \quad (46)$$

where we define θ<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (·|X \∆),h<sup>⋆</sup> (ε/PX (X \ ∆)) = 1 in the case PX (X \ ∆) = 0 (which coincides with the value θ<sup>P</sup>∆,h<sup>⋆</sup> (ε) for such ∆).

We may note that θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) indeed provides a *refinement* of the star number, in that it is *never larger*. Specifically, since [Hanneke and Yang](#page-13-2) [\(2015\)](#page-13-2) have shown

$$\sup_{P_X} \sup_{h \in \mathbb{C}} \theta_{P_X, h}(\varepsilon) = \mathfrak{s} \wedge \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \quad (47)$$

for every ε ∈ (0, 1], the expression in [\(46\)](#page-54-3) of θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) as the disagreement coefficient under *conditional* distributions immediately implies

$$\theta_P(\varepsilon; \tau) \leq \mathfrak{s} \wedge \frac{1}{\varepsilon}. \quad (48)$$

Thus, replacing s in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) by θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) would indeed yield a (never-larger) P-dependent refinement.

We give examples below (Appendix [F.2.1\)](#page-56-0) of calculating and upper-bounding θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) under various scenarios (C, P). We remark that, due to the supremum over regions ∆, the quantity θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) is often *much* more involved to calculate or bound compared to the original disagreement coefficient θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) in Definition [25.](#page-51-1) We might therefore think of θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) as a kind of *intermediate* complexity measure, which is useful in that it provides a P-dependent refinement of s, while also admitting general upper bounds which are more accessible than directly calculating θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ). Concretely, there are at least *weak* relations between θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) and the more-familiar disagreement coefficient from Definition [25:](#page-51-1) namely, θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) and

$$\begin{aligned} \theta_P(\varepsilon; \tau) &\leq \sup_{r>\varepsilon} \frac{P_X(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_X}(h^*, \tau + r)))}{r} \vee 1 \\ &\leq \theta_P(\tau + \varepsilon) \left( \frac{\tau + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon} \right) \leq \theta_P(\tau + \varepsilon)^2 + \left( \frac{\tau + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon} \right)^2. \end{aligned} \quad (49)$$

These upper bounds on θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) are noteworthy since θ<sup>P</sup> (τ + ε) is typically significantly easier to calculate compared to directly calculating θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) (and there are already many works deriving bounds on θ<sup>P</sup> (τ + ε) for various scenarios; see [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014\)](#page-13-1).

The quantity θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) is particularly well-suited for the analysis of <sup>A</sup>avid, since the algorithm explicitly maintains low diameter of V<sup>k</sup> under a region-excluded measure A 7→ PX (A \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ). Specifically, Lemma [<sup>12</sup>](#page-40-3) implies <sup>V</sup>k−<sup>1</sup> <sup>⊆</sup> <sup>B</sup><sup>P</sup>∆ik (h ⋆ , εk), while Lemma [20](#page-45-0) implies PX (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 5β, so that PX (Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (εk; 5β)εk, and hence the number of queries in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> is O(θ<sup>P</sup> (εk; 5β)εkmk) = O˜(θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β)d). Formally, this leads to the following result, which simply replaces s with θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) in the lower-order term compared to Theorem [5.](#page-33-0) Due to [\(48\)](#page-54-4), the query complexity bound in this result is *never larger* than that of Theorem [5](#page-33-0) (and below we discuss scenarios where it is strictly smaller). We remark that, based on the comment preceding Lemma [20,](#page-45-0) the factor "5" in θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) in this theorem can be reduced to any value c > 2 by appropriately adjusting the constants C, C ′′ in the algorithm.

<sup>17</sup>The remarks concerning the choice of h ⋆ in footnote [14](#page-51-2) also apply here, noting that the lemmas concerning h ⋆ in Appendix [E](#page-36-0) actually apply simultaneously to all functions h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>C</sup> satisfying [\(10\)](#page-36-1).

Theorem 27 (Distribution-dependent Query Complexity of AVID Agnostic). *For any concept class* C *with* VC(C) < ∞*, letting* d = VC(C)*, for every distribution* P *on* X × {0, 1}*, letting* β = infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h)*, for any* ε, δ ∈ (0, 1)*, if the algorithm* <sup>A</sup>avid *is executed with parameters* (ε, δ)*, with any number* m ≥ M(ε, δ; β) *of i.i.d.-*P *examples (for* M(ε, δ; β) *as in Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) defined in Lemma [24\)](#page-49-0), then with probability at least* 1 − δ*, the returned predictor* hˆ *satisfies* er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε *and the algorithm makes a number of queries at most* Q(ε, δ; P) *satisfying*

$$\begin{aligned} Q(\varepsilon, \delta; P) &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \min\left\{ \theta_P(\varepsilon; 5\beta) \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right\} \left( \mathbf{d} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) \\ &= \tilde{O}\left( \mathbf{d} \frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} + \mathbf{d} \theta_P(\varepsilon; 5\beta) \right). \end{aligned}$$

Proof. The result follows identically to Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) with only one minor change: replacing s with 2Cθ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) in Lemma [22.](#page-46-0) Note that this one change will suffice, since every subsequent appearance of s in the proof is due to its appearance in Lemma [22,](#page-46-0) and hence changing s to 2Cθ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) in this lemma allows us to make the same change in every subsequent appearance of s in the proof.

To see why Lemma [22](#page-46-0) remains valid with this change, first note that its proof establishes that, on the event E<sup>0</sup> ∩ E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E2, in the non-trivial case of PX (X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ̸= 0, [\(38\)](#page-47-5) holds. Rather than relaxing the third expression in [\(38\)](#page-47-5) using the star number, we can instead relax it using θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β): that is, for P<sup>k</sup> as defined in that context, [\(38\)](#page-47-5) implies

$$\begin{aligned} P_X(D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) &= P_k(D_{k-1}) P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \leq P_k\left(\text{DIS}\left(B_{P_k}\left(h^*, \frac{\varepsilon_k}{P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k})}\right)\right)\right) P_X(\mathcal{X} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \\ &= P_{\Delta_{i_k}}\left(\text{DIS}\left(B_{P_{\Delta_{i_k}}}(h^*, \varepsilon_k)\right)\right) \leq \theta_P(\varepsilon/(2C); 5\beta) \varepsilon_k, \end{aligned}$$

where the last inequality follows from Definition [26,](#page-54-1) the fact that ε<sup>k</sup> > ε 2C , and the fact (from Lemma [20\)](#page-45-0) that PX (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ) ≤ 5β. We then note that (as in Corollary 7.2 of [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014\)](#page-13-1) for any ∆ ⊆ X ,

$$\theta_{P_\Delta, h^*}(\varepsilon/(2C)) = \sup_{r > \varepsilon} \frac{P_\Delta(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_\Delta}(h^*, r/(2C))))}{r/(2C)} \vee 1 \leq 2C \sup_{r > \varepsilon} \frac{P_\Delta(\text{DIS}(\text{B}_{P_\Delta}(h^*, r)))}{r} \vee 1,$$

and therefore θ<sup>P</sup> (ε/(2C); 5β) ≤ 2Cθ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β). Altogether, we have that Lemma [22](#page-46-0) remains valid while replacing s with 2Cθ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β). ■

We emphasize that <sup>A</sup>avid *does not need to know* the value θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) (or anything else about P) to achieve this query complexity: that is, it is *adaptive* to the value of θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β).

Together with [\(49\)](#page-54-5), the above result further implies a (sometimes loose) relaxation, in which the lower-order term has a *quadratic* dependence on θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε), as formally stated in the following corollary (compare this with Appendix [F.1,](#page-52-0) which showed it is impossible to generally reduce this θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) 2 term to a linear term θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) or even θ<sup>P</sup> (0)).

Corollary 28. *The query complexity bound* Q(ε, δ; P) *in Theorem [27](#page-54-0) (achieved by* <sup>A</sup>avid*) satisfies*

$$\begin{aligned} Q(\varepsilon, \delta; P) &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) + \tilde{O}\left(\min\left\{ \mathbf{d}\theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon) \left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{\mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon} \right\} \right) \\ &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) + \tilde{O}\left(\min\left\{ \mathbf{d}\theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon)^2, \frac{\mathbf{d}}{\varepsilon} \right\} \right). \end{aligned}$$

Proof. Due to the first two inequalities in [\(49\)](#page-54-5), and θ<sup>P</sup> (5β + ε) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε), the second term in the expression of Q(ε, δ; P) in Theorem [27](#page-54-0) is at most

$$O\left(\min\left\{\theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon) \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\} \left(\mathbf{d} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)\right). \quad (50)$$

Relaxing d log 1 ε + log 1 δ ≤ log ε d + log 1 δ and noting that

$$\theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon) \log^2\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right) \leq \theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon)^2 \log^4\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right)^2,$$

and β+ε ε 2 ≤ 4 β ε <sup>2</sup> + 4, the quantity [\(50\)](#page-55-1) is at most

$$O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \min\left\{ \theta_P(\beta + \varepsilon)^2 \log^4\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) \right\} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right).$$

Adding this to the first term in the expression of Q(ε, δ; P), the result follows. ■

In particular, Corollary [28](#page-55-0) implies that, whenever θ<sup>P</sup> (0) < ∞, the dependence on β, ε in the query complexity bound in Theorem [27](#page-54-0) is of order <sup>β</sup> 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + polylog ε . For instance, see [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014,](#page-13-1) Chapter 7) for some general conditions on (C, P) under which this occurs. We remark that the *first* bound in Corollary [28](#page-55-0) is at least never larger than the upper bound in Theorem [1,](#page-3-0) since we always have θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) β+ε ε ≤ ε ; however, we note that this is *not* the case for the *second* upper bound in Corollary [28.](#page-55-0) Beyond these basic observations, there exist scenarios (C, P) where both upper bounds in Corollary [28](#page-55-0) are *loose* compared to Theorem [27,](#page-54-0) to such an extent that they are sometimes even larger than the s-dependent bound in Theorem [5](#page-33-0) (see Example [6](#page-59-0) below). It is for this reason that we have chosen to express Theorem [27](#page-54-0) in terms of the more-complicated quantity θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ), to provide a starting point for P-dependent analysis that is at least never worse than Theorem [5.](#page-33-0)

### F.2.1 Examples

We next present some examples illustrating the values of the lower-order terms in Theorem [27](#page-54-0) and Corollary [28](#page-55-0) by bounding the quantities θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) and θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) . Specifically, Example [1](#page-56-1) achieves this via the relation to s, Example [2](#page-56-2) provides a simple scenario with s = ∞ where it is possible to directly bound θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ), Example [3](#page-56-3) expresses a bound on θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) which is known in the literature, but when combined with Corollary [28](#page-55-0) provides an improved P-dependent query complexity bound compared to previous works. Example [5](#page-57-0) revisits the example from Appendix [F.1](#page-52-0) to illustrate that θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) provides a valid lower-order term for this example. In Appendix [F.4,](#page-67-0) we will present additional examples of P-dependent query complexity bounds, for some classes with VC(C) = ∞, via PX -dependent *covering numbers*.

Example 1 (Thresholds). Due to [\(48\)](#page-54-4), any <sup>C</sup> with finite star number s admits a bounded θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ). A simple example of this is *threshold* classifiers: namely, X = <sup>R</sup> and <sup>C</sup> = {**1**[t,∞) : t ∈ <sup>R</sup>}. This class has s = 2 [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2), and hence θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) ≤ 2 for any P.

Example 2 (Linear classifiers under 1-sparse distributions). To illustrate a simple example where θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) = O(1) while s = ∞, consider the class <sup>C</sup> of linear classifiers in X = <sup>R</sup> p , p ≥ 2: that is, <sup>C</sup> = {x 7→ **1**⟨w,x⟩+b≥<sup>0</sup> : w ∈ <sup>R</sup> p , b ∈ <sup>R</sup>}. This class has s = ∞ [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). However, if we consider PX as a distribution supported entirely on *one axis* (e.g., Uniform([0, 1] × {0} p−1 )), then it is a simple exercise to show that θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) ≤ 2: the concepts in B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , r) are those that disagree with h <sup>⋆</sup> on at most r measure (under P∆) either to the left or right of where the h ⋆ separator intersects the axis, so that DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , r)) is simply the union of these two (at most) r-measure regions, hence has P<sup>∆</sup> measure at most 2r.

While the above examples merely recover known results, the following example derives a previouslyunknown P-dependent query complexity bound, which significantly improves over the best previously-known bound for this scenario.

Example 3 (Rectangles). Consider the case X = R p , p ≥ 1, and <sup>C</sup> = {**1**[a1,b1]×···×[ap,bp] : a<sup>1</sup> ≤ b1, . . . , a<sup>p</sup> ≤ bp}: the class of *axis-aligned rectangles* [\(Mitchell,](#page-14-7) [1979\)](#page-14-7). This class is known to have s = ∞ [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2). Consider PX = Uniform([0, 1]<sup>p</sup> ) (the example trivially extends to any product distribution PX with marginals on each axis having continuous CDFs) and any P with well-defined h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ argminh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) satisfying PX ({x : h ⋆ (x) = 1}) =: λ > 0. The optimal firstorder query complexity under these conditions is not yet precisely known. However, [Wiener, Hanneke,](#page-16-9) [and El-Yaniv](#page-16-9) [\(2015\)](#page-16-9) have shown that θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) = O d λ log(d) ∧ 1 β+ε for this scenario, and based on this, the best known query complexity upper bound is of the form O˜ minn d 2 λ (β+ε) ε <sup>2</sup> , d β+ε ε 2 o. We can derive a bound which improves over this, as follows. We first recall that Theorem [1](#page-3-0) provides a query complexity bound O˜ d β ε <sup>2</sup> + d ε , which *already* improves over the query complexity bound

of [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv](#page-16-9) [\(2015\)](#page-16-9) in all regimes with ε ≪ β ≪ 1 (for every λ). However, we can further refine the lower-order term by introducing a dependence on λ. Specifically, the first bound in Corollary [28](#page-55-0) provides a query complexity bound O˜ d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + d (β+ε) λε , which offers a refinement over Theorem [1](#page-3-0) whenever λ ≫ d(β + ε). Moreover, the second bound in Corollary [28](#page-55-0) provides a query complexity bound O˜ d β 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + d λ<sup>2</sup> . In particular, for λ = Θ(1) and β = O˜( √ ε), this yields a query complexity bound poly(d)polylog εδ , which was only available in the bound of [Wiener,](#page-16-9) [Hanneke, and El-Yaniv](#page-16-9) [\(2015\)](#page-16-9) in the more-restrictive regime β = O˜(ε). We leave open the question of identifying the *optimal* query complexity for this scenario. In particular, one concrete technical question toward that end would be to determine whether, for λ > 2τ , θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) = O˜ d λ .

Example 4 (Linear Classifiers). Consider the commonly-studied concept class of *linear classifiers*, defined as: X = R d−1 (d ≥ 3) and <sup>C</sup> = {hw,b : w ∈ <sup>R</sup> d−1 , b ∈ <sup>R</sup>}, where hw,b(x) = **1**[⟨w, x⟩+b ≥ 0]. This is perhaps the most well-studied concept class in the active learning literature. Its VC dimension satisfies VC(C) = d [\(Vapnik and Chervonenkis,](#page-16-4) [1974\)](#page-16-4), and while its star number satisfies s = ∞ [\(Hanneke and Yang,](#page-13-2) [2015\)](#page-13-2), the disagreement coefficient has been shown to be bounded or sublinear under various distributional conditions [\(Hanneke,](#page-13-0) [2007b,](#page-13-0) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Balcan, Hanneke,](#page-10-13) [and Vaughan,](#page-10-13) [2010;](#page-10-13) [Friedman,](#page-12-19) [2009;](#page-12-19) [Mahalanabis,](#page-14-15) [2011;](#page-14-15) [Wiener, Hanneke, and El-Yaniv,](#page-16-9) [2015\)](#page-16-9). These results compose directly with Corollary [28](#page-55-0) to yield previously-unknown bounds on the query complexity under these same conditions. For instance, if PX is a mixture of a finite number t of multivariate Gaussian distributions with full-rank diagonal covariance matrices, then [Wiener, Hanneke,](#page-16-9) [and El-Yaniv](#page-16-9) [\(2015\)](#page-16-9) provide a bound θ<sup>P</sup> (r) ≤ cd,t log<sup>d</sup>−<sup>2</sup> 1 r for a (d, t)-dependent constant cd,t. Plugging into Corollary [28](#page-55-0) (or rather, the explicit bounds in the proof thereof) yields a novel query complexity bound of order <sup>β</sup> ε d + log δ + c 2 <sup>d</sup>,t log2(d−2) β+ε log<sup>4</sup> ε d + log δ . More generally, if PX admits a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure on R d−1 , then (taking h ⋆ as in footnote [14\)](#page-51-2) [Hanneke](#page-13-1) [\(2014\)](#page-13-1) argues that θ<sup>P</sup> (r) = o r (where the specific form of this function θ<sup>P</sup> (r) varies depending on P). Recalling that (as ε → 0) the lower-order term becomes relevant only in the regime β ≪ √ ε, combining this with Corollary [28](#page-55-0) yields a query complexity bound which often provides refinements over Theorem [1.](#page-3-0) In particular, under sufficient regularity conditions on PX (see the proof of [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014\)](#page-13-1) to ensure this o 1 r function further satisfies θ<sup>P</sup> (r) log<sup>2</sup> r = o r , the resulting asymptotic dependence on (ε, β) is of the form <sup>β</sup> 2 ε <sup>2</sup> + o ε . Moreover, if additionally the density of PX is bounded and has finite-diameter support, and if the hyperplane boundary corresponding to h <sup>⋆</sup> passes through a continuity point of this density in its support, then [Hanneke](#page-13-1) [\(2014\)](#page-13-1) argues θ<sup>P</sup> (r) = O(1), so that Corollary [28](#page-55-0) yields a query complexity bound with asymptotic dependence on (ε, β) of the form <sup>β</sup> ε <sup>2</sup> + log<sup>4</sup> ε . Moreover, under the further restrictions (density bounded away from 0, compactness of the support), [Friedman](#page-12-19) [\(2009\)](#page-12-19); [Mahalanabis](#page-14-15) [\(2011\)](#page-14-15) argue θ<sup>P</sup> (r) is asymptotically bounded by O(d) (for the precise statement, see the original works, or discussion thereof by [Hanneke,](#page-13-1) [2014\)](#page-13-1).

Example 5 (Coupled thresholds and singletons). Let us revisit the example from Appendix [F.1,](#page-52-0) for which we argued that θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) cannot itself be used to replace the star number in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (for *any* algorithm). We will here explain how the region-excluded disagreement coefficient θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) explicitly corrects for the issue with θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) in this example. Specifically, consider again X = <sup>Z</sup>, and <sup>C</sup> = <sup>C</sup>ts := {**1**{−t}∪[t,∞) : t ∈ N}, the class of *coupled* thresholds and singletons. Let ε, β ∈ (0, 1/3), let n = 1−2β 2ε (and assume n ∈ N), and consider again the distributions Pt, t ∈ {1, . . . , n}, as defined in Appendix [F.1:](#page-52-0) that is, all P<sup>t</sup> have marginal PX on X , where for x ∈ {1, . . . , n}, PX ({x}) = <sup>2</sup><sup>β</sup> n , PX ({−x}) = 2ε, and for x ∈ {−1, . . . , −n}, Pt(Y = 1|X = x) = **1**[x = −t], while every x /∈ {−1, . . . , −n} has Pt(Y = 1|X = x) = <sup>1</sup> 2 . Note that, for ∆ = [0, ∞), we have PX (∆) = 2β. Moreover, for h ⋆ as defined under Pt, we have B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , 4ε) = <sup>C</sup>ts (since only the disagreements on the *singleton* part are measured by P∆). This implies DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , 4ε)) = <sup>Z</sup> \ {0}, so that P∆(DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , 4ε))) = 1 − 2β. Therefore, θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) ≥ θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 2β) ≥ <sup>P</sup>∆(DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (<sup>h</sup> ⋆ ,4ε))) <sup>4</sup><sup>ε</sup> = 1−2β 4ε . Since we always have θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) ≤ 1 ε , we conclude that θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) = Θ 1 ε . As argued in Appendix [F.1,](#page-52-0) the minimax optimal query complexity (constraining to P ∈ {P<sup>t</sup> : t{1, . . . , n}}) is Ω 1 ε , so that, unlike θ<sup>P</sup> (ε), the quantity θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) is an appropriate replacement for s in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0) Of course, Theorem [27](#page-54-0) shows this replacement is *always* valid, so the point here is merely to illustrate how the exclusion of the ∆ region in the definition of

θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) is precisely the right type of correction, compared to θ<sup>P</sup> (ε), for this example, as it explicitly removes the issue underlying the failure of θ<sup>P</sup> (ε): namely, the fact that the *threshold* portion of the concepts **1**{−<sup>t</sup> ′}∪[t ,∞) is irrelevant to the learning problem inherent in the P<sup>t</sup> distributions. It is also worth noting that the *first* upper bound θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (5β + ε) 5β+ε ε from [\(49\)](#page-54-5) also yields a value Θ 1 ε (since this upper bound is *never* larger than <sup>1</sup> ε ). However, the *second* upper bound θ<sup>P</sup> (5β +ε) <sup>2</sup> + 5β+ε ε 2 can be significantly looser for this example, in most regimes of ε, β (namely, <sup>β</sup> ̸= Θ(√ ε)).

# F.2.2 The Error Disagreement Coefficient

It is also possible to derive Corollary [28](#page-55-0) via another intermediate P-dependent variant of the disagreement coefficient: namely, the *error disagreement coefficient*, defined as follows.

Definition 29. *For any probability distribution* P *on* X × {0, 1}*, for any* ε ≥ 0*, define*

$$\theta_P^{\text{er}}(\varepsilon) := \sup_{r > \varepsilon} \frac{P_X(\text{DIS}(\mathbb{C}_P(r)))}{r} \vee 1,$$

*where* <sup>C</sup><sup>P</sup> (r) := {h ∈ <sup>C</sup> : er<sup>P</sup> (h) − infh′∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h ′ ) ≤ r} *is known as the* r*-*minimal set*.*

Similarly to θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ), the quantity θ er P (ε) has direct relations to the original disagreement coefficient from Definition [25.](#page-51-1) Specifically, for h ⋆ as in [\(10\)](#page-36-1), [<sup>18</sup>](#page-58-0) since B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , r/2) ⊆ <sup>C</sup><sup>P</sup> (r) ⊆ B<sup>P</sup><sup>X</sup> (h ⋆ , 2(β + r)) for any r > ε, we immediately have

$$\frac{1}{2}\theta_P(\varepsilon/2) \leq \theta_P^{\text{er}}(\varepsilon) \leq 2\theta_P(2(\beta + \varepsilon)) \left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right) \leq \theta_P(2(\beta + \varepsilon))^2 + 4\left(\frac{\beta + \varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right)^2. \quad (51)$$

By definition, we always have θ er P (ε) ≤ 1 ε . However, unlike θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) in [\(48\)](#page-54-4), the quantity θ er P (ε) is *not* always upper-bounded by the star number s (see Example [6](#page-59-0) below), so that we need be careful when replacing s by θ er P (ε) in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0)

It is also worth noting that θ er P (ε) is often not as easy to use for studying specific scenarios, compared to θ<sup>P</sup> (ε), due to the dependence on the conditional distribution Y |X (whereas θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) depends only on PX and h ⋆ ). Nonetheless, below we will state a query complexity bound in terms of θ er P (ε) (Theorem [30\)](#page-58-1) which is sometimes smaller than that in Theorem [27](#page-54-0) (as we illustrate in examples below), and moreover (together with [\(51\)](#page-58-2)) provides another route to proving the query complexity bound in Corollary [28.](#page-55-0)

The quantity θ er P (ε) essentially arises naturally in many existing analyses of disagreement-based active learning (e.g., [Hanneke,](#page-13-3) [2009b,](#page-13-3) [2011,](#page-13-13) [2014;](#page-13-1) [Koltchinskii,](#page-14-5) [2010;](#page-14-5) [Foster, Rakhlin, Simchi-Levi,](#page-12-2) [and Xu,](#page-12-2) [2021\)](#page-12-2), wherein certain algorithms are shown to makes queries in a subset of DIS(C<sup>P</sup> (ε ′ )) for an appropriate ε ′ ≥ ε (decreasing as the algorithm runs). In those contexts, it is traditional to upper bound PX (DIS(C<sup>P</sup> (ε ′ ))) by θ<sup>P</sup> (r(ε ′ ))r(ε ′ ), where r(ε ′ ) ≥ suph∈C<sup>P</sup> (<sup>ε</sup> ′) PX (h ̸= h ⋆ ): for instance, r(ε ′ ) = 2(β + ε ′ ) suffices in the agnostic setting. However, one can alternatively upper bound PX (DIS(C<sup>P</sup> (ε ′ ))) by θ er P (ε ′ )ε ′ . Such arguments are also valid in the context of Aavid, since Corollary [18](#page-44-1) implies PX (Dk−1) ≤ PX (DIS(C<sup>P</sup> (εk))) ≤ θ er P (εk)εk, so that the number of queries in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> in round k is of order θ er P (εk)εkm<sup>k</sup> = O˜(θ er P (εk)d), which will lead to a lower-order term O˜(θ er P (ε)d). Together with reasoning similar to the proof of Theorem [27,](#page-54-0) this implies the following.

Theorem 30. *Under the same conditions as Theorem [27,](#page-54-0) with probability at least* 1 − δ *the predictor* hˆ *returned by* <sup>A</sup>avid *satisfies* er<sup>P</sup> (hˆ) ≤ infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) + ε *and the algorithm makes a number of queries at most* Q(ε, δ; P) *satisfying*

$$\begin{aligned}Q(\varepsilon, \delta; P) &= O\left(\frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} \left( \mathbf{d} + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) + \min\left\{ \theta_P^{\text{er}}(\varepsilon) \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right), \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right\} \left( \mathbf{d} \log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right) + \log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right) \right) \right) \\ &= \tilde{O}\left( \mathbf{d} \frac{\beta^2}{\varepsilon^2} + \mathbf{d} \theta_P^{\text{er}}(\varepsilon) \right).\end{aligned}$$

<sup>18</sup>The relation sharpens to θ<sup>P</sup> (ε) ≤ θ er <sup>P</sup> (ε) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (2β + ε) 2β+ε ε if we take h <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ argminh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h), supposing this exists (or otherwise, taking h ⋆ as discussed in footnote [14\)](#page-51-2).

Since θ er P (ε) ≤ 1 ε , the query complexity bound in Theorem [30](#page-58-1) is never larger than that in Theorem [1.](#page-3-0) However, unlike Theorem [27,](#page-54-0) since the quantity θ er P (ε) is *not* always upper-bounded by the star number s, the query complexity bound in Theorem [30](#page-58-1) is sometimes *larger* than that in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (see Example [6](#page-59-0) below). That said, the quantities θ er P (ε) and θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) are generally incomparable (see Examples [6](#page-59-0) and [7\)](#page-59-1), so that either bound may be useful depending on the scenario being studied. Moreover, in light of [\(51\)](#page-58-2), Theorem [30](#page-58-1) is also useful for providing another route to establishing Corollary [28,](#page-55-0) which is therefore an immediate corollary of *either* Theorem [27](#page-54-0) or Theorem [30.](#page-58-1)

As mentioned, depending on (C, P), the quantitative difference between θ er P (ε) and θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) can be better or worse. We illustrate this in the following two examples.

Example 6 (θ er P (ε) ≫ s ≥ θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β)). As mentioned, for some scenarios, θ er P (ε) can be quite large, even larger than the *star number* s, so that the bound in Theorem [30](#page-58-1) becomes even worse than the P-independent bound in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) (in contrast to Theorem [27,](#page-54-0) which is never worse than Theorem [3\)](#page-4-0). For instance, consider a *singletons* class: X = {1, . . . , 2 β }, <sup>C</sup> = {**1**{t} : t ∈ X }, where β ∈ (0, 1/2) satisfies <sup>2</sup> β ∈ <sup>N</sup> for simplicity. Let PX = Uniform(X ), P(Y = 1|X = x) = <sup>β</sup> 2(1−β) for all x ∈ X . Note that every h ∈ <sup>C</sup> has er<sup>P</sup> (h) = β. Moreover, s = |X | − 1 = <sup>2</sup>−<sup>β</sup> β . However, consider 0 < ε ≪ β. Since <sup>C</sup><sup>P</sup> (ε) = <sup>C</sup> and DIS(C) = X , we have θ er P (ε) = <sup>1</sup> <sup>ε</sup> = Θ s β ε . For instance, for β = ε 2/3 , the bound in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) is (ignoring logs) of order <sup>β</sup> ε <sup>2</sup> + 1 <sup>β</sup> = 2 ε <sup>2</sup>/<sup>3</sup> ≪ <sup>1</sup> <sup>ε</sup> = θ er P (ε). In light of [\(51\)](#page-58-2), this example also witnesses a scenario where both of the query complexity bounds in Corollary [28](#page-55-0) are worse than the s-dependent bound in Theorem [3;](#page-4-0) more directly, in this example, we have θ<sup>P</sup> (β + ε) β+ε ε = <sup>ε</sup> <sup>≫</sup> <sup>s</sup>. In contrast, for any r < <sup>β</sup> 2 and ∆ ⊂ X , we have DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , r)) ∈ {∅, ∆} (depending whether the x with h ⋆ (x) = 1 is in ∆ or not), so that P∆(DIS(B<sup>P</sup><sup>∆</sup> (h ⋆ , r))) = 0; this immediately implies θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) ≤ β (indeed, by careful reasoning, we can observe that any τ ≥ β 2 has θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; τ ) = <sup>2</sup> <sup>β</sup> − 1 = s). More generally, by [\(48\)](#page-54-4), the bound in Theorem [27](#page-54-0) is never worse than that in Theorem [3.](#page-4-0)

On the other hand, there are scenarios (C, P) where the opposite occurs, so that in general neither quantity θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) nor θ er P (ε) dominates the other. This is illustrated in the following example.

Example 7 (θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) ≫ θ er P (ε)). Consider again the class from Appendix [F.1](#page-52-0) (and Example [5\)](#page-57-0): that is, X = <sup>Z</sup> and <sup>C</sup> = <sup>C</sup>ts := {**1**{−t}∪[t,∞) : t ∈ N}. Let ε, β ∈ (0, 1/3) with ε ≪ β, and define P with marginal PX on X as defined in Appendix [F.1:](#page-52-0) that is, n = 1−2β 2ε , and for x ∈ {1, . . . , n}, PX ({x}) = <sup>2</sup><sup>β</sup> n , PX ({−x}) = 2ε. However, rather than the distributions P<sup>t</sup> described there, consider the family P ′ t , t ∈ {1, . . . , n}, with P ′ t (Y = 1|X = x) = β + (1 − 2β)**1**{−t}∪[t,∞)(x) for every x ∈ X . These distributions represent a scenario with *uniform classification noise*. For P = P ′ t for any t ∈ {1, . . . , n}, letting h <sup>⋆</sup> = **1**{−t}∪[t,∞) , it is easy to see that er<sup>P</sup> (h ⋆ ) = infh∈<sup>C</sup> er<sup>P</sup> (h) = β. Moreover, θ er P (ε) = θ<sup>P</sup> (ε/(1 − 2β)) ≤ θ<sup>P</sup> (0) ≤ 1−2β <sup>β</sup> + 3, where the last inequality was established in Appendix [F.1.](#page-52-0) In contrast, we argued in Example [5](#page-57-0) that θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) ≥ 1−2β <sup>4</sup><sup>ε</sup> <sup>≫</sup> <sup>1</sup>−2<sup>β</sup> <sup>β</sup> + 3 ≥ θ er P (ε). Thus, in this scenario, θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) is larger than θ er P (ε).

A natural question is whether the gaps between θ<sup>P</sup> (ε; 5β) and θ er P (ε) in Examples [6](#page-59-0) and [7](#page-59-1) can also arise in cases where the smaller of the two corresponding query complexity bounds (either Theorem [27](#page-54-0) or [30\)](#page-58-1) is actually nearly-*sharp* (in a minimax analysis over a family of distributions). This is straightforward to obtain, by defining a family of possible distributions P each obtained as a uniform *mixture* of one of the above two scenarios and the simple 2-point construction of [Kääriäinen](#page-14-2) [\(2006\)](#page-14-2) giving rise to the <sup>β</sup> 2 ε <sup>2</sup> lower bound. For brevity, we omit the details of this.

The fact that Theorem [27](#page-54-0) at least provides a starting point for P-dependent analysis which is never worse than the P-independent bound in Theorem [3](#page-4-0) is a desirable feature. In contrast, the bound in Theorem [30](#page-58-1) is sometimes better and sometimes worse than that in Theorem [3,](#page-4-0) so that one should be careful when using Theorem [30.](#page-58-1) Nonetheless, as illustrated in Example [7,](#page-59-1) there are at least some scenarios where θ er P (ε) may be useful for describing favorable scenarios (particularly concerning the Y |X conditional distribution).

#### F.3 Querying in Subregions of the Region of Disagreement

In the active learning literature, one technique for going beyond disagreement-based queries is to query examples in a carefully selected *subregion* R ⊆ DIS(V ) of the region of disagreement of the set V of surviving concepts. This idea originates in the work of [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang](#page-10-12) [\(2007\)](#page-10-12) on *margin-based* active learning of homogeneous linear classifiers under certain marginals PX in realizable and Tsybakov-noise scenarios, and was extended to a technique for general concept classes and the agnostic case by [Zhang and Chaudhuri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1) (see Appendix [A](#page-17-0) for further discussion of the history). For instance, the most well-known case of this technique providing improvements over disagreement-based queries (see Example [8](#page-67-1) below) is homogeneous linear classifiers under a uniform distribution on a sphere (alternatively, any isotropic log-concave distribution), where the query complexity of this technique is O˜ d (β+ε) 2 ε [\(Zhang and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014\)](#page-16-1) (minimax optimal up to log factors), compared to disagreement-based active learning for which the best known bound is O˜ d 3/2 · (β+ε) ε [\(Dasgupta, Hsu, and Monteleoni,](#page-12-17) [2007\)](#page-12-17).

In this section, we show this technique is also compatible with the AVID principle, and propose a refinement of the AVID Agnostic algorithm which replaces Dk−<sup>1</sup> = DIS(Vk−1) in Steps [2](#page-6-2) and [9](#page-6-3) with a well-chosen *subregion* Rk−<sup>1</sup> ⊆ Dk−1. We argue that this change does not affect the validity of Theorem [5,](#page-33-0) and admits refined P-dependent query complexity bounds compared to those presented in Appendix [F.2.](#page-53-0) In particular, this shows that the AVID principle can recover the optimal query complexity of homogeneous linear classifiers under the uniform distribution, and generally any isotropic log-concave distribution (indeed, with improved log factors compared to prior works).

The basic argument (building from the original ideas of [Balcan, Broder, and Zhang,](#page-10-12) [2007,](#page-10-12) and [Zhang](#page-16-1) [and Chaudhuri,](#page-16-1) [2014\)](#page-16-1) is that, rather than querying all examples in S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> in Step [2](#page-6-2) of <sup>A</sup>avid, the algorithm identifies a *subset* of these examples Q<sup>k</sup> ⊆ S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩ Dk−<sup>1</sup> \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> which suffices for the purpose of updating V<sup>k</sup> in Step [3.](#page-6-1) Specifically, we aim to identify a subset Q<sup>k</sup> ⊆ S 1 <sup>k</sup> ∩Dk−<sup>1</sup> \∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> for which, for any h, h′ ∈ Vk−1,

$$\left| \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\{h \neq h'\} \cap Q_k) - \hat{P}_{S_k^1}(\{h \neq h'\} \cap D_{k-1} \setminus \Delta_{i_k}) \right| \leq \frac{\varepsilon_k}{3C''}.$$

In other words, most of the significant disagreements in X \ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> among concepts in Vk−<sup>1</sup> are captured in the Q<sup>k</sup> set. In particular, this retains the guarantees of Lemmas [15](#page-42-0) and [16](#page-43-0) with only minor adjustments to the constants in the bounds (accounting for the potential <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> <sup>8</sup>C′ probability disagreements that are lost).

Formally, consider the algorithm A sub avid stated in Figure [2,](#page-61-1) where the Q<sup>k</sup> data subset is defined below. The values C, C′ , C′′, N, m<sup>k</sup> and data subsets S 1 k , S 4 k are all as defined in Aavid. The data subsets S 2 k , S 3 k,i are defined analogously to <sup>A</sup>avid except allocated in the corresponding steps of <sup>A</sup> sub avid: that is, if and when the algorithm reaches Step [2](#page-61-2) with a value k, or reaches Step [9](#page-61-3) (in which case let k = N + 1), then for the value i<sup>k</sup> and the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> as defined at that time in the algorithm, letting pˆ<sup>k</sup> := 2Pˆ S<sup>4</sup> k (∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> ), the algorithm allocates to S 2 k the next m′ k := l C ′′c <sup>1</sup>pˆ<sup>k</sup> ε k <sup>d</sup> + log 4(3+N−k) δ m consecutive examples not previously allocated to any data subset, and likewise, if and when the algorithm reaches Step [5](#page-61-4) with values (k, i), it allocates to S 3 k,i the next m<sup>k</sup> consecutive examples which have not yet been allocated to any data subset.

We define the Q<sup>k</sup> data subset via a technique analogous to the work of [Zhang and Chaud](#page-16-1)[huri](#page-16-1) [\(2014\)](#page-16-1), specified via a discrete *linear program* with a finite number of constraints imposed by the set of realizable classifications of S 1 k . Let t<sup>k</sup> = P<sup>k</sup>−<sup>1</sup> <sup>k</sup>′=1 mk′ , and recall S 1 k := {(X<sup>t</sup>k+1, Y<sup>t</sup>k+1), . . . ,(X<sup>t</sup>k+m<sup>k</sup> , Y<sup>t</sup>k+m<sup>k</sup> )}. The algorithm inductively constructs sets V<sup>k</sup> ⊆ <sup>C</sup> in Step [3](#page-61-5) (analogous to the V<sup>k</sup> sets in <sup>A</sup>avid). For any given k ∈ {1, . . . , N + 1}, denote by

$$V_{k-1}(S_k^1) := \{(h(X_{t_k+1}), \dots, h(X_{t_k+m_k})) : h \in V_{k-1}\} \subseteq \{0, 1\}^{m_k}$$

,

the set of Vk−1-realizable classifications of S 1 k . For the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> (which is defined inductively based on previous rounds of the algorithm, analogously to the set ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> in Aavid), consider the following integer linear program with binary variables[<sup>19</sup>](#page-60-1) ζ1,0, ζ1,1, q1, . . . , ζ<sup>m</sup>k,0, ζ<sup>m</sup>k,1, q<sup>m</sup><sup>k</sup> .

<sup>19</sup>For simplicity, in this work, we present a technique based on an *integer* linear program, to arrive at a deterministic querying strategy. It is straightforward to extend the result to allow for non-integer solutions

Algorithm A sub avid Input: Error parameter ε, Confidence parameter δ, Unlabeled data X1, . . . , X<sup>m</sup> Output: Classifier hˆ 0. Initialize i = i<sup>1</sup> = 0, ∆<sup>0</sup> = ∅, V<sup>0</sup> = <sup>C</sup> 1. For k = 1, . . . , N 2. Query all examples in Q<sup>k</sup> and S 2 <sup>k</sup> ∩ ∆<sup>i</sup><sup>k</sup> 3. V<sup>k</sup> ← n h ∈ Vk−<sup>1</sup> : ˆer1,2 k (h) ≤ ˆer1,2 k (hˆ <sup>k</sup>) + <sup>ε</sup><sup>k</sup> C′ o 4. If V<sup>k</sup> = ∅ or ˆer1,2 k (hˆ <sup>k</sup>) < minh∈V<sup>k</sup> ˆer1,2 k (h) − ε<sup>k</sup> 4C′ , Then Return hˆ := hˆ k 5. While maxf,g∈V<sup>k</sup> <sup>P</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> S<sup>3</sup> k,i ({f ̸= g} \ ∆i) > εk+2 6. Query all examples in QN +1 and S 2 <sup>N</sup> +1 ∩ <sup>∆</sup><sup>i</sup><sup>N</sup> +1 and Return <sup>h</sup><sup>ˆ</sup> := <sup>h</sup>ˆ<sup>N</sup> +1
